# I. Arc 1 — Jess
@pov Jess

### The Transfer Student

The cafeteria was noisy like always.
Jess sat across from Asa, stirring the melting ice in her lemonade even though it didn’t need stirring. Chairs scraped. Trays clinked. Somewhere behind her a table of boys laughed too loudly at something that probably wasn’t funny.
Zen was there too, tipped back in his chair with a pen spinning between his fingers, and Dan beside him, saying nothing as usual. But it was Asa everyone listened to.
Asa was talking again. She was always talking, always smiling, always halfway through one story when someone walked up to start another. Twice already this lunch, people had stopped at their table just to say hi to her. Neither of them had looked at Jess.
Jess liked that about her.
It made things easier. When Asa was there, nobody waited for Jess to say something. She could sit at the warm edge of all that noise and feel like she belonged to it.
Because Jess had never been good at talking.
She still didn’t know why Asa had picked her. One day there had been an empty seat beside Jess, and then there had been Asa in it, asking to borrow a pen, and she had never really left. Jess had stopped wondering about it a long time ago, in case the answer was no reason.
That was not quite true. Sometimes, when Jess looked up without warning, Asa was already looking at her, and looked away a second too fast, and made a joke. Jess had noticed. She had decided not to notice. If she never asked, nothing would ever have to change.
“Jess? Are you even listening?” Asa laughed.
Jess blinked. The straw stopped moving.
“S-sorry… what did you say?”
Asa sighed dramatically and dropped her chin into her hand.
“I said there’s a new transfer student today. Nobody’s seen her properly yet.”
Jess nodded quietly.
Transfers happened sometimes. For a few days everyone stared, and then they disappeared into the background like everybody else. Jess knew the background well. There was plenty of room in it.
But then the cafeteria doors opened.
And someone walked in.
The girl moved slowly, like the noise didn’t belong to her world. She didn’t hurry. She didn’t search the room for a friendly face the way new students always did. She had a calm face, almost unreadable, and her eyes passed over the cafeteria table by table, like she was observing something rather than participating in it.
She walked straight to the counter.
“Iced coffee with milk,” she said.
Her voice was steady. It wasn’t loud, but Jess heard it from three tables away.
“Little ice. Seventy-five percent sweetness.”
Jess noticed the way she ordered. So specific. So confident. Jess had been ordering the same lemonade for two years, because the first time she had panicked and pointed at it.
Asa noticed too.
“Ooh,” she murmured, leaning sideways to see better. “She’s interesting.”
The girl counted out her coins, took her drink, and turned around.
For a moment their eyes met.
It lasted only a second. But it didn’t feel like being glanced at. It felt like being read.
Jess quickly looked away.
A few minutes later, Asa’s chair scraped back.
“Wait here.”
“Asa—”
But she was already gone, slipping between the tables toward the corner where the new girl had sat down alone.
Asa talked to everyone easily. Even strangers. Jess had watched her do it a hundred times and still didn’t understand how it worked, how you could simply walk up to a person and begin.
Jess lowered her head, pretending to check her phone while sneaking glances. There was nothing on the screen. She scrolled anyway.
The mysterious girl looked surprised at first. She sat very straight, both hands around her cup, while Asa leaned on the back of the empty chair and talked.
But then she smiled.
A small smile. It was there and gone, like she wasn’t used to it.
They talked. Asa said something with her hands. The girl answered in what looked like very few words.
Then Asa turned and waved excitedly across the whole cafeteria.
“Jess! Come here!”
Jess froze.
Heads turned. Her ears went hot.
She hated meeting new people. She hated the first ten seconds most, when they looked at you and waited, and whatever you said in those ten seconds was who you were to them forever.
But she stood anyway. She always did, when Asa called. She picked up her lemonade so her hands would have something to hold and walked over.
“This is Delta,” Asa said brightly.
Delta. Jess turned the name over once. It sounded like something from a math textbook. It suited her, somehow.
The girl nodded slightly.
“Hi.”
Jess gave a small bow of her head.
“H-hi.”
Delta’s eyes were calm. Almost too calm. They moved from Jess’s face to the lemonade in her hand, to the straw she had started turning again without noticing, and back up.
It felt like she was observing Jess carefully. Like she was noting things down somewhere Jess couldn’t see.
Jess couldn’t explain why, but it made her a little nervous.

### The Seat Beside Asa

Days passed.
Then weeks.
Then months.
And somehow Delta became part of their group.
Nobody decided it. One lunch she was at their table with her iced coffee, and the next day she was there again, and by the second week Asa was saving her a seat.
But something was different.
Asa laughed more when Delta was around. It wasn’t the bright laugh she handed out to everyone. It was a lower, surprised one, like Delta had said something she hadn’t seen coming. Delta never said much. That was what made it strange.
Sometimes, in the middle of laughing, she glanced over at Jess. Just for a second, as if to check that Jess was still there. Jess thought it was kind of her.
They sat together often.
One Thursday a lecturer kept Jess behind to ask about a late assignment, and by the time she reached the cafeteria the others were already there. Delta was in the seat beside Asa.
Jess had never thought of it as her seat. Not until someone else was sitting in it.
Their heads were close together. Asa was showing Delta something on her phone, and Delta was looking at it with that same quiet attention she gave everything, and neither of them noticed Jess standing there with her tray.
She counted. She didn’t mean to.
Twelve seconds.
“Jess! There you are.” Asa patted the table. “Sit, sit.”
Jess sat down across from them. The lemonade tasted the same as always.
Asa seemed so comfortable with Delta.
Jess had never once had to wonder where Asa was looking. She had always known it would be at her.
Too comfortable.
Jess tried to ignore the feeling in her chest.
It wasn’t jealousy.
It couldn’t be. You couldn’t be jealous over a chair.
After all, Delta was nice.
She helped Jess with homework sometimes. The first time was in the library, when Jess had been stuck on the same math problem for so long that she had started erasing answers that were right. Delta pulled her chair over without being asked and looked at the page once.
“You’re not wrong,” she said. “You just stopped trusting it halfway. Keep going from here.”
She didn’t take the pencil. She waited, hands folded, while Jess worked through the rest.
“See?”
She listened patiently when Jess spoke, too. Most people finished Jess’s sentences for her when the words got stuck. Delta never did. She only waited, calm and unhurried, until the words came.
That should have made things easier.
Somehow it made them harder.
Once Jess tried to thank her properly. “Why are you so—”
Delta looked up from her book. “Hm?”
“N-nothing. Thank you. For the math.”
Delta looked at her a moment longer than she needed to.
“You’re welcome, Jess.”
Then she went back to her page.
That was how it always went. Delta knew which problems Jess got wrong. She knew Jess stirred her drink when she was nervous. She probably knew a dozen other things Jess had never said out loud.
And Jess knew how Delta took her coffee.
That was all.
There was still a strange invisible wall between them.
A distance Jess couldn’t cross.
She wasn’t even sure she was meant to.

### Zen

Zen noticed Delta too.
Jess realized it the day they were studying together.
It was a review session before exams, the kind nobody wanted to be in. The windows were open and the room was warm and half the students were asleep with their eyes open. Zen had his chair tipped back on two legs and his phone under the desk. Jess sat beside him. She had been trying for ten minutes to think of something to say to him.
Then the lecturer asked for someone to solve the problem on the board, and Delta stood up.
She didn’t look at her notes. She took the marker and began to write, and as she wrote she explained, each step following the one before it like there had never been any other way to do it.
She spoke clearly.
Confidently.
Even the lecturer nodded.
Beside Jess, the phone screen had gone dark in Zen’s hand. His chair came down onto four legs. He leaned back again, slower this time, watching Delta at the whiteboard.
It wasn’t the way boys looked at pretty girls. Jess could have understood that. It was the way you look at something you thought you had lost.
Zen smirked.
“Smart,” he murmured.
He didn’t say it to Jess. He didn’t say it to anyone.
Jess felt something twist inside her.
Zen was her boyfriend.
He had asked her in the hallway one afternoon, as easily as asking for the time, and she had said yes before she could believe he meant it. He walked her back to her dorm in the evenings. He answered her messages, usually. In crowds he let her hold on to his sleeve.
But sometimes it felt like he was just… playing the role.
That afternoon they walked back toward the dorms together like always.
“She’s good at explaining,” Jess said. She wanted to be the one who said it first.
“Who?”
“L-Delta.”
“Oh.” Zen shrugged. “Yeah.”
They reached the steps of her building. He was already looking down the path.
“Zen?”
“Mm?”
“I love you.”
It came out small. It always did.
He smiled, the easy smile everyone liked, and ruffled her hair.
“I know. Text me before you sleep.”
Then he walked away, taking his phone out of his pocket as he went.
Jess stood on the steps until he turned the corner.
Still, she loved him.
Even if he didn’t say it back often.
Some people just weren’t good with words. She, of all people, should understand that.

### Dan

Dan was different.
Quiet.
Gentle.
He didn’t talk much but he always listened.
He was the one who noticed when your cup was empty. He pushed the napkins across the table before you asked for them. Once, when Jess stuttered through an answer in class and someone at the back snickered, Dan’s textbook hit the floor with a bang, and the whole room turned to look at him instead.
He picked it up without a word. He never mentioned it afterward.
Jess never thanked him. She wasn’t sure he wanted her to.
Sometimes she noticed him watching everyone silently. He watched the way a person watches rain from a window, without being part of it.
Especially Delta.
It happened at lunch one day. Asa was telling a story, and Zen was laughing, and Jess happened to glance to the side.
Dan wasn’t laughing. He was looking at Delta.
And Delta, as if she had felt it, looked up.
For a second the two of them just looked at each other across the table. Neither of them smiled. Neither of them looked surprised.
Then Dan lowered his eyes to his food, and Delta turned back to Asa, and the story went on.
Jess stirred her lemonade.
It hadn’t been the way Zen looked at Delta. It was something else. She didn’t have a word for it.
She couldn’t tell what he was thinking.
Dan was impossible to read.
Jess was beginning to think he wasn’t the only one.

### The Mountain

Then came the mountain trip.
It was supposed to be the seaside.
Asa said it first, at lunch, with both hands flat on the table.
“The sea! Come on. Two days.”
Jess had already started to smile when Zen said, “No.”
He said it too fast. Everyone looked at him.
“We shouldn’t go.”
Jess had never heard his voice like that. Zen wasn’t afraid of anything.
Before anyone could ask why, Dan spoke.
“If not the sea, we could go camping in the mountains.”
Dan almost never suggested anything. Zen stared at him for a long moment, as if Dan had said it in another language. Then he shrugged, and Asa clapped her hands, and it was decided.
Delta said nothing at all. She only looked from Zen to Dan and back, and drank her coffee.
It was supposed to be fun.
Fresh air.
Campfire.
Friends together.
Jess was excited. She packed two days early, then unpacked and packed again.
She thought maybe this trip would make everything feel normal again.
Maybe she could talk to Zen more.
Maybe she could talk to Asa like before.
Maybe the strange distance around Delta would disappear.
On the bus, Asa sat with Delta. Jess sat beside Zen, who put his earphones in and fell asleep before they had left the town. She watched the buildings thin out into fields, and the fields into trees. Across the aisle, Dan peeled an orange and held half of it out to her without saying anything.
The trail was steeper than anyone had expected. Halfway up, the loose stones slid under Jess’s shoe, and a hand caught her elbow before she fell.
Delta.
“Careful.”
She let go as soon as Jess was steady.
“Th-thanks,” Jess said. But Delta was already walking.
It was the closest they had ever been.
They reached the clearing in the late afternoon. Zen and Dan fought with the tent poles. Asa took pictures of them losing. For an hour it was exactly what Jess had hoped for.
Asa hooked an arm through hers and turned her toward the edge of the clearing.
“See? Who needs the sea.”
Jess looked. The whole valley lay below them, turning gold.
But the mountains were quiet.
Too quiet.
There were no other campers. No voices from the trail. No birds, Jess realized. She hadn’t heard a bird in a long time.
Delta was standing at the far side of the clearing. She wasn’t facing the view. She was facing the trees, and she was very still.
“Delta!” Asa called. “Come help with the fire!”
“Coming.”
But she looked at the trees a moment longer before she turned around.
The sun was setting when it happened.
Footsteps. Not from the trail.
Shouting.
Three men appeared from the trees.
“Hands up!”
Jess froze.
A gun.
One of them had a gun.
He was younger than the other two, and thin, and he held it out in front of him with both hands like he was afraid of it.
Her heart started pounding so loudly she could barely hear anything else.
Asa grabbed Jess’s hand. Her fingers were cold.
Zen stepped slightly forward.
Dan stood still.
Delta didn’t move.
The robber pointed the gun.
“Phones. Money. Now.”
Everyone obeyed quickly.
One of the men went down the line with an open sack. Jess’s hands trembled as she handed over her bag. Her phone was inside it. So was the orange peel she had kept in her pocket from the bus, for no reason at all.
It should have ended there.
The other two were already backing toward the trees.
But the robber with the gun looked nervous.
His hand shook. His eyes kept jumping from one face to the next.
“Don’t move! Nobody move!”
Jess wasn’t going to move. She was staring at the ground, at her own shoes, at the pine needles.
Then she looked up, only for a second, to find Zen.
He had stepped forward again. He wasn’t standing in front of her. He was standing in front of Delta, with one arm held a little out from his side, the way you put an arm across someone when a car brakes too hard.
The gun suddenly pointed toward them.
Jess felt her chest tighten.
Everything slowed.
It was only one step.
Someone moved.
Someone shouted. A name, maybe. She couldn’t tell whose.
Then—
BANG.
Pain exploded in Jess’s body.
She fell.
The ground felt cold. Pine needles pressed into her cheek.
Her vision blurred.
Next to her, Delta collapsed too. She lay close enough that Jess could have touched her hand.
Branches cracked. The men were running.
Jess could hear people shouting.
Zen’s voice.
“Delta!”
Not her name.
Footsteps running.
Someone lifting Delta.
Jess tried to speak.
Her mouth wouldn’t work.
Zen.
She wanted to call him.
But he didn’t come.
Her eyes searched desperately. Asa’s hand was gone from hers. She didn’t know when she had let go.
She saw Zen carrying Delta away.
Running.
Getting help.
That was all it was. He was getting help. Delta was hurt, and someone had to carry her.
Jess tried to reach out.
Her hand barely moved. Her fingers closed on pine needles.
The voices were getting farther away.
The sky above the mountain was darkening. One star had come out over the valley. It was a beautiful view. She wished someone were looking at it with her.
Jess felt strangely calm.
Maybe Zen would come back.
He would.
He had to.
After all…
He was her boyfriend.
Right?
Her vision faded slowly.
The last thing Jess heard was the wind through the trees.
Then everything went quiet.
Then everything went quiet.

## II. Arc 2 — Asa
@pov Asa

### The Smile

People always thought Asa had the perfect life.
She was friendly.
Confident.
Liked by everyone.
Lecturers trusted her. They gave her the sign-in sheet to pass around and the new students to look after. Classmates wanted to sit with her. People laughed when she joked, sometimes before she had finished the joke.
It was easy.
All she had to do was smile.
But smiles were easy when they didn’t mean anything.
At home, nobody really talked.
Her father worked late every night. His dinner waited on the table under a plate, going cold, and the only proof he lived there was the sound of his shoes in the hallway after midnight.
Her mother filled the silence with criticism.
All through school she had been waiting at the table when Asa came in, with a test paper or a report slip laid flat in front of her like evidence. Now that Asa lived in the dorms, she did it by phone, every Sunday.
“Why is your grade not higher?”
“I’ll do better.”
“Why are you always out with friends?”
“Sorry.”
“Why can’t you be more serious?”
Asa answered in the same pleasant voice she used on campus. Then the call ended, and there was only the hum of the dorm corridor.
Warmth didn’t exist in that house.
She had tried once. Years ago she had come home with a first-place certificate and held it out with both hands. Her mother had looked at it for a moment.
“And in math?”
At some point, Asa stopped trying.
Instead, she learned how to become someone everyone liked. She learned which laugh worked on which person. She learned to ask questions, because people who were busy talking about themselves never asked any back.
If people liked you, they wouldn’t look too closely.
Jess was the closest person she had.
Jess was gentle. Loyal. Always there.
Asa had chosen the seat beside her on the very first day, though she could not have said why. There had been thirty empty chairs in the room, and one quiet girl by the window stirring a drink that didn’t need stirring. Asa had asked to borrow a pen. She’d had three in her bag.
She knew a great many things about Jess now. That she ordered lemonade because she had once panicked and pointed at it. That she bowed her head a little when she said hello. That when Zen walked into a room her whole face changed, like a window when someone switches on a light behind it.
Asa always looked somewhere else when that happened. She kept a joke ready for those moments. She always had a joke ready.
She liked Zen. That was the annoying part. He was funny, and he was generous, and he had never once been unkind to her. She counted him as a real friend, and Dan too.
On certain days she could hardly stand to look at him.
So even Jess never saw the real Asa.
Because Asa never showed her.
Some doors stayed closed.

### The Girl Who Looked

The day Delta arrived, the cafeteria was loud like usual.
Asa sat across from Jess, talking about nothing in particular. A transfer student. A rumor. It didn’t matter what. Talking was like breathing. It kept things moving.
Jess was stirring her drink again.
She always did that when she was nervous. She was nervous most of the time.
Asa smiled.
Jess was sweet.
But simple.
Sometimes too simple. You could sit across from Jess for two years and she would never once wonder what you were thinking. It was restful. It was also, on some days, very hard.
Then the cafeteria doors opened.
A girl walked in.
She didn’t look around nervously like most new students.
She observed.
Like she was studying the room.
Asa leaned slightly forward.
Interesting.
The girl went to the counter.
“Iced coffee with milk. Little ice. Seventy-five percent sweetness.”
Her voice was calm.
Precise.
Most people stumbled when ordering in a crowded cafeteria. They changed their minds. They apologized to the person behind them.
She didn’t.
She took her drink to an empty table in the corner and sat down alone, as if alone was what she had come for.
Asa stood up.
“Wait here,” she told Jess.
Jess nodded quietly.
Approaching strangers was easy for Asa.
She had done it hundreds of times. A smile, a question, a hand on the back of a chair. New students were the easiest of all. They were grateful.
“New student?” Asa asked.
The girl turned.
Their eyes met.
For a moment, Asa felt something strange.
Like the girl could see through her smile. Like she had looked at it, understood how it was built, and set it aside.
“Yes,” the girl said.
“I’m Delta.”
They talked.
At first it was normal conversation.
Where are you from?
Which class are you in?
Asa asked the questions. Delta answered them in as few words as possible. Asa filled the gaps with her hands and her laugh, the way she always did.
But then Delta said something strange.
“You seem tired.”
Asa blinked.
“What?”
“You smile a lot,” Delta continued calmly. “But your eyes look tired.”
Asa laughed automatically.
Nobody had ever said that before. People said she was bright. Cheerful. Lucky.
“Wow,” she joked. “You figured me out already?”
But Delta didn’t laugh.
She turned her cup a little on the table. Then she said quietly,
“People who grow up without warmth learn to become warm for others.”
Asa’s smile froze.
The cafeteria was still loud. A tray dropped somewhere. Nobody else had heard.
For the first time in years, she didn’t know what to say.
Delta didn’t press. She only looked at her and waited, and after a moment the corner of her mouth moved in a small smile, as if to say that it was all right, that nothing had to be said.
So Asa did the only thing she knew how to do.
She turned around and waved across the room, as brightly as ever.
“Jess! Come here!”
Her voice came out perfect.
Her hand, when she lowered it, was not quite steady.

### Someone Who Knew

After that day, everything changed.
Delta became part of their group quickly.
Jess liked her.
Zen noticed her intelligence.
Dan watched quietly as usual.
But Delta and Asa became something else.
It started on the back stairs after class, where nobody went. Asa said one small thing, as a test, and she said it as a joke.
“My mom’s kind of intense about grades.”
Anyone else would have laughed and said mine too.
Delta said nothing. She waited.
And the rest came out.
After that they talked for hours. On the stairs. At the bus stop, letting three buses go by. On the phone at night, with Asa whispering under her blanket long after the dorm lights were out.
About things Asa had never told anyone.
Her family.
Her loneliness.
Her anger. She hadn’t known how much of it there was until she heard herself say it out loud.
Delta always listened carefully.
Then she gave advice.
Simple advice.
“Try speaking to your father when he isn’t busy.”
So on her next weekend home, Asa made a second cup of coffee on Sunday morning and put it beside his newspaper. He looked at the cup, then at her, as if a stranger had sat down at his table. She asked about his work. He talked for ten minutes, stiffly, about a project she didn’t understand. At the end he cleared his throat.
“School is all right?”
It was the first question he had asked her in a year.
“Your mother criticizes because that’s the only way she knows how to communicate.”
“Don’t fight their behavior. Change the direction of the conversation.”
So the next time a marked paper was lying on the table at home, Asa didn’t say sorry.
“Which part do you think I should fix first?”
Her mother opened her mouth and closed it again. Then she pulled out the chair beside her and pointed at the second page.
It wasn’t warm. Her mother’s voice was as sharp as ever. But they sat at the same table for half an hour, and when Asa stood up her mother said, without looking at her, “There is soup.”
Slowly, things at home changed.
Not dramatically.
But enough.
For the first time, Asa felt like someone understood her.
Jess noticed the change too.
Asa could tell.
Sometimes Jess looked at them strangely.
Like she wanted to join their conversations but didn’t know how.
There was one lunch when Asa looked up from her phone and found Jess standing beside the table with her tray, not sitting down. She had been there for a little while. Asa had felt it, the way you feel the sun on the side of your face. Delta was in the seat beside her. Jess’s seat.
“Jess! There you are. Sit, sit.”
Jess sat down across from them and stirred her lemonade and said nothing.
Asa felt a little guilty.
And under the guilt, something else. Something small and warm that she did not look at directly.
Jess had been watching her. Not Zen. Her.
But she couldn’t stop.
Being around Delta felt…
Comfortable.
Like standing next to someone who already knew every part of you.
It was only later, lying awake, that Asa noticed the other half of it. In all those hours, Delta had never said a single thing about her own home. Asa didn’t know if she had a father, or a mother, or a door of her own that stayed closed.
She would ask, she decided.
Tomorrow.

### The Mountain

The mountain trip was supposed to be relaxing.
Fresh air.
Friends.
Asa needed that. Things at home were better, but better was tiring too. Every conversation there still had to be steered. She wanted two days where nothing needed steering.
She had wanted the sea. But Zen had said no, strangely, sharply, and Dan had said mountains, and in the end it didn’t matter. Anywhere would do.
On the bus she sat with Delta. A few rows ahead she could see the top of Jess’s head beside Zen, who was asleep, and Jess’s face turned to the window.
This weekend, Asa thought. I’ll sit with her at the fire. I’ll fix it.
Dan was the only one who didn’t seem glad to be there. He barely touched his food. On the trail he kept dropping to the back of the line and looking down the path behind them.
“You okay?” she asked him.
“Fine.”
He didn’t look at her when he said it.
At the clearing she hooked her arm through Jess’s and showed her the valley, and Jess smiled, a real one, and for a moment it was like it used to be.
But the mountains felt uneasy that evening.
The air was too quiet.
Then the robbers appeared.
Everything happened too fast.
Shouting.
Fear.
The gun.
She grabbed Jess’s hand without thinking. It was the only thing she did.
Asa felt her body freeze.
She couldn’t move.
She had talked her way through everything in her life, and now there was a man with a shaking hand shouting don’t move, and she could not find a single word.
Jess’s fingers slid out of hers.
Then the gunshot exploded through the forest.
Asa’s hand closed on nothing.
Jess fell.
Delta fell too.
Asa screamed.
Zen ran toward Delta immediately. He didn’t look left or right. He went past Jess as if the ground there were empty.
Asa saw that. Even then, some part of her saw it.
She tried to step toward Jess. Her knees were gone.
Dan grabbed Asa before she collapsed. His arms came around her from behind, hard, holding her up, holding her back.
The world spun.
The last thing she saw was Jess on the ground with her eyes open, looking up at the sky.
Darkness swallowed everything.

### The Hospital

When Asa woke up, the hospital room smelled like antiseptic.
The light was grey. She couldn’t tell if it was morning or evening. There were pine needles still caught in the sleeve of her jacket, folded over the chair.
Dan sat beside her bed.
His face looked pale.
“You fainted,” he said softly.
Asa sat up slowly.
Her head hurt.
“Where is everyone?”
“Delta’s out of surgery. They say she’ll be all right. Zen is with her.”
He stopped there.
“And Jess?”
Dan hesitated.
“Jess is dead.”
The words hit her like ice.
Asa stared at him.
“No.”
Dan looked down.
“They couldn’t save her.”
Silence filled the room.
Asa looked at her own hand on the blanket. She could still feel the shape of Jess’s fingers in it. She had been holding on. She had been holding on, and then she hadn’t.
This weekend. I’ll fix it.
She waited for the crying to start. It didn’t. There was only a cold, clear space where it should have been.
Then a question escaped Asa’s lips before she could stop it.
“Why did you help me?”
Dan looked confused.
“You fainted.”
“Jess needed help more than me.”
Dan didn’t answer immediately.
His fingers tightened slightly on his knees.
“I… didn’t think.”
But something in his voice felt wrong.
Dan always thought. Dan was the one who noticed the empty cup, the missing napkin, the person about to be laughed at. In all the time she had known him, Asa had never once seen him do anything without thinking.
She had spent her whole life saying things she didn’t mean in a voice that sounded like she did.
She knew what it sounded like.

### The Bruise

The days after the accident felt strange.
Everyone called it that. The accident. As if nobody had been holding the gun.
On campus, people came up to Asa in the hallway to say they were sorry. She thanked them. She even smiled. It was the same smile as before, and nobody noticed anything wrong with it, and that frightened her more than anything.
At lunch she bought a lemonade without thinking and carried it to the table. Then she sat and looked at it. Nobody stirred it. The ice melted on its own.
The group had broken apart.
Zen stopped smiling.
Delta recovered quietly. She came back to campus after a few weeks, thinner, moving carefully, and took her seat as if she had never left it. Asa wanted to talk to her. For the first time, she didn’t know how to begin. She had never asked her question, either. It didn’t seem like the time.
But Dan…
Dan changed.
Asa noticed it first.
He was restless.
Always watching.
Like someone waiting for something terrible to happen.
Even during the trip, before the robbery, he had seemed uneasy. She remembered him at the back of the line, looking down the trail.
Now it was worse.
His eyes followed Zen constantly. Across the classroom. Down the corridor. Out through the gate.
At first, Asa thought he was worried about him.
But then she started hearing things.
Small comments.
Sharp words.
Dan began talking to Zen privately.
Again and again.
The first time she heard it properly, she was on the back stairs, the ones where she used to sit with Delta. The voices came up from the landing below.
“You left her.”
“I was getting help.” Zen’s voice was hoarse. “Delta was bleeding, I had to—”
“You chose.”
“I didn’t choose anything. I didn’t see—”
“She was your girlfriend. She was right there.” A pause. Dan never raised his voice. “You could have saved her.”
There was no answer. Only footsteps going down, fast.
Zen tried to ignore him.
But Dan didn’t stop.
Every day.
Every moment.
In the corridor. On the stairs. In the seat behind him, in a voice too low for the lecturer to hear.
Slowly.
Carefully.
Dan pushed.
Like someone pressing on a bruise again and again.
Zen’s confidence disappeared.
His jokes stopped.
He stopped tipping his chair back. He stopped eating at lunch. He sat with his phone in his hand, scrolling up through old messages, small ones, the kind a person sends when she gets home safe.
His eyes looked hollow.
Asa tried to intervene once. She caught Dan alone outside the lecture hall after the last class.
“Dan… maybe you should stop.”
Dan looked at her calmly.
It was the same gentle face. The same quiet voice that had once made her think he was the kindest of them all.
“Truth shouldn’t be comfortable.”
He picked up his bag and walked away.
Asa stood there for a long time. She had talked her way past teachers, parents, strangers. She could not think of one thing to say to that.

### The Empty Desk

A month later, Zen didn’t come to class.
His seat was empty when the lecture began. It was still empty an hour later. The lecturer came in late, and her eyes were red, and she stood at the front of the room for a while without opening her notes.
By then the phones had already told everyone.
The news spread quickly.
Zen was dead.
Suicide.
Someone at the front began to cry. Someone else said no way over and over, quietly, like a hiccup.
Asa sat silently when she heard.
She turned her head to the left, toward the seat where Jess would have been, to see her face. It was a habit. There was nobody there.
Two empty desks now.
Her mind replayed everything.
Jess dying.
Dan whispering.
Zen breaking.
Something didn’t feel right.
She looked for Delta. Delta was sitting very straight, both hands flat on her desk, looking at nothing. Her face told Asa nothing at all.
Then she looked behind her.
Dan sat in the back of the classroom, staring at the window.
He wasn’t restless anymore. His hands were still. His shoulders had come down.
His face was calm again.
Too calm.
Like the storm inside him had passed.
For the first time, Asa felt a chill run down her spine.
All her life she had been the one who hid things behind a pleasant face. She had thought she was the only one at that table who knew how.
Dan had never been the same since the mountain.
And somehow…
She felt like he had been waiting for something.
Something that had finally happened.

## III. Arc 3 — Zen
@pov Zen

### The Easy Life

The cafeteria was loud.
It always had been.
Zen sat lazily in his chair, spinning a pen between his fingers while half-listening to Asa talk. Jess sat across from her, nodding at everything Asa said.
It was always like this.
Asa filled the air.
Jess filled the silence.
Zen leaned back.
Life had always been easy for him.
His parents were famous scientists. Their research on mysterious forces and unknown phenomena had made them respected across the country. There were photographs of them shaking hands with ministers. There was an institute with the family name on the gate, somewhere out in the hills, where they spent most of the year. Zen had been raised by housekeepers and by the sound of other people praising him.
When they did come home, they measured him. Height. Weight. A little blood. Every visit, since before he could remember. His mother wrote the numbers in a book. He had always assumed that all parents did that.
From childhood, he grew up surrounded by admiration.
Teachers praised him.
Students envied him.
And he deserved it.
He was smart.
He was confident.
He was always at the top of the class.
Even debates bored him sometimes because nobody could challenge him.
He could not remember ever being unhappy. He could not remember very much about being small at all, in fact. But nobody did.
He had one bad dream, and it was always the same. He was lying on his back under white lights, and he could not turn his head. It should have been a nightmare. But in the dream there was always someone sitting beside the bed, just out of sight. A girl, he thought, though he never saw her. She never spoke. He always woke up calm.
He had a girlfriend because Jess had looked at him across a lecture hall for a whole semester as if he had personally arranged the weather, and he had found that he minded when she looked sad. He liked her. He wanted her safe and happy, the way he would have wanted it for a little sister, if he had had one. He had never been in love, so he took that for love, and asked her. Nobody had ever told him there was a difference.
His friends were the realest thing he had. Dan, who never needed him to be impressive. Asa, who was the only person who made fun of him to his face. He would have done anything for either of them. It would never have occurred to him to say so.
Then the cafeteria door opened.
A girl walked in.
She didn’t look around nervously like new students usually did.
She simply observed.
Zen noticed her immediately.
Interesting.
The girl approached the counter.
“Iced coffee with milk. Little ice. Seventy-five percent sweetness.”
Her voice was calm.
Something went through him when he heard it. It wasn’t recognition. He had never seen her before. It was more like walking out of an air-conditioned building into the afternoon. A warmth on the skin, all at once, with no reason behind it.
He decided it was curiosity.
Zen smirked slightly.
Precise people were usually intelligent.
Asa stood up.
“Wait here,” she told Jess before walking over to the girl.
Zen watched them talk.
Then Jess joined them.
Soon Asa waved at him and Dan.
“Come meet our new friend!”
Zen walked over casually. Dan followed a step behind.
“This is Delta,” Asa said.
Delta nodded slightly.
Zen studied her.
Her eyes were calm.
Not shy.
Not impressed.
Just calm.
They rested on him a moment longer than they had on the others. It wasn’t the way girls usually looked at him. It was a careful, checking look, as if she already knew him and only wanted to make sure of something. Then they moved on.
For the first time in a long while, Zen felt curious.

### The Flaw

He realized how different she was during their first debate.
The topic was controversial.
Is a person guilty of harm they never meant to cause?
Most people avoided it.
Zen loved topics like that.
They showed who could actually think.
He took the side nobody wanted.
“No,” he said. “A person is only guilty if they meant to cause harm.”
He gave an example. If a storm floods a village, nobody blames the storm. The storm did not intend anything. It simply happened.
A person who hurts someone by accident is the same, he said. They did not choose the harm. They did not want it. You can call it a tragedy, but you cannot call it their fault. Guilt needs intention. Without intention, there is no guilt.
It was a good argument. He watched it land on the room. The lecturer was nodding.
But when he finished, Delta tilted her head slightly.
“That logic has a flaw.”
Zen laughed.
“Oh?”
“You are only looking at it from the side of the person who caused the harm,” she said. “From that side, almost nobody is ever guilty, because almost nobody means to hurt anyone. Now look at it from the side of the person who was hurt. They are hurt just the same, whether you meant it or not.”
She wasn’t finished.
“And a person is not a storm. A storm cannot think. It cannot choose. A person can. A person is able to pay attention to what their actions might do to someone else. If they don’t pay attention, and someone is hurt, then ‘I didn’t mean to’ is not an excuse. Very often it only means ‘I didn’t look.’”
She calmly dismantled his entire argument in less than two minutes.
Not emotionally.
Logically.
Zen stared at her.
Then he smiled.
Finally.
Someone interesting.

### Equal

After that, he kept talking to her.
Debates became frequent.
Sometimes they lasted hours. They sat on the library steps until the lights went off behind them and neither had won.
Delta didn’t care about people’s opinions.
She spoke honestly.
Confidently.
It fascinated him.
He began noticing her more.
The way she thought.
The way she didn’t try to impress anyone.
The way she could stand beside him as an equal.
And the warmth. That never went away. Sitting next to her was like sitting in a patch of sun. He didn’t examine it. He had never needed to examine things that felt good.
Zen knew that was why Asa liked her too. Though with Asa it was harder to tell. She laughed loudest with Delta when Jess was close enough to hear.
But somewhere along the way…
Zen started liking her for another reason.
He just didn’t admit it.
It confused him.
He had thought he knew what love was. Love was what he felt for Jess. Wanting her safe. Wanting her happy. Minding when she was sad.
What he felt near Delta was nothing like that. It was not calm. It did not feel like looking after someone. He had no name for it, and if this was love, then he did not know what to call the other thing.
Jess noticed.
Of course she did.
Jess always noticed.
But she never said anything. She only got quieter, and stirred her drink, and smiled when he looked at her.
That made Zen feel strangely guilty.
Yet he couldn’t stop looking at Delta.

### The Warning

One afternoon the group decided to plan a trip.
“To the seaside!” Asa suggested.
Everyone liked the idea. Jess said she couldn’t really swim, and Asa said she would hold her hand the whole time, and everyone laughed.
Everyone except Dan.
Zen noticed immediately.
Dan was his closest friend.
Quiet.
Gentle.
Reliable.
But recently Dan had started acting strangely.
He often looked distracted.
Sometimes he seemed lost in thought.
Like he was somewhere else entirely. At lunch his eyes went around the table from face to face, slowly, like someone counting.
A week before the trip, Dan approached him.
“We shouldn’t go.”
Zen raised an eyebrow.
“Why?”
Dan hesitated.
“It… isn’t safe.”
Zen laughed.
“Since when are you paranoid?”
Dan lowered his gaze.
“I changed it. We’re not going where we went before…”
His voice was so quiet Zen almost didn’t hear it.
“But what if the outcome is the same?”
Zen frowned.
“What?”
Dan didn’t explain. He only looked at Zen for a moment with an expression Zen had never seen on him before. It was the way you look at an old photograph of someone.
Then he walked away.
Zen had forgotten about it by dinner.

### The Sea

The day of the trip arrived.
The sea was beautiful.
Waves rolling slowly onto the sand.
They had rented a cottage on the beach, an old wooden one with a veranda roof held up on thin, salt-eaten posts.
Jess and Delta went into the water together.
Jess couldn’t swim well, so they stayed in the shallow area.
Asa relaxed near the cottage.
Dan and Zen watched the two girls playing in the water.
Delta was laughing. Zen had never heard her laugh properly before. She had turned her face up to the sun with her eyes closed, and the light came off the water all around her.
He was watching that.
At the edge of his attention he was aware of Jess turning toward the beach. Looking for him. He meant to wave.
He didn’t.
Then everything changed.
Jess stepped slightly deeper. One step backward, then another, still facing the shore.
A strong wave crashed toward her.
It dragged her suddenly into deeper water.
Jess screamed.
Delta shouted for help and rushed toward her.
But Delta wasn’t a strong swimmer either.
Zen ran immediately.
He reached Jess first.
He pulled her out of the water and dragged her onto the sand.
“Jess! Jess!”
He began CPR.
One breath.
Two breaths.
Water spilled from Jess’s mouth.
She coughed.
Alive.
Her eyes opened. They found his face and stayed there.
Zen felt relief flood through him.
Then he realized something.
“Where’s Delta?”
The ocean stretched endlessly.
There was no sign of her.
Behind him, chaos had unfolded. He didn’t see it. He put it together afterward, from the things Dan shouted at him.
Asa had tried to run toward them.
But she slipped on a wet rock at the bottom of the cottage steps.
Her head struck the stone.
She collapsed against one of the veranda posts.
Dan saw it happen.
He hesitated only a second, looking from her to the place where Delta had gone under, before turning back toward the ocean.
That was the second the post gave way, and the rotten roof came down on top of her.
Dan ran toward her immediately.
But it was too late.
Asa was already dead.

### Hollow

Three days later, the coast guard stopped searching.
Delta was declared missing.
Presumed dead.
Zen felt like his chest had been hollowed out.
Jess survived.
But Delta was gone.
Jess stayed close to him in those days. She brought him food he didn’t eat. She held on to his sleeve.
“It’s not your fault,” she said.
And once, very quietly, “I didn’t think the water would—”
She didn’t finish. He wasn’t listening. He was thinking that he had done the right thing, that he had saved his girlfriend, and that some part of him wished—
He never let that thought finish either.
Dan blamed him.
“Why didn’t you notice?” Dan demanded. “You were right there on the sand. You were looking straight at them.”
Zen shouted back.
“You were the one who told us not to go! You knew something was going to happen!”
“And you didn’t listen!” Dan said.
“Because you didn’t explain!” Zen said. “You never explained anything!”
Their arguments grew worse every day.
Dan would not let it go.
He found Zen in the corridor. In the cafeteria. Outside his room at night. After the first week he stopped shouting. He spoke quietly, and that was worse.
“You were sitting ten steps from the water.”
“You saw Jess go deeper. You were looking right at her.”
“I told you not to go. I begged you. You laughed at me.”
“Asa would be alive. Delta would be alive. All you had to do was listen.”
Every sentence was true. That was what made it unbearable. Zen could not find a single one to argue with.
Eventually they stopped speaking.
It didn’t help. By then Zen could say Dan’s lines to himself, and he did, all day and most of the night.
He went over that afternoon again and again.
He had been looking at Delta. That was the truth of it. Jess had turned toward the beach to find him. He had seen her do it, and he had not even lifted his hand. If he had waved, she would have stayed where she was. If he had been watching her, he would have seen her go deeper, and he would have been in the water before the wave came.
He had reached Jess first. He had been so relieved when she coughed. And in those same seconds, while he was feeling relieved, Delta was going under behind him, and Asa was running across the rocks toward them.
Asa had only run because of the screaming. If he had been faster, there would have been no screaming, and she would never have slipped.
And Dan had warned him. A week before. It isn’t safe. Zen had laughed, and asked him since when he was paranoid.
He couldn’t escape the guilt.
Two people were dead.
Because of him.
He stopped sleeping. He stopped going to debates. Food tasted of nothing. He had always been the one with an answer. Now there was a question he could not answer, and it was the only one that mattered.
He remembered his own argument. A person is only guilty if they meant to cause harm. He hadn’t meant anything. He had only been looking the wrong way.
It didn’t hold. She had been right about the flaw. He had not meant it, and they were dead just the same. He could have paid attention, and he had not.
Jess tried to reach him. That made it worse. Every time he saw her alive, he saw the two who were not.
It felt as though something heavy had been set on his chest, and every day someone added a little more. He could not see any way for it to be lifted.
One night, he made a decision.
If he disappeared…
Maybe the guilt would disappear too.

### Again

Then—
Zen opened his eyes.
His own room. Dark. The clock beside the bed said 12:00. March the twenty-first.
He lay very still. He remembered everything. The beach. The boats going up and down the bay for six days. The man from the coast guard with his cap in his hands. Dan’s voice in the corridor. It was not a dream. It was too clear for a dream. It had happened. It had happened, and then somehow it had not.
He did not sleep again that night. In the morning he went to the cafeteria because he did not know what else to do.
The same noise.
The same table.
Asa talking.
Jess stirring her drink.
The pen dropped out of his fingers and rolled across the table. Nobody noticed.
Zen stared around in shock.
This was impossible.
Then the cafeteria doors opened.
A girl walked in.
“Iced coffee with milk. Little ice. Seventy-five percent sweetness.”
Delta.
Alive.
Zen’s chest tightened painfully.
Relief.
Shock.
Nostalgia.
And the warmth, exactly as it had been the first time.
From that moment on, he couldn’t stop watching her.
In their next debate he made his old argument again, word for word, only to hear her say it.
“That logic has a flaw.”
He tried to be better to Jess, too. He walked her back to her dorm. He answered her messages. She was alive. He had saved her once, and he held on to that.
But his eyes kept going where they went.
Jess noticed.
He knew she did.
But he couldn’t help it.
Because he remembered losing her.
Then one day someone suggested a trip again.
“To the seaside!”
Zen froze.
“No.”
Everyone looked at him.
“We shouldn’t go.”
Before anyone could argue, Dan suddenly spoke.
“If not the sea, we could go camping in the mountains.”
Zen stared at him.
Something was wrong.
Dan from before would never suggest another dangerous trip. Dan from before had begged him not to go.
This Dan met his eyes calmly. Almost patiently.
But Zen thought maybe it was fine.
After all…
They weren’t going to the sea anymore.
History had changed.
Or so he believed.

### The Mountain

The robbery in the mountains proved him wrong.
Three men. A gun in a shaking hand. Don’t move.
Zen had already stepped in front of Delta. He hadn’t decided to. His body remembered a flat, empty sea.
There was a movement at his side. Someone coming closer.
When the gun fired, both Jess and Delta fell.
For a split second Zen remembered the sea.
The moment he lost Delta.
This time he grabbed her first.
He carried her away to get help. He didn’t look back. He told himself the others were with Jess.
Jess died.
Later, Zen confronted Dan.
“Why did you help Asa instead of Jess?”
Dan looked at him calmly.
“For the same reason you helped Delta.”
Zen felt his blood run cold.
“You remember too.”
Dan nodded slowly.
“Then why take us there?”
Dan looked away.
“You wouldn’t understand.”
No matter how much Zen demanded answers, Dan stayed silent.
Instead, he began whispering things again.
“You knew the future.”
“You still couldn’t change it.”
“People died because of you again.”
The guilt crushed Zen slowly.
When Delta came back to class she sometimes sat on a bench in the courtyard, in the sun. He sat near her once. It was as warm as it had ever been. He got up and left. He didn’t think he was allowed to have it.
Even knowing the future…
He couldn’t save everyone.
Maybe fate couldn’t be changed.
Maybe everything would always end in tragedy.
Eventually the pressure became unbearable.
Zen made the same decision again.
The world faded into darkness.

## IV. Arc 4 — Dan (Part 1)
@pov Dan

### Midnight

Dan opened his eyes.
White ceiling.
For a few seconds, he simply stared at it without moving.
Then slowly he turned his head toward the clock.
12:00 AM.
Exactly midnight.
Dan smirked faintly.
“Same as always… twelve o’clock. March twenty-first.”
Four months after Delta transferred to their university.
Two weeks before the planned trip.
He already knew the date without looking. He knew the crack in the ceiling paint and the sound of the fan and the exact place where his phone lay face down on the desk.
Because this wasn’t the first time.
Dan had lost count of how many times he had woken up in this room.
Always the same night.
Always the same moment.
The beginning of the loop.

### Five

Dan had always been a quiet person.
Most people thought he lived inside his own world.
But he still had friends.
Zen.
Jess.
Asa.
To someone introverted like him, those few people meant everything.
They were his entire world. He didn’t need to talk much to be part of it. He only needed to sit at the end of the table and know that all of them were there.
Then one day Delta appeared.
At first he didn’t think much of her.
She seemed calm.
Interesting.
But slowly he noticed something.
Asa became closer to Delta.
Zen started paying attention to her.
Jess looked more unhappy every day.
Dan didn’t like it.
Not because he hated Delta.
But because he felt something changing inside their group.
Something unstable. Like a table with one leg cut shorter than the rest.
Still, he accepted it.
That was how relationships worked.
People changed.
Groups changed.
Until the first tragedy.

### Siem Reap

The first trip they ever planned was to Angkor Wat in Siem Reap, Cambodia.
Everyone was excited.
Dan remembered their laughter clearly. Asa making them pose on the temple steps at sunrise. Jess holding on to Zen’s sleeve. Delta standing a little apart in the first light, with her face turned up and her eyes closed.
The night everything ended.
They went clubbing.
Music.
Alcohol.
Dancing.
Jess wasn’t dancing. He remembered that afterward. She sat at the table and drank.
She did not usually drink at all. That night she finished one glass after another, quickly, watching Zen, who was watching Delta. By the time Dan noticed, her face was flushed and her eyes were too bright. He meant to say something to her when he came back.
Eventually Zen and Dan went to the bathroom together, leaving the three girls at the table.
It was only a few minutes.
Just a few minutes.
But when they returned—
The girls were gone.
One chair had been pushed back so hard it had tipped against the wall. A glass lay on its side in a spreading pool. Asa’s bag was gone, and Delta’s. Jess’s was still hanging from her chair.
A waiter shrugged when they asked. The small one had run out, he said. Upset, maybe. The other two had gone after her.
At first they thought they had simply gone somewhere else. Someone had felt sick. Someone needed air.
But they never came back.
The search lasted hours.
Then days.
When the police finally found them…
Dan wished they hadn’t.
Jess.
Asa.
Delta.
All three had been kidnapped, raped, and murdered.
What he was shown that day never left him.
Zen was the same.
They went back to the campus, because there was nowhere else to go. The table by the window was still there. Nobody sat at it.
They both drowned in guilt. A few minutes. They had been gone for a few minutes.
Dan went over those minutes until he knew them by heart. He had seen Jess drinking. He had seen her face. He had meant to say something to her when he came back. He had been standing at a sink, washing his hands, while it happened.
If he had stayed at the table. If he had let Zen go alone. If he had been thirty seconds quicker. There were a hundred ways it did not happen, and every one of them was some small thing he had not done.
There were three funerals. He stood at the back at each one. Asa’s mother did not cry. Jess’s parents thanked him for being such a good friend to her, and he could not answer them.
Zen stopped talking. He sat in his room with the lights off. When Dan knocked, he said through the door that he was fine.
For Dan it was worse than grief. Those four people had been his whole world. He had never needed anything more than to sit at the end of the table and know that all of them were there. Now three of the five chairs were empty, and he was the one who had walked away and left them alone.
He stopped eating. He stopped going to class. At night he lay awake and counted, out of habit. One. Two. There was nothing after two.
Somewhere in those weeks, Dan decided he couldn’t live anymore.
It did not feel like a decision. It felt like the only thing left that made any sense. He could not bring them back. He could not carry what he had been shown. He told himself that it was a debt, and that this was the only way to pay it.
He planned to end it quietly. He told no one. He tidied his room. He returned a book he had borrowed from Zen months before.
It was the book that gave him away.
Zen stood in his doorway that evening, holding it. He looked as if he had not slept in a week.
“Why did you give this back?”
“It’s yours.”
“You’ve had it since last year. You never give anything back.” Zen’s voice was unsteady. “And you cleaned your room. Dan. You never clean your room.”
Dan said nothing.
But Zen found out. He looked past Dan into the room, and saw the knife lying on the desk, and he understood all of it at once.
“No,” Zen said. “No. Not you too.”
Zen tried to stop him.
He pushed his way into the room. He was crying. Dan had never seen him cry before.
“They’re gone. All three of them. You’re the only one I have left. You don’t get to leave me here by myself.”
“I left them,” Dan said. “I walked away from that table.”
“So did I! We both did! Do you think I don’t know that?” Zen reached for the desk. “Give it to me.”
Dan got to it first. He did not want to hurt Zen. He only wanted him to leave. Then they were both holding on, both pulling, both shouting, and neither of them was thinking at all. Zen’s foot caught the leg of the chair.
During the struggle, the knife slipped.
It went into Zen’s chest.
It took less than a second. There was no anger in it. It was an accident, as small and stupid as a stumble.
Dan still remembered the exact sound Zen made.
A soft gasp.
Zen looked down, and then up at Dan. He looked surprised. He tried to say something. It might have been Dan’s name.
Then silence.
Dan stared at his friend on the floor.
He knelt down beside him. He said Zen’s name. He said it again. He shook him by the shoulder, the way you wake someone who has overslept.
Zen had come to save him. That was all he had done.
Dan had counted them every day for years. One, two, three, four. He had been so afraid of losing them. Now he had lost every one, and the last had died at his own hand.
His mind broke.
After that there was nothing left in it to break. It was almost peaceful. There was no one left to protect, and nothing left to be afraid of.
So he smiled.
And followed him.

### The Oath

Then he woke up.
In this room.
On this bed.
At 12:00 AM, March 21st.
For a while he did not understand that he was awake. He lay in the dark and waited for whatever comes after dying. Nothing came. The fan turned. Somebody laughed in the corridor outside.
He sat up. He put both hands on his own chest. There was nothing wrong with him.
He picked up his phone. The date on the screen was March twenty-first. That was two weeks before they had left for Siem Reap. It was a day that had already happened.
He told himself it had been a dream. All of it. The club, the police, the funerals, the knife. But he had never had a dream that lasted five weeks. And he could still feel the weight of that book in his hand, when he had given it back.
The book was on his shelf.
The world had reset.
He lay there until morning with his hand pressed over his mouth, so that nobody would hear him.
When it was light, he got up and walked to Zen’s room. He stood outside the door. He could not make himself knock. Then he heard Zen inside, humming, badly, the way he always did when he was getting dressed.
Dan put his forehead against the wall and stayed there until he could breathe.
Then he went to the cafeteria.
All four of them were at the table by the window. Alive. Arguing about nothing. Asa was in the middle of a story. Jess was stirring her lemonade. Delta had both hands around her coffee. Zen came in behind him, dropped into his chair, and started spinning his pen.
Dan stood in the doorway with a tray in his hands. He had stood at the back of three funerals for the girls at that table.
“Dan?” Asa was waving at him. “Are you coming, or are you going to stand there all day? You look like you’ve seen a ghost.”
He sat down at the end of the table. He could not eat. He looked at each of them in turn.
One. Two. Three. Four.
He did not know why he had been sent back. He did not know who had done it, or whether it would ever happen again. He only knew that he had been given the one thing nobody is ever given. A second chance.
That was when Dan swore an oath.
He would stop the tragedy.
All of it. Every one of them.
Whatever it cost him. However many times it took.

### The Sea

When the group suggested visiting Siem Reap again, he rejected it immediately.
“No.”
His voice was unusually firm.
All four of them stared at him. Dan never said no to anything. Asa had been halfway through describing the sunrise over the temple.
“Not there,” he said. “Anywhere else. Please.”
He could not tell them why. Nobody pushed him. Asa only shrugged, and laughed, and picked something else.
So they changed the destination.
The seaside.
Dan hesitated.
He thought it through carefully. What had killed them in Siem Reap was a nightclub, a dark lane, and strangers. The sea had none of those. It was a quiet beach with one rented cottage. They would be there in daylight. There would be nobody around but the five of them.
Maybe if the location changed…
Maybe the future would change.
He still could not make himself feel safe. Before the trip he warned Zen.
“This trip is dangerous.”
Zen laughed.
“You’re overthinking.”
Dan did not argue. He made his own plans instead. He would not drink. He would not let anyone go out after dark. He would not leave any of them alone. Not for a few minutes. Not for one.
At the beach, he did exactly that.
He sat on the sand beside Zen, but he hardly looked at the water. He watched the road behind the cottage. He watched the track through the trees. He was waiting for a van, for men, for strangers. That was the form the danger had taken last time, and it was the only form he knew to look for.
Jess and Delta were in the shallows. Asa was resting on the veranda. Everyone was where he could see them. He counted them, and for the first time in weeks his shoulders came down.
And the tragedy still came.
It did not come from the road.
He heard Jess scream. When he turned round, she was already out of her depth, and a wave was pulling her further out. Delta was going after her. Neither of them could swim well.
Zen was on his feet and running before Dan had understood what he was looking at.
Dan ran too. Then he heard a different sound behind him, and looked back.
Asa had come running down from the cottage. She had slipped on the wet rock at the bottom of the steps. She was lying against one of the veranda posts, and she was not getting up.
In the water, Zen had reached Jess. Delta’s head went under. It did not come up again.
Dan stopped.
Asa was behind him. Delta was in front of him. He could only go to one of them.
Dan had seen her fall. He had stood between her and the water for one second, deciding. He would count that second for the rest of his lives.
He turned toward the sea.
That was the moment the post gave way, and the old roof of the veranda came down on top of her.
He ran back. He pulled the boards off her with his bare hands. It was too late.
At the beach, Jess survived.
But Delta disappeared.
And Asa died.
They searched for Delta for six days.
The coast guard sent boats up and down the bay from first light until dark. Divers went down off the rocks at the end of the beach. Fishermen from the village went out in their own boats without being asked, and came back in the evenings shaking their heads. A helicopter flew low over the water twice a day, so low that the sea went flat and white under it.
Zen did not leave the beach. He stood at the edge of the water from the first boat in the morning until the last one came in at night, still in the clothes he had been wearing that day. He would not eat unless Jess put the food into his hands. When the police came to ask him questions, he answered them without once taking his eyes off the sea.
Dan walked the beach from one end to the other, again and again. He looked at every dark shape in the waves. Every one of them was a rock, or weed, or nothing.
There was no news. Nothing washed up. Nobody had seen her.
On the seventh morning, a tired man from the coast guard came down to the sand with his cap in his hands. He spoke to them quietly. The currents in the bay were very strong, he said. After this long, there was no hope. They were stopping the search. He was very sorry.
Delta was presumed dead.
That evening, after the boats had gone and the beach was empty, Zen was still there, kneeling at the edge of the water. Jess sat a little way behind him on the sand with a blanket around her shoulders, watching him. He was not looking at her. He was staring out at the place where Delta had gone under, and he did not seem to know that anyone else was there.
Dan heard him say something, very quietly, to nobody.
“I won’t forget her. I’m never going to forget her.”
Dan thought it was only grief. He did not think about those words again for a long time.
Another broken ending.
He had changed the place. He had watched the road all day. It had made no difference at all.
Dan lost his mind again.
He could not bear to look at what he had done with that one second. So he looked at Zen instead. Zen had been sitting ten steps from the water, facing it. Zen had been warned.
This time he blamed Zen.
Relentlessly.
It was easier than counting the second.
He said it in the corridor. He said it in the cafeteria. He waited outside Zen’s room at night to say it.
“You were sitting ten steps from the water.”
“You saw Jess go deeper. You were looking right at her.”
“I told you not to go.”
He told himself that it was the truth, and that Zen deserved to hear the truth. He did not ask himself why he needed to say it so often.
He watched Zen get thinner. He watched him stop going to class. He saw Jess sitting outside Zen’s door with a plate of food going cold beside her. None of it stopped him. Every time he felt that one second rising in his own chest, he went and found Zen, and handed it to him instead.
Eventually they stopped speaking. By then it made no difference. Dan could see that Zen was saying the words to himself.
Until Zen couldn’t bear it.
Zen took his own life.
Jess was the one who found him. Dan heard her screaming from the far end of the corridor.
Dan ran to the scene.
Jess was crying beside Zen’s body. She was holding his hand in both of hers and saying his name, over and over, as if he were only refusing to answer her.
“He said something,” she kept saying, to nobody, to Dan, to the room. “He was still awake when I came in. He said, don’t let me forget her. He said it twice. Don’t let me forget her. Who? Dan, who did he mean?”
Dan did not know. Asa, he thought. Or Delta. He could not ask.
Dan stood in the doorway.
He understood it then, completely. He had done this. Not with his hands, this time. With his mouth. He had found the place where his friend was already hurt, and he had pressed on it every day until something gave way.
Zen had died once trying to save him. Now Zen had died because of him. Both times, it was Dan.
There was a knife on the floor.
Dan picked up the knife.
He was very calm. It seemed simple. He had destroyed the last of them, and he could think of only one thing left to do about that.
But Jess stopped him.
She came up off the floor and threw her arms around him, and held on with all her strength. She was small. She was shaking so hard that he could feel it through his whole body.
She hugged him tightly.
“Stop.”
Her face was pressed into his shoulder.
“Stop. Please. Not you too. I can’t lose everyone. Dan, please.”
He stood there with her arms around him. He had forgotten that there was still one of them left. He had forgotten to count her.
That was the last thing he remembered.
Then he woke up again.
Same room.
Same bed.
Same time.
March twenty-first. Midnight. The book was back on his shelf.
The loop continued.

### The Bus

The next time he refused every trip.
He had worked it out, lying awake. Two trips. Two disasters. The place did not matter. So the answer was not a different place. The answer was no place at all.
No traveling.
No leaving campus.
Asa suggested Siem Reap, and he said no. She suggested the sea, and he said no. Somebody said the mountains, and he said no to that too. When he said no to the sea, Zen let out a breath, very quietly, as if he had been holding it. Dan did not notice. He was already thinking about the roads.
“What is wrong with you lately?” Asa asked. She was laughing, but she meant it.
“I just think we should stay here.”
They gave up asking in the end. The five of them spent those two weeks on campus, in the cafeteria, in the library, on the steps outside the dorms. Dan watched all of them every day.
The date of the trip came. Nobody went anywhere.
Nobody died.
He did not sleep that night. He sat on his bed and waited for something to happen, and nothing did. In the morning all four of them were at the table.
For the first time, he let himself think that it might be over.
Eventually everyone returned home for break.
He could not forbid that. People go home. And home was the safest place he could think of. It was not a trip. It was an ordinary bus on a road he had taken twenty times.
Except Delta and Zen, who waited for their parents.
A dark car with tinted windows was already parked outside Zen’s dorm that morning. Dan noticed it, and forgot it.
Zen walked them to the stop. “See you after the break,” he said, and hit Dan on the shoulder.
Dan did not like leaving two of them behind. But he could not be in two places. He told himself that Zen and Delta were only waiting on a bench in the sun, and that the three on the road needed him more.
Jess, Asa, and Dan boarded a bus to Phnom Penh.
He made the girls sit on the side away from the oncoming traffic, Asa by the window and Jess beside her. He took the seat directly behind them. He looked at every passenger who got on. He watched the driver’s hands on the wheel.
It was an ordinary bus on an ordinary road. Rice fields. Sugar palms. A boy on a bicycle with a sack of something tied on the back.
For the first hour, Asa and Jess talked quietly, the way they always did, with their heads close together. Dan did not listen. He was watching the road.
In the second hour, something changed.
He noticed it the way you notice a change in the sound of an engine. Their voices had gone lower, and sharper. Jess had turned her face toward the aisle, away from Asa. Asa was sitting very straight.
He only caught pieces of it, over the noise of the bus.
“—didn’t have to say it like that,” Jess said.
“Like what? I’m just telling you the truth.”
“Not here. Not now.”
“Then when?” Asa’s voice cracked a little, and she brought it down again. “When is it ever going to be a good time for you, Jess? Two years. I’ve waited two years.”
Dan frowned. He did not know what they were talking about. A plan, he thought. Something about the break. Something one of them had promised and not done.
He looked back at the road. A truck was coming the other way. The driver moved over for it, carefully. Good.
“—not asking you for anything,” Asa was saying. “I just can’t keep pretending I’m fine.”
“Then don’t tell me. Why are you telling me?”
“Because you already know!”
A woman across the aisle looked round at them, and then away.
Jess said something too quiet to hear. Asa answered, and her voice was shaking now. Dan caught the words every day, and you let me, and you knew. Jess shook her head, hard, again and again, with her hands over her ears like a child.
Dan leaned forward. “Hey. Are you two okay?”
Neither of them heard him.
“Look at me,” Asa said. “Jess. Just look at me, once, and tell me you never—”
“STOP!”
Jess screamed it. She was half out of her seat, with her hands still over her ears. It was so loud that the whole bus went quiet. People turned round in their seats.
The driver turned round too. Only his head, and only for a moment, to see who was screaming.
They were on a bend.
Dan saw it all happen, and it took less than a second. He saw the driver’s head turn. He saw the driver’s hands drift with it, a little, on the wheel. He saw the front of the bus slide across the line in the middle of the road. And he saw, through the windscreen, another bus coming round the bend the other way, very fast, filling the glass.
He had been watching the driver’s hands the whole way. He had been watching the road and the trucks and every stranger who got on. He had not been watching the two girls in the seat in front of him.
Metal screamed.
Glass shattered.
Then everything was sideways, and very quiet.
Dan saw his friends die in front of him.
He could not move to reach them. He could not even lift his hand. Asa’s hand was lying across the seat, very close to Jess’s. He could not tell whether they were touching.
He had not taken them anywhere. He had kept them inside the walls for two weeks. He had made them sit on the safe side. He had watched the driver. He had done everything right.
With his final breath he wondered one thing.
What did I do wrong?
Then darkness.
And again—
He woke up.

### The Watcher

This time he broke.
Dan cried uncontrollably in his room.
He sat on the floor with his back against the bed and could not stop. He had stayed calm after Siem Reap. He had stayed calm after the sea. But this time he had done everything right, and it had made no difference.
He went through it, again and again.
He had changed the destination, and they had died. He had refused every destination, and they had died on the way home. He had watched for strangers, and it was the sea. He had watched the water, and it was a bus.
No matter what he changed…
Someone always died.
If it was not the place, and it was not the trip, then it could be anything. A staircase. A road. A bowl of food. Anything at all, at any moment.
So he became paranoid.
Nobody could leave.
Nobody could travel.
When the break came, he told them the buses were not safe. He said there had been accidents on that road. He said it so many times, and so badly, that in the end they agreed to stay on campus just to make him stop.
He followed his friends everywhere. He waited outside their classes. He walked behind them to the dorms and stood under the windows until the lights went out.
He pulled Jess back from the edge of the road when there was no car coming. He took the stairs ahead of Asa, so that he would be underneath her if she fell. He stopped eating until he had seen all four of them eat. He slept two or three hours a night, in his clothes.
Jess and Asa felt uncomfortable.
Jess found him under her window one night and went white. Asa stopped him in the corridor the next day. She was not laughing.
“Dan. You’re scaring her. You’re scaring me. What is going on?”
He could not answer. There was nothing he could say that would not sound mad.
Even Zen tried. “Talk to me,” he said. “Whatever it is. I’m not going to laugh.” He said it carefully, as if he had something of his own that he could not explain either. Dan did not wonder, then, why.
Dan looked at him, alive, and worried, and kind. He had killed this boy twice. He said he was fine.
The girls began to talk about telling someone. A counselor. His parents.
But Delta calmed them.
“Maybe Dan is just stressed after exams,” she said gently.
“Let him be.”
She looked at him as she said it. It was a long, steady look. He thought, at the time, that she was only being kind.

### The Party

Eventually the group insisted on having a party in their dorm room.
It was Asa’s idea. She said that if Dan was going to keep them all locked up like prisoners, the least they could do was eat well.
They couldn’t travel.
They couldn’t go home.
At least they could celebrate together.
Dan didn’t like it.
But he couldn’t stop them. He had already taken the trips away from them, and the break. He could see that if he took this too, they would stop listening to him altogether.
So he prepared instead. He checked the wiring on the electric grill. He moved it away from the curtains. He made sure the window was latched. He put a bucket of water by the door, and Asa laughed at him for a full minute.
He was watching for a fire. He was watching for a fall. He was not watching the five people in the room.
They grilled meat.
Drank alcohol.
For an hour it was good. Zen told a story badly, and Asa told it again properly. Delta ate more than anyone and said very little. Dan sat by the door and counted them, and almost let himself enjoy it.
Jess became very drunk.
He should have seen it sooner. She was filling her own glass, again and again, quickly, with her eyes on Zen. Zen was explaining something to Delta, and Delta was listening.
Dan had seen Jess drink like that once before. At a table in a nightclub, in a life that only he remembered. He reached over and moved the bottle away from her.
“I think that’s enough,” he said quietly.
Then everything fell apart.
Jess suddenly exploded.
“Don’t touch that!”
She was on her feet before anyone understood what was happening. Her glass went over.
“Who are you to tell me what’s enough? You follow us. You stand under my window at night. You won’t let us go home. You won’t let us go anywhere. And now you’re counting my drinks?” Her voice was shaking. “I’m not a child, Dan. I’m not yours to look after. I am so sick of being watched by you!”
Dan sat there with the bottle in his hand. He could not think of anything to say. Everything she had said was true.
“Jess. Hey. Hey.”
Asa was up at once, with her bright voice on and her hands held out. She stepped between them.
“He’s just worried about you. He’s an idiot, but he’s our idiot. Come on. Sit down. Have some water.”
She put a hand on Jess’s arm.
Jess shook it off.
It was the wrong hand, at the wrong moment. Dan watched Jess turn round. Whatever she had been holding back from Asa for four months was already on its way out, and Asa had just stepped in front of it.
She confronted Asa.
“And you. Don’t you manage me. Don’t use that voice on me.” She was crying now. “Why are you so close to Delta?!”
Her voice shook with resentment.
“I was your friend first!”
The room went silent. Zen stopped in the middle of a sentence.
Something crossed Asa’s face before the anger did. If it had made any sense, Dan would have called it relief.
“Oh, now you notice me?” Asa said. “Have you ever noticed that I don’t want to just be your friend?”
Dan did not understand what she meant. He had no time to think about it. Jess was already shouting over her.
“Notice you? I sat next to you for two years!” Jess was shouting. “I laughed at every joke you made. And you never told me one real thing. Then she comes, and you’re whispering with her on the stairs every day. Did you think I couldn’t see?”
Asa, also drunk, felt attacked.
“You never asked!” she shouted back. “You never asked me anything! You were too busy staring at him!”
They began arguing loudly.
Dan stood up. He did not know what to do with his hands. He had prepared for a fire. He had a bucket of water by the door. He had nothing at all for this.
“Stop,” he said. “Both of you. Please. Sit down.”
Neither of them heard him.
Delta looked uncomfortable. She had put down her plate. She was looking from one girl to the other, slowly, like someone watching a thing she had been expecting for a long time.
Then Jess turned toward Zen.
“And you. You think I can’t see where you look?”
Her accusations made the room even more awkward.
She said things nobody had known were in her. That Asa was supposed to be hers. That she thought about Delta being gone, and was not sorry. That she was tired of everyone thinking she was sweet.
She knocked Delta’s cup off the table, and it broke against the wall.
Dan had known Jess for years. He did not recognize her.
Finally Jess stormed out.
Asa followed her.
Zen followed them both.
Dan started to go too—
But he hesitated.
He didn’t want to leave Delta alone.
So he sat beside her.
Neither of them spoke for a moment.
Then Delta asked quietly,
“Why didn’t you follow them?”
“I’ll keep you company.”
“Why?”
Dan answered simply.
“I don’t want you to be alone.”
Delta smiled slightly.
“I’m fine.”
She looked directly at him.
“I won’t die.”
Dan’s expression darkened.
“You have no idea.”
Delta suddenly smiled brightly.
“Have you ever seen me die with your own eyes?”
Something clicked in Dan’s mind.
She was right.
In every timeline—
Delta was either missing.
Or already dead when he found her.
He had never seen her die himself.
Was Delta the key to the loop?
Before he could think further—
A loud noise echoed outside.
They ran out.
And froze.
Jess and Asa lay on the ground below the university building.
They were lying close together. Asa’s hand was still closed around Jess’s sleeve.
Broken.
Dead.
