A window opened somewhere above. Somebody began to scream. People were coming out of the dorms in ones and twos, and stopping when they saw.
Dan looked up.
Zen stood on the rooftop above.
His hand still stretched outward.
He was leaning over the low wall at the edge, with his arm reaching down into the empty air. He was not moving. He looked as if he were still trying to catch them.
Dan collapsed to his knees.
He had kept them on campus. He had not let them travel. He had not let them go home. He had checked the grill and latched the window and put a bucket of water by the door.
And they had died fifty steps from that room, while he was sitting inside it.
He had left them alone again. Only for a few minutes. It was always only a few minutes.
Why…
Why can’t I change anything?
Delta was standing beside him. She did not say anything. She did not kneel.
He closed his eyes.
Waiting for the reset.
He knew what came next. The dark. Then the white ceiling, and midnight, and March. He would wake up and they would be alive, and he would try again. He counted his own breaths and waited for it.
He heard a siren, far off, coming closer.
He heard someone crying.
He felt the cold of the ground coming through the knees of his trousers.
He opened his eyes. He was still there. They were still there.
He closed his eyes again, harder.
But it never came.
The night went on. People came and covered them. Somebody led Zen down from the roof. Somebody put a blanket around Dan’s shoulders and asked him his name.
Then, for the first time in more lives than he could count, the sun came up on the day after.

### Morning After Morning

Three months passed.
Life moved forward for the first time.
March became April. April became a month he had never seen before. Dan woke each morning and checked the date and did not know what to do with it.
For the first few weeks he still woke at midnight, every night, with his eyes already on the ceiling. Each time it was the wrong day. Each time the two of them were still dead.
There were two funerals. He had been to both of them before, in another life, and he knew where to stand.
Classes started again. People stopped whispering when he walked past. The table by the window had three chairs at it, and then nobody sat there at all.
He questioned everything.
Why hadn’t the loop restarted?
He went through it the way he went through everything. Siem Reap had reset. The sea had reset. The bus had reset. What was different this time? It was the same campus, the same month, the same people. He could not find it.
He began to wonder whether this was simply how it ended. Whether he had used up all his chances, and this was the world he would have to live in.
Zen eventually explained what happened that night.
It took him weeks. He told it on the back stairs, late, without looking at Dan, in short pieces with long silences between them.
Jess was drunk and emotional.
She ran to the rooftop.
Asa followed her.
Zen had gone after both of them. He was a flight of stairs behind. He could hear their voices before he reached the door.
They argued.
Jess confessed she hated Delta.
“She kept saying it,” Zen said. “‘I hate her. I hate her. I wish she’d never come.’ And then she said something to Asa. I didn’t hear all of it. Something like, ‘and I hate you for choosing her.’”
Asa slapped her.
“I don’t think she meant to. I think she was as shocked as Jess was.”
Jess stepped back from it, and the wall at the edge was low.
Jess slipped.
Asa tried to catch her.
“She got hold of her sleeve,” Zen said. His voice had almost gone. “She wouldn’t let go. If she’d let go, she’d be alive.”
They both fell.
Zen arrived too late. His hand had been an arm’s length short.
“I was right there, Dan. I was three steps away. If I’d run up those stairs instead of walking. If I hadn’t stopped at the door to listen.”
Dan knew that voice. It was his own, counting one second on a beach.
He should have said something. He should have said, it wasn’t your fault. He could not make the words come.
Nobody had pushed anyone. That was what he could not get past. There had been no stranger, no wave, no bus. Only a few drinks, a few words, a slap, and one step backward.
Dan felt empty.
Nothing made sense anymore.
Delta was the one who surprised him. He had expected her to be calm about it, the way she was calm about everything. Instead she stopped coming to the cafeteria. Once he saw her alone at their old table with a lemonade in front of her, a drink she never ordered. She didn’t touch it. She sat there until the ice had melted.

### The Trigger

Months later, Zen confessed something to Dan.
They were in the corridor outside the lecture hall. Zen had been quiet for days. He kept starting sentences and not finishing them.
“I need to tell someone,” he said at last. “And you’re the only one left.”
Dan waited.
“I think I like Delta.”
He said it as if it frightened him.
“I thought I loved Jess. I really did. But it was never like this. I didn’t know there was a difference until now. And she’s dead, and I can’t even tell her I’m sorry.”
Dan exploded.
He had not known it was in him. Three months of empty mornings came out all at once.
“Because of her, Asa and Jess died!”
He heard himself shouting. He could not stop.
“We were fine before she came. The four of us were fine. Then she sat down at our table, and Asa couldn’t stop talking to her, and you couldn’t stop looking at her, and Jess got smaller every single day. You saw it. I saw it. None of this would have happened without her!”
Zen shook his head.
“That’s not her fault.”
“Then whose fault is it?”
“I don’t know. Mine, probably. But not hers. She didn’t do anything, Dan. She only sat with us.”
They argued.
It was the worst argument they had ever had. Things were said about Jess that should not have been said so soon.
And Delta heard everything.
Dan saw her over Zen’s shoulder, too late.
She was standing at the end of the corridor with a plastic cup of coffee in her hand. He did not know how long she had been there. Her face showed nothing at all.
She walked away silently.
After that she avoided them.
She changed where she sat in class. She left by a different door. If she saw either of them on a path, she turned onto another one.
Zen’s mental state collapsed again.
He was carrying the roof already. Now he was carrying this as well. He believed he had been too slow to save two friends, and that the girl he cared about had heard his best friend blame her for their deaths. He stopped sleeping. He stopped eating in the cafeteria. Dan saw him sitting on the back stairs for hours, doing nothing.
Dan knew he had made it worse. He did not know how to take it back.
One day he stopped Delta to talk.
Dan was on the far side of the courtyard. He saw Zen step in front of her on the path. He saw Zen speaking, quickly, with his hands open. He could not hear the words. He saw Delta standing very still while she listened.
But she looked at him with cold eyes.
She spoke clearly. Dan heard every word, even from that distance.
“I wish the one who died that day was you… not Asa and Jess.”
It was the first time Dan had ever heard her voice shake.
The words destroyed him.
Dan saw it happen. Zen did not flinch, or argue, or step back. Something simply went out of his face.
Zen stood motionless.
Delta’s hand had come up, a little, as if she wanted to take the words back out of the air.
Dan watched from far away.
He had started walking toward them. He was not yet running.
Then suddenly—
Zen ran.
He did not run away from her. He ran past her, across the courtyard, toward the far side of the campus.
Dan realized too late where he was going.
The rooftop.
The same building.
Dan ran after him. He shouted Zen’s name. He went through the door and up the stairs two at a time, with his breath tearing in his chest. He could hear Zen’s footsteps above him, always one flight ahead.
He came out onto the roof.
Zen was already at the low wall, at the place where the two girls had fallen. He did not look back.
“STOP!”
But Zen had already jumped.
Dan reached the wall with his arm stretched out. His hand closed on nothing. He was an arm’s length short.
He knew then exactly what Zen had felt on this roof, three months before.
He did not have time to feel anything else.
Everything faded.
It happened at once this time. There was no waiting on his knees, no siren, no night to get through. The roof and the sky simply went dark.
Dan opened his eyes again.
Back in his room.
March 21st.
12:00 AM.
The fan was turning. The book was on his shelf.
He did not cry this time. He lay still and went back through all of it.
Three months ago he had knelt beside Jess and Asa and waited for this ceiling, and it had not come. Today it had come in a moment. Something had been different between those two days. He made himself think about what.
Siem Reap. The three girls had died, and the world had gone on for weeks. Then Zen on the floor, and then the white ceiling.
The sea. Asa and Delta had been gone for weeks. Then Zen dead, Jess’s arms around him, and then the white ceiling.
The bus. He had died himself that time, so he could not know what happened afterward. But Zen had not been on that bus.
The rooftop. Jess and Asa on the ground, and no reset. Three months of mornings, for as long as Zen was breathing. And then, the same second Zen was gone, the ceiling.
It had never been the girls. It had never been Dan. Every time, it was the same one.
That was when he finally understood.
Zen’s death…
was the trigger for the regression.
Dan looked at the ceiling for a long time.
He had spent all these lives waiting for the reset as if it were weather. Something that came or did not come. Now he knew what brought it.
He did not let himself think yet about what that meant. He only held on to the fact.
For the first time in many lives, he had something he could use.

## V. Arc 5 — Dan (Part 2)
@pov Dan

### The Ceiling

Dan opened his eyes.
He didn’t move.
He stared at the white ceiling above him.
The first time he woke here, he had thought he was dead. The second time he had sworn an oath. After the bus he had cried on the floor until morning.
This time there was none of that.
No tears.
No pain.
Only calm.
It was not peace. It was what is left when a person has been afraid for so long that the fear wears out.
At this point, he already knew something.
He had tried changing the place. He had tried refusing every place. He had tried watching them every hour of the day and night.
No matter what he changed…
No matter what decision he made…
The ending always returned to tragedy.
A club. The sea. A road. A roof. It found them every time. It did not seem to care what he did.
He had been treating each loop as a new chance to win. He saw now that he had only been losing the same game in different rooms.
So instead of trying to save everyone again, he decided something different.
He would stop asking how to keep them alive for two more weeks. He would ask the question he had never had time for. Why was this happening at all? Why this night? Why him?
If he couldn’t stop the ending…
Then he would find the reason for the loop.
And break it.
He had one fact to start from. Zen’s death sent the world back. He did not understand it, but it was solid, and it was more than he had ever had before.
Dan began replaying every timeline he could remember.
He went through them slowly, one person at a time, looking for anything that did not fit.
Jess.
She stirred her lemonade the same way in every life. She was surprised by the same things. She had never once looked at him as if she knew.
Asa.
She told the same stories on the same days, and laughed in the same places. If she had remembered dying, she could not have laughed like that.
Zen.
He had laughed at Dan’s warning before the sea. He had confessed about Delta as if it were the first time he had ever felt it. It was always the first time for Zen.
None of them ever remembered the loops.
He was sure of that. He had watched them too closely to be wrong.
But what about Delta?
Sometimes she said strange things…
Let him be. I won’t die. Have you ever seen me die with your own eyes?
But that didn’t necessarily mean anything.
Maybe she was just comforting him when he looked uneasy.
Still… something about her felt different.
Special.
But he couldn’t explain why.

### The Bench

The next morning, Dan walked toward the cafeteria.
He had not slept. He had spent the night going over everything Delta had ever said to him, in every life. It was not much. He realized that in all this time he had never once asked her a question about herself.
Before he reached it, he noticed someone sitting alone on a bench.
It was Delta.
She sat under the sunlight quietly, looking at a field of flowers across the courtyard. There was a plastic cup of iced coffee on the bench beside her. Most of the ice was already gone.
She was the only person out there. Everyone else was hurrying along the covered walkway, keeping to the shade. She sat in the full light with her face turned up a little, perfectly still, as if she had all the time in the world.
Dan stopped.
He had passed that bench in a dozen lives. He had seen her on it before, and he had never thought anything of it. He had always been on his way to count the others.
This time he did not go to the cafeteria.
Dan walked over and sat down.
She did not look round. She did not seem surprised.
For a moment, neither of them spoke.
He did not know how to begin. He could hardly ask her what he wanted to ask. So he began with nothing at all, to see what she would do with it.
Then he asked casually,
“Are those flowers beautiful?”
Delta hummed softly.
“Hmm.”
It was not a yes, and it was not a no.
Dan looked at them again. They were ordinary flowers. Yellow, mostly. He had never noticed them before either.
“Do you like them?”
Delta shook her head slightly.
“I’m sitting here because I like the sunlight.”
She stretched her hand slightly into the warm light. She turned it over, slowly, and looked at the sun on her palm, the way another person might look at something they had been given.
“I like the warmth of sunlight. I can’t get enough of it.”
Dan laughed lightly. It surprised him. He had not laughed in a very long time.
“Most people try to avoid sunlight. But not you.”
Delta kept looking forward.
“I used to live in a place that had no sunlight for a very long time.”
She said it plainly, the way someone might mention the town they grew up in.
Dan tried to think where that could be. A country far to the north. A basement room. A hospital. None of them seemed right, and he did not know why.
Then she slowly turned her head and looked straight into Dan’s eyes.
“So I really yearn for it.”
Dan smirked and teased her.
“You know sunlight can give you skin cancer, right? That could make you die pretty quickly.”
Delta answered calmly.
“Cancer isn’t as painful as drowning.”
She paused.
“Or being raped… and then killed.”
Dan’s smile froze instantly.
The courtyard went on around them. Someone laughed near the cafeteria doors. A bicycle went past.
“…What did you say?”
Delta tilted her head slightly.
“Hm?”
She smiled innocently.
Dan stared at her in shock.
“How do you know that?”
He shook his head quickly.
“No… that’s not the right question.”
His voice became serious.
“Who are you exactly… Delta?”
Delta looked at him calmly.
“If I tell you I’m a witch… would you believe me?”
A week ago he would have laughed. But a week ago, for him, was several deaths back.
Dan took a slow breath, trying to calm himself.
“Then explain something to me.”
His eyes sharpened.
“Why are you trapping us in this loop?”
“Delta… what do you want?”
Delta shook her head gently.
“I didn’t trap you in this loop.”
She looked at him quietly.
“You trapped yourself.”
Dan frowned.
“…How?”
Delta spoke softly.
“This loop should have ended a long time ago.”
“But your obsession keeps it alive.”
Dan’s mind started racing.
“I don’t understand.”

### Seventy-Five

Delta picked up the cup beside her and turned it in the light.
“Someone once taught me how to drink coffee.”
“If the sweetness is zero, it’s honest. But it’s too bitter. Nobody finishes the glass.”
“If it’s a hundred, it’s too sweet. You can’t taste the coffee at all. You’re drinking sugar and telling yourself it’s coffee.”
Dan muttered,
“So… fifty-fifty?”
Delta shook her head.
“No.”
“At fifty they fight. And bitter always wins a fair fight. One bad spoonful ruins a glass. One good spoonful doesn’t fix one.”
She smiled faintly.
“Seventy-five is the best. Three parts sweet, to carry one part bitter.”
“You can taste the sweetness…”
“But the bitterness is still there to give it flavor. It has to be there. The bitterness is the coffee.”
She looked directly into Dan’s eyes.
“Humans are the same.”
Dan’s brain went blank.
“…I don’t understand.”
“What does that have to do with my obsession?”
“What have you been ordering, Dan?” she asked. “Every time you wake up.”
He said nothing.
“A hundred percent. Everyone alive. Nothing lost. Not one bitter drop anywhere in the glass.”
She set the cup down.
“That isn’t an ending. That’s why it never ends.”
Delta stood up slowly.
Then she stepped in front of him.
She leaned slightly closer and asked calmly,
“Dan.”
“Do you think humans are purely evil?”
Dan said nothing.
After a moment, she spoke again.
“Or purely good?”
Silence.
Dan looked completely lost.
So Delta asked again.
“Let me ask differently.”
She looked directly into his eyes.
“Dan… do you believe you are a good person?”
Yes, he almost said. Of course. He was the one trying to save them. He was the only one who had ever tried.
But the word didn’t come.
His mind was filled with too many memories.
Too many deaths.
Too many failures.
One second on a beach. A voice saying you chose, you chose, you chose until a boy stopped smiling. It had been his voice.
He just sat there silently.
Delta straightened up.
“Maybe you need time to process this.”
She turned and began walking away.
“If you figure it out…”
“You can come find me.”
Dan remained sitting on the bench.
Watching her back slowly disappear in the sunlight.
Beside him, the last of the ice melted in her cup.
His mind was completely blank.

## VI. Arc 6 — Delta (Escape)
@pov Delta

### The Tunnel

Darkness.
The tunnel stretched endlessly forward.
A girl ran through it.
She looked about nineteen. She was not.
Her white dress had long stopped being white.
It was soaked with dirt, dried blood, and the stains of wounds that never had time to heal. It was the only thing she owned. She could not remember when it had been given to her.
Her bare feet slipped on the cold floor.
There was water on it, a thin, cold sheet that ran the same way she was running. The stones under the water were slick with something soft. Twice already she had gone down on her hands and knees.
Still she ran.
She had not run in a very long time. For longer than she could count, she had walked only as far as the men allowed, from a cell to a table and back. Her legs did not remember how. They shook under her at every step.
Her breathing was broken, shallow, like every breath was cutting her lungs open.
The air was wet and old. It tasted of rust.
She couldn’t see anything.
The tunnel was completely black.
It was not the dark of a room at night. There was no edge to it, no grey, no shape. She held her eyes wide open and it made no difference. She ran with one hand stretched out in front of her and the other trailing along the wall.
The wall turned.
She slammed into the wall.
The rough stone tore open the skin on her shoulder.
She felt the blood start, warm against the cold. She hardly noticed the pain. She had been hurt far worse, far more carefully, by people who took their time.
She didn’t stop.
She found the wall again with her fingers, and found where it bent, and pushed herself off it.
She ran again.
And again.
She fell. She got up. She hit the wall on the other side and got up from that.
Behind her there was nothing. No footsteps. No voices. Only the sound of the water and her own breath.
That was worse than hearing them. It meant she could not tell how close they were.
Because she knew.
If she slowed down…
If she stopped even for a second…
They would catch her.
They had always caught the ones who ran. She had heard it happen, through the wall, more times than she wanted to remember.
And when they caught her—
Cold metal.
The table. It was always cold, however long she lay on it. It took the warmth out of her back and never gave any of it back.
Bright white lights.
They hung directly over her face. When she shut her eyes she could still see them, red, through her own eyelids. In that room there had been no such thing as dark.
Straps cutting into her wrists.
Her wrists. Her ankles. One across her forehead, so that she could not turn her head away from what they were doing.
Scalpels.
She knew the sound a tray of them made when it was set down beside her. She knew it better than she knew her own name.
Screams.
Her screams.
In the first hundred years she had screamed words. She had begged. Later she only made the sound. The men wrote things down while she made it, and none of them ever looked at her face.
Her legs moved faster.
Her body was already beyond exhaustion.
Her knees were bleeding. Her shoulder was bleeding. There was a pain in her side like a hand closing. Any human would have been lying in the water by now.
But fear pushed her forward.
She would rather die in this tunnel than go back to that table. She knew that with her whole body. And she knew they would not let her die.
Run.
Run.
Run.
She could not think any more. She could not have said what she was running toward, or what she would do if she reached it. Almost everything she had ever been was gone. Her family’s faces. The house she was born in. The sound of her own voice when it was not screaming.
She only remembered one thing.
Sunlight.
She remembered a morning. It was the last one, though she had not known that then. She had been sitting on a doorstep with her eyes closed and her face turned up, doing nothing. The light had lain on her skin like a hand.
She could no longer remember what colour the sky had been. She could not remember the street, or the year. But she remembered exactly how that had felt.
For centuries she had dreamed about sunlight.
On the table, under the white lights, she went back to that doorstep. In the cell, in the dark, she went back to it. It was the only place they had never been able to follow her.
Warm.
Soft.
Alive.
She wanted to see it again.
She was not asking for freedom. She did not really believe in that. She was not even asking to live.
Just once.

### Forty Minutes

It had begun with the lights.
She had been on the table when they went out. All of them, at once, with a sound like a breath being let go. In four hundred years the lights had never gone out.
For a moment nobody in the room moved. Then the men began to shout. Something metal fell and rolled across the floor. Somebody ran into the edge of the table in the dark and swore.
She lay still. She thought it was something they were doing to her. A new test. She waited for it to hurt.
It did not hurt. It was only dark. Real dark, the first she had seen in that room, soft and complete. She had forgotten how gentle it was on the eyes.
The straps on that table had no buckles anymore. They had a small red eye that held them shut. The eye closed. The straps fell open.
She felt them go slack on her wrists. She did not understand it at first. She lifted one hand, and it came up. Nothing stopped it.
In the old days there had been iron and keys, and iron does not forget to be locked. They had replaced the iron with something cleverer. Clever things fail.
She sat up. She put her bare feet on the floor. The men were still shouting to each other about the power, and the door, and who had the keys to something. None of them was thinking about her. They had not needed to think about her for a very long time.
She walked out of the room. The door, which had always sealed itself with a hiss, was standing open.
She never learned why the lights went out. Not for a long time.
She had gone to the cells first. She did not even think about it. Her feet took her there.
The cells were older. They still had iron.
She could hear the others in the dark. They had felt the lights go too. They were calling to each other, low and quick, in the old language the men had never learned.
She pulled at the first door until her nails broke. She threw her whole weight against it. She tried what little magic she had left, and it was like trying to lift a stone with a breath.
A hand came through the bars and closed over hers. It was thin, and very cold.
A voice on the other side, a voice she had known for three hundred years, said, Go. See it for us.
It was the one who had taught her the names of the others, through the wall, when she was new. They had never once seen each other’s faces.
She did not want to let go of that hand. She did not promise to come back. She did not believe she would live long enough to keep a promise.
So she went.
She knew the way. She had been carried in along it, when the place was still a cellar under a fortress, before the concrete and the steel. She had been tied, and there had been torches, and a smell of river.
The old water tunnel. They had sealed it with a grate. The grate had been rusting for longer than any of them had been alive.
She found it by the cold air on her face. She took hold of the bars and pulled. The first one came away in her hands in flakes, like wet bread.
Then she was through, and running.
The escape only lasted forty minutes.
But to her…
It felt longer than the four hundred years she had spent inside that laboratory.
In the laboratory, at least, she had known what would happen next. Here she knew nothing. Every step might be the one where a hand closed on her arm.
Her legs trembled.
Her vision blurred.
She was seeing things that were not there. Sparks. Faces. A doorstep. She knew they were not real. There was nothing in that tunnel to see.
Then—
A faint glow appeared ahead.
It was grey, and very small, no bigger than a coin held at arm’s length.
At first she thought it was another illusion.
She shut her eyes and opened them again. It was still there. It did not move when she moved her head.
But the light grew brighter.
Warmer.
It was not white. That was how she knew. Every light she had seen for four hundred years had been white, or the colour of fire. This was yellow. It was the colour she had forgotten.
Her heart began pounding violently.
She stumbled forward.
Then suddenly—
The tunnel ended.
And sunlight exploded across her vision.
She collapsed to her knees.
The warmth touched her skin.
Soft.
Gentle.
Real.
For the first time in four hundred years, she saw the sun again.
The warmth she had begged for.
The warmth she had cried for.
The warmth she had almost forgotten.
Her fingers slowly touched the ground.
Warm dirt.
Real earth.
Not cold steel.
Not stone walls.
She began to laugh.
A broken, trembling laugh.
This girl was Delta.
And she was a witch.

### Four Hundred Years

Long ago, witches had lived quietly among humans.
They were not what the stories said. They did not fly, or curse cattle, or steal children. Most of their magic was small. They knew which plants eased a fever. They could tell when a person was lying, or grieving, or about to fall ill. They could go unnoticed in a crowd when they wished to. And they lived a very long time.
They were midwives and healers and the old woman at the edge of the village whom everyone visited and nobody mentioned. For centuries that was enough to keep them safe.
But humans discovered them.
A long life is the hardest thing to hide. Sooner or later someone notices that the healer who delivered them is delivering their grandchildren, and that her face has not changed.
And humans were curious creatures.
At first they were only afraid. Then somebody asked the question that mattered. If these women did not grow old, and did not fall sick, what was it inside them that made it so? And could it be taken out?
They wanted witch abilities.
Their magic.
Their long lifespan.
Kings wanted it. Bishops wanted it. Rich men who were afraid of dying wanted it more than anyone.
So they hunted them.
Captured them.
They did not burn them. That was only what they told the villages. A burned witch was of no use to anyone. The ones who were taken were brought alive, at night, to a fortress in the hills.
And turned them into experiments.
Delta had been taken from a doorstep, on an ordinary morning. She was young, as witches count it. She had not yet learned most of what her mother knew.
The first men who came for her carried torches and holy books, and they called what they did questioning. Their grandsons carried lamps and bone saws and called it medicine. The ones after that wore white coats and called it research.
She watched them grow old and die, one generation after another. She learned each new set of faces, and then those faces wrinkled and were gone, and their sons came down the stairs to take their places. Not one of them ever seemed to wonder what it was like to be the one who stayed.
The prison changed around her. Torchlight became gaslight. Gaslight became the white lights that never went out. The stone was covered in tile, and the tile in steel.
The language the men spoke changed too, so slowly that she learned it without noticing. Their tools became smaller and cleaner and quieter.
The work on the table stayed the same.
When Delta was first imprisoned, there had been hundreds of witches.
Children.
Adults.
Elders.
Whole families had been taken together. In the first years the cells were so full that they slept sitting up.
All locked in the underground laboratory.
They could not see each other. But they could talk through the walls, and they did, for centuries. They passed names along the corridor, and stories, and the old songs, so that nothing would be lost. That was how she knew every one of them.
Four hundred years later…
Only a few dozen remained alive.
The rest had died on surgery tables.
Their bodies cut open again and again.
Their screams echoing through the corridors.
Delta remembered every single one.
She remembered the smell of blood.
The sound of bones breaking.
The feeling of being alive when she should have died long ago.
And dying had not ended it for them. That was the worst of what she knew. At the very end the men took one more thing from each body, a thing that was not flesh, and they kept it. A witch could not rest without it. Delta could feel them still, somewhere behind the walls, every one of them. Not gone. Waiting.
Near the end, the men had learned to take it from the living too. There was a hollow under her own ribs now, where something had been.
In four hundred years, not one of the men in that place had ever looked at her and seen a person. Not one. She had watched for it, in the beginning.
Hatred filled her chest.
A hatred so deep it had kept her alive all these centuries.
She had sworn something long ago.
If she ever escaped…
If she ever regained her strength…
She would destroy humanity.
Every last one of them.

### The Road

So she ran.
Even after escaping the tunnel.
Even after reaching the surface.
She had allowed herself a few minutes on her knees in the sun. Then the fear came back. The men would find the open grate. They would come out of the same hole she had come out of.
She got up.
Her legs could barely hold her weight anymore.
But she kept moving.
She went downhill, because it was easier, and away from the sun, because the hills had been to the east of the fortress when she was carried in. She did not know if that was still true. She did not know if anything was still true.
The world was too large. For four hundred years the farthest thing she had been able to see was a wall. Now there was no wall anywhere. The sky went up and up. It made her dizzy, and she had to look at her own feet to keep walking.
Everything was loud and soft at once. Wind in leaves. Insects. A bird that she remembered the shape of, though not its name. She kept stopping to touch things. Bark. Grass. A stone that had been warmed by the sun.
She drank from a stream with both hands. It was the first water in four centuries that nobody had measured out for her.
Farther.
Farther.
She saw things she did not understand. A line of tall metal frames marching across the hills, with ropes strung between them. A white scratch being drawn slowly across the sky. Far below, a grey road with small bright shapes sliding along it faster than any horse.
Humans had made those. She was sure of it. They had been busy while she lay on the table.
She kept away from all of it.
Far away from the place that had stolen four hundred years of her life.
The sky slowly turned dark.
She stopped walking to watch it, though she knew she should not stop. The sun went down red behind the hills, and the clouds caught fire and went out. She had forgotten that it did that. She had forgotten that it was different every time.
Night fell.
This dark was not like the tunnel. There were stars in it. She had forgotten those too.
In the distance, small lights flickered.
Human houses.
She stood and looked at them. There were people in there, eating, sleeping, safe behind their doors. Their grandfathers’ grandfathers had strapped her to a table, and they did not even know her name.
The hatred came up in her so strongly that for a moment it held her upright.
Then it was not enough.
Her body swayed.
Her vision spun.
She had not eaten since before the lights went out. She had lost a great deal of blood. She had been running, or walking, for the whole of a day.
Then suddenly—
Her knees gave out.
She collapsed in the middle of a dirt road.
She did not feel herself land.
Rain woke her.
Cold drops hit her face.
She did not know what it was at first. She thought someone was standing over her. Then she understood that it was only water, falling out of the sky, on everything, for nothing.
Her body was soaked within seconds.
But she didn’t move.
She could have crawled to the side of the road. She could have looked for a tree to lie under. She did not see the point.
She simply lay there.
Looking up at the dark sky.
The rain fell straight into her open eyes. The road turned to mud under her back. It was cold, and she was shaking, and none of it seemed to be happening to her.
She had done it. She was out. For four hundred years she had thought of nothing else.
And she was lying in the mud, alone, in a world she did not know, with nobody left alive who had ever loved her. Everyone she could call her own was still down there behind an iron door. She had left them.
This was what she had run toward.
Then something inside her broke.
She began laughing.
It came out of her before she knew it was coming.
Loud.
Unstable.
It did not sound like her. It did not sound like anyone. She laughed until she could not breathe, with the rain running into her mouth.
Then the laughter turned into sobbing.
She had not cried in more than three hundred years. She had stopped on the table, long ago, when she understood that it changed nothing. She had thought she no longer could.
It all came now.
Four hundred years.
Four hundred years of pain.
Of humiliation.
Of being looked at and never seen. Of being handled like a thing. Of hearing children taken down the corridor and not come back.
Of rage.
Rage with nowhere to go. She had swallowed it every day for four centuries, because there had been nothing else to do with it.
She screamed into the rain.
She screamed at the sky, and at the little lights of the houses, and at every human sleeping warm inside them.
But the sound disappeared into the night.
No one came. No window opened. The rain went on falling exactly as before.
She lay there with her throat torn, breathing.
Then—
There was a sound she did not know. A low, rough growl, far off, getting louder.
A motorbike approached.
Its headlight cut through the rain. Something rattled behind it, glass against glass.
The light was white.
A single white eye, coming straight down the road toward her.
She knew what came with white light.
Delta didn’t move.
She did not have the strength to run again. And she found that she did not want to.
Instead, her fingers slowly curled.
Magic gathered weakly in her palm.
There was very little of it left. The men had seen to that. It was not enough to break an iron door. But a human body was a much softer thing than a door.
When the rider came closer…
She would kill him.
Just one human.
Just one.
To release the hatred burning inside her chest.
The motorbike got closer.
Closer.
The headlight found her. The bike swerved.
Then suddenly—
CRASH!
The bike slipped and fell.
Glass broke. Ice scattered across the wet road like spilled teeth.
A man’s voice shouted angrily.
“Damn it! Who leaves something in the road like—”
His voice stopped.
The old man stared at the girl lying in the rain.
“…What the hell…”
His voice turned panicked.
“Hey! Hey! Are you alive?!”
Delta couldn’t make sense of what he was saying.
Her vision was fading again. The magic went out in her hand like a wet match.
But she felt something strange.
Warm arms lifting her carefully.
Nobody had touched her in four hundred years except to hold her down.
The old man carried her like something fragile.
And the darkness swallowed her again.

### The Glass on the Tray

When she woke up again…
She was inside a hospital room.
Old white walls.
Paint peeling with age.
The smell of medicine filled the air.
White walls. A bed. The smell. For one terrible moment she thought she had never left.
She thought the tunnel had been a dream. The sun, the stream, the stars, all of it. She thought she had only closed her eyes on the table and they had let her sleep.
She tried to lift her hands. She was sure they would not come.
They came. There were no straps. There was only a thin blanket, and a needle taped into the back of her hand with a tube running up from it.
But there was a window. And there was a square of sun on the blanket.
She stared at it. The laboratory had never had a window. Not in four hundred years.
The old man stood beside her bed.
He was small and brown and wrinkled, with a grey moustache and a plaster across the bridge of his nose. One lens of his glasses was cracked. He was holding his cap in both hands.
“She’s awake!” he shouted toward the hallway.
A nurse rushed in.
Delta tried to pull herself back against the wall. She could barely move.
They checked her injuries.
The nurse took hold of her wrist. Delta went rigid. But the woman only held it lightly, with two fingers, and looked at a watch, and let go again.
Bandaged her wounds.
Nobody held her down. Nobody wrote anything while she flinched. When the nurse touched her torn shoulder and Delta hissed with pain, the woman said something low, and worked more slowly.
Delta did not understand it. She did not trust it.
Delta glared at them with pure hatred.
The nurse did not seem to notice. The old man noticed. He looked back at her, mildly, the way a person looks at a dog that has been beaten and might bite.
But she was too weak to move.
So she waited.
She was there for many days. She learned things by listening. The old man had told the nurses she was his niece from the countryside. He had paid for the bed. He sold iced coffee from a cart fixed to the side of his motorbike, outside the market, and the nurses called him Ta, the way you would call any old man grandfather.
She learned that he had broken his glasses and half his stock when he swerved to miss her on the road. He had lost three days of selling while his cart was mended. The nurses teased him about it. He only laughed.
Every evening, after he closed the cart, he came. He brought rice in a tin box and a glass of iced coffee on a tray. He talked about the weather and the price of condensed milk. She never answered.
He did not seem to need her to. He pulled the chair up beside the bed and sat down in it with a grunt, and talked to her as if she had asked.
Nobody had ever sat down beside her bed before. In four hundred years the men had always stood.
He never asked her name. He never asked who had hurt her, though he must have seen the marks on her wrists. He called her child, and left it at that.
She ate the rice, because she needed her strength.
She never touched the coffee. Every morning the glass was still there, the ice gone, the coffee thinned to brown water.
He took the glass away without a word and brought a fresh one the next evening.
Recovered slowly.
And secretly prepared something.
She could not fight them. She knew that. She had barely enough magic left to warm her own hands. There were more humans in the world now than she could count, and they had machines.
But there was one kind of magic that did not need strength.
A curse.
Her mother had told her about it once, when she was small, in the voice people use for things that must never be done. A curse did not draw on a witch’s power. It drew on her life. Any witch could cast one, however weak she was, as long as she was willing to pay.
A massive one.
A curse born from centuries of suffering.
She did not have to search for the words. She had been collecting them for four hundred years without knowing it. Every name she had heard passed along the corridor. Every one who had been taken to the table and not come back. She built the curse out of their names.
A curse that would spread across humanity like a plague.
She built it at night, a word at a time, under her breath. It would open from her like a ring on water. The nearest first. Then the building. Then the town. Then on, and on, shore after shore.
It would not choose. It would not ask who had been cruel and who had not. It would pass through every human it reached, and stop their hearts, and move on to the next.
But such magic required a price.
It would burn her life as it travelled. The farther it went, the more of her it would use.
Her life.
A witch’s life is long. She had centuries still in front of her. Even so, it would not be enough.
She had done the sum. One life, however long, could not pay for every human in the world. There were far too many of them now. The curse would cross the town, and the province, and perhaps the whole country. Somewhere beyond that her life would run out. The curse would stop there, and she would be dead.
It would not be all of them. It would only be as many as she could reach.
Delta didn’t care.
She had no use for those centuries. She had nobody to spend them with, and nothing she wanted to do in them except this.
So in the daytime she lay still and ate the rice and let the nurse change her bandages. She listened to the women laughing in the corridor. She listened to the old man telling her about his day. None of it reached her. She was only waiting for dark.
And every night, after the lights in the corridor went down, she added another name.
She could not kill them all. But she was glad of every one she could.
If as many of them as possible died with her…
It would be enough.

### Midnight

The night she finished preparing the curse…
The old man had not come that evening. It was the first time. She had noticed it, and told herself that it made no difference.
The corridor lights went down. The hospital grew quiet. Somewhere a clock said that it was almost midnight.
She had one name left to add. She had kept it for the end. It was her mother’s.
She activated it.
She spoke the last word, and felt it leave her like a held breath. The first ring was no wider than the room.
It was a strange feeling. It was like a thread being drawn slowly out of the middle of her chest. It did not hurt yet. That would come later, when the ring was wide.
The curse worked slowly at first. She had made it that way. A human standing inside the ring would feel nothing for a little while. Then their heart would stop. By the time anyone understood what was happening, it would already have passed over them and gone on.
She lay back on the pillow and felt it begin to spread.
A dark smile slowly appeared on her lips.
Four hundred years. And now, at last, it was their turn.
Then—
There were footsteps in the corridor. Slow ones, with a slight limp. She knew them.
The door opened.
The old man walked in.
Right through it.
She felt the ring pass over him as he stepped across the doorway. It was like feeling someone walk through a spider’s web that was tied to her own skin.
He carried a tray.
Food.
And a glass of iced coffee.
He was out of breath. “Late, late, I know,” he said. “The cart broke a wheel. I had to push it home.”
She looked at him.
She could have told him to leave. Three words. She knew them in his language. Go away. Now. He would have been puzzled, and hurt, and he would have gone, and he would have been outside the ring before it took hold.
He was a human. That was what she told herself. He was one of them. His grandfathers had done nothing to stop it. If she began making exceptions, there would be no end to it.
She said nothing.
He laughed casually.
“You should eat before sleeping.”
He placed the tray beside her. He had brought the rice in the same dented tin box as always, and a boiled egg, which was new.
“I know coffee at night isn’t healthy…”
“But it helps calm the mind.”
He made her eat. He stood there with his arms folded until she had finished the rice and the egg, the way you stand over a child. She ate. It was easier than looking at him.
Then he pushed the coffee toward her.
“Thirty years I make this. Every night you let it die on the tray. Tonight, try it.”
Delta hesitated.
She looked at the glass. It was sweating in the warm air. The ice knocked softly against the side.
She did not know why she picked it up. Perhaps because it was the last night, and it no longer mattered what she did.
Then drank it.
It was cold. That was the first thing. Then it was sweet, sweeter than anything she had tasted in four hundred years. In the laboratory they had fed her the same grey paste every day of her life.
And then, under the sweetness, there was something dark and sharp that stayed on the back of her tongue.
She frowned slightly.
The old man laughed loudly.
“Hahaha! How is it?”
“It’s not too sweet, right?”
“But still a little bitter.”
“That’s the correct way to drink coffee.”
He sat down in the chair with a grunt, like a man settling in for a story he had told many times.
She could feel the ring. It had reached the walls of the room. It was moving out into the corridor now, slowly, like water soaking into cloth. He was sitting in the very middle of it.
She did not know how long he had. Not long.
“Customers tell me how sweet. Zero, twenty-five, fifty, all the way up. Thirty years I watch what they order.”
He held up his hand and counted on his fingers.
“The ones who ask for a hundred, too much sugar, it becomes too sweet. You can’t taste the bitterness anymore. Then it’s not coffee. It’s milk with a lie in it.”
“I have a customer like that. A young man. Always smiling. Everything is wonderful with him, every day. He cannot stand one bitter thing in his mouth. I worry about that boy. One day something bitter will come, and he will have no practice.”
“The ones who ask for no sugar, it’s just bitter. Honest. But they never finish the glass.”
“There is a widow who comes. Black, no sugar, every morning for six years. She says life is bitter and she won’t pretend. She takes two sips and leaves the rest. Six years, and I have never once seen her finish.”
Delta held the glass in both hands. She had not meant to listen.
“Fifty-fifty doesn’t work either. People think half and half is fair. But the bitterness overwhelms the sweetness. Bitter is stronger. One bad spoon spoils a glass. One good spoon doesn’t save one.”
“You know this already. A person can be kind to you a hundred times, and you forget. Somebody is cruel to you once, and you remember it all your life. That’s how we are made. So equal is not enough.”
He raised a finger.
“So seventy-five percent sweetness is best. Three sweet, to carry one bitter.”
“You taste the sweetness…”
“But the bitterness is still there.”
“Just enough to give it flavor. You don’t hide it. You carry it.”
He nodded at the glass in her hands, pleased with himself.
She took another mouthful. She had not decided to.
Then he added,
“And don’t add too much ice.”
“It ruins the coffee. By the bottom of the glass it’s only water, and you’ve forgotten what you were drinking.”
“But without ice, it feels dull. Too heavy for this heat.”
“So a little ice is best. Enough to get you to the end.”
He smiled.
“Understand?”
She understood the words. She did not understand him. He had lost three days of work because of her. He had paid for this bed. He had come every night to talk to someone who would not answer. And now he was explaining ice to her, as if nothing in the world were more important.
In four hundred years, no human had ever given her anything without taking something first.
Delta stared at him coldly.
“Why did you help me?”
Her voice was rough. She had not used it for words in a very long time.
It was the first thing she had ever said to him. He didn’t seem surprised.
He only nodded, slowly, as if he had been waiting for exactly that question and had known it would come in its own time.
The old man sighed quietly.
“Coffee is like humans.”
“No one is completely good.”
“No one is completely evil.”
“We’re mixed. And the bitter is in the glass already, from the start. Nobody has to add it. The sweet, you have to put in yourself. One spoon at a time. Your whole life.”
He was quiet for a moment. He rubbed his knee.
Then he spoke about his past.
About his wife.
“She was a quiet woman. She liked to sing when she thought nobody could hear. I have not thought about that in a long time.”
His job. He had worked in an office once, for a man who shouted.
“Every day he shouted. At me, at everyone. I was nothing to him. I said ‘yes, sir’ for eleven years.”
The stress that slowly destroyed his family. He had brought the shouting home with him every night, in his own mouth.
“I could not shout at him. So I went home and shouted at her. About the food. About the money. About nothing. She was the only person in the world who was smaller than me.”
He did not look at Delta while he said it.
His wife had miscarried.
“She was seven months. She had been carrying water up the stairs by herself. I was at the office, being shouted at.”
And died.
“Three days later. They said it was the bleeding. I think she was only tired of me.”
The room was very still. Somewhere down the corridor a trolley went by.
“If my daughter lived,” he said quietly,
“She’d be around your age.”
Delta was four hundred and some years old. She did not correct him.
She was thinking about the ring. It had reached the end of the corridor. She could feel it on the stairs.
He looked out the window.
“I used to hate my boss.”
“For years after. I thought, he did this. He made me into that man. If I ever saw him in the street, I would kill him.”
“But later I realized… his boss was crushing him too.”
“I saw him once, at the market. Old. Small. Buying medicine. Somebody had shouted at him every day of his life as well, and he had passed it down to me, and I had passed it down to her. Nobody at the top of it. Only people, all the way up.”
“That’s when I understood.”
“Nothing in this world is completely black or white.”
“He was not a devil. I was not a victim. We were both men who chose the bitter spoon because it was the one in our hand.”
He looked back at her.
“I am not a good man. Ask my wife, if you could. But that night on the road, I had a choice of which spoon.”
“I could have ridden round you. It was raining. I was tired. Nobody would have known.”
“So we should try to keep the sweetness larger than the bitterness.”
“I left the office after she died. I bought the cart. I thought, every day I will make one small sweet thing and hand it to a stranger. It does not bring her back. It is only what I can do.”
He smiled gently.
“That’s why I helped you.”
“I want my coffee to stay seventy-five percent sweet.”
Delta looked down at the coffee in her hands.
Neither of them spoke.
Minutes passed. She could feel the ring widening. It was out in the corridor now.
Then the old man stood up.
“Alright.”
“Rest well.”
“You can leave tomorrow.”
He paused at the door. He pressed a hand to his chest for a moment, like a man who has climbed too many stairs.
“Maybe you should contact your family.”
Then he left.

### The Window

Moments later—
She heard his footsteps going away down the corridor. Slow, with the slight limp. Then they stopped.
There was a sound like a sack being dropped. A tin box rang on the floor and rolled.
A scream echoed outside.
It was one of the nurses.
Doctors running.
Panic.
Delta got out of bed. Her legs held. She went to the door and looked out.
The old man had collapsed.
He was lying on his side on the tiles, a few steps from the stairs, with his cap beside him. The nurse who had bandaged her shoulder was kneeling over him, pushing on his chest with both hands, saying, “Ta. Ta. Ta,” over and over. A young doctor came running and dropped down on the other side.
They worked on him for a long time. Then the doctor sat back on his heels and shook his head.
Dead.
Because of her curse.
Nobody looked at the girl in the doorway. Nobody in that corridor had any idea what she was.
She went back into the room. She sat down on the edge of the bed. The glass was still in her hand. She had carried it to the door and back without knowing.
Delta stared at the coffee.
Her hands trembled slightly.
She had waited four hundred years for one of them to look at her and see a person. He had sat in that chair every evening and done it, and she had not noticed, because it was the wrong century and he was the wrong kind of man.
He had told her the worst thing he had ever done. He had sat inside her curse and handed her a glass of coffee and explained to her how to be good.
And she had known. The whole time he was talking, she had known. Three words would have saved him.
One tear fell into the glass.
The first victim of her revenge…
Was the man who saved her life.
Not a man in a white coat. Not anyone who had ever touched a blade. A coffee seller who had swerved in the rain.
She had built a curse for a world that was all bitter. There wasn’t such a world. There was this one, with him in it. With however many of him.
She could feel the ring. It had not stopped. It was on the stairs now, and in the room below. The nurse was kneeling in the middle of it, still holding the old man’s hand. The young doctor was inside it. So was a woman somewhere on this floor who had given birth that afternoon.
They had a few minutes. Perhaps less.
She did not know how many of them were like him. She did not know how many were like the men in the laboratory. That was the point. The curse did not know either.
After a long silence—
She raised her hand.
And ended the curse.
A curse was not meant to be ended. Nobody had taught her how. She took hold of the thread that was being drawn out of her chest, and she pulled it back.
Even though the backlash tore through her body. Pulling it back was like drawing a hook out through her own chest. Something in her tore that would never fully mend.
She did not scream. She bit through her own lip. When it was over she was on the floor beside the bed, and most of what little magic she had left was gone.
Out in the corridor, the nurse was still alive. She was crying. She would never know how close it had been.
It did not bring him back. Magic could take a life. It had never once returned one.
She sat on the floor for a long time.
Then she reached up and found the glass.
She finished the coffee. The ice had melted. It was thin and too cold, and still sweet, and still bitter underneath.
That same night…
She quietly climbed out the hospital window.
And disappeared into the darkness.

## VII. Arc 7 — Delta (The Human World)
@pov Delta

### The Cart

She walked until the sky turned grey.
She was barefoot, in a hospital gown, with her shoulder bandaged and her lip split where she had bitten through it. She kept to the fields. She did not know where she was going. She only knew which way was away.
Then she stopped in the middle of a field and watched the sun come up. All of it. From the first thin line to the moment it was too bright to look at. Nobody came to drag her back inside. She kept waiting for it, and nobody came.
It was the thing she had wanted for four hundred years. She stood in it with her eyes streaming. She waited to feel what she had always imagined she would feel.
But what she felt was something else. There was a heavy ache in the middle of her chest, like a weight, that had not been there the day before.
Any human would have known at once what it was. It was grief. She was grieving for the old man.
She did not know that. For four hundred years she had felt rage, and almost nothing else. She had no way of recognizing this.
She thought it was the backlash. The curse had torn something in her when she pulled it back, and torn things ache. That was all.
She should have kept walking. Every step away from the hills was a step she had dreamed of for four hundred years.
She did walk, for a day. She slept in a shed that smelled of animals. She ate green fruit off a tree and was sick. And the whole time she was thinking about a tin box rolling on a tiled floor.
Instead, two days later, she was standing across the road from the pagoda in the old man’s town.
She had not decided to come back. She had simply stopped walking away, and then her feet had turned round.
There was music from a loudspeaker, slow and sour. People in white. The nurses from the hospital were there, and the woman who sold noodles beside his cart, and they were crying. Monks chanted. Smoke went up from the tall chimney behind the temple.
There was a photograph of him on a stand, with flowers round it. He was younger in it, and not smiling, and wearing a shirt with a collar. It did not look like him.
A man read his name out over the loudspeaker.
She had never known it. She had lain in a bed he paid for, and eaten his rice, and killed him, and she had never once asked what he was called.
She said it to herself, under her breath, to hear how it sounded.
Delta watched from under a tree.
There were more people than she had expected. The old man had no family. But the street was full. Market women. Motorbike drivers. Children. A row of schoolgirls who must have bought his coffee on their way home.
She had seen hundreds of her own kind die. She had heard every one. She had never once seen what was done afterward, when the ones left behind were allowed to do something.
In the laboratory the dead were taken away on a trolley, and the cell was washed, and that was the end of it.
So this was what they did. They wore white. They stood together. They cried in front of each other and were not ashamed.
The weight in her chest was worse here. She pressed her hand against it. It did not help.
When the people had gone, she walked to the market. She did not know the way. She followed the noise, and the smell of food.
She had never seen his cart. She had been unconscious on the road the night he found her, and after that she had not left her bed. But she knew it the moment she saw it. She could not have said how.
It stood by itself at the edge of the market, chained to a post, covered with a blue tarp. Everything around it was moving, and it was the one still thing. Somebody had put a small bunch of bananas and a stick of incense on the ground beside the wheel.
It was his. She was as sure of it as if he had been standing next to it.
A little boy was standing in front of it, holding a coin. He looked at the tarp for a while. Then his mother came and led him away by the hand.
She sat down next to it.
She sat there all afternoon. She did not know why. She only knew that every time she stood up to leave, the weight pulled her down again.
People passed. Some of them touched the tarp as they went by, lightly, with two fingers, the way you touch something for luck.
I did this, she thought. She had thought it before, about many things, and it had always felt like victory.
She had imagined this for four hundred years. A human dead, by her hand. Other humans weeping.
This did not feel like that. She had no word for what it felt like. In four hundred years she had never needed one.
Toward evening the noodle woman came out from her stall and looked at the barefoot girl in the hospital gown.
She was a broad woman with her sleeves rolled up and red eyes. She looked for a long moment. Delta waited to be driven off.
She did not ask anything. She went away and came back with a bowl of soup and set it on the ground, the way she had set down the bananas.
“Eat,” she said. “He would have fed you.”
Delta ate. The soup was too hot, and salty, and she finished all of it.
She did not understand why her eyes were wet. It wasn’t raining.

### Everything Is Loud

The human world was not what she remembered.
The noodle woman had given her an old shirt, a faded skirt, and a pair of rubber sandals that were too big. In those she looked like any poor girl from the countryside. Nobody stared at her now. That was the only thing that had become easier.
When they took her, there had been oxcarts and wooden houses and darkness after sunset. Now the darkness was gone. They had killed it. Lights burned all night on poles above the road, and nobody looked at them.
There were so many humans. That was what she could not get used to. In her time a town had been a few hundred people, and everyone knew everyone. Here they went past in thousands, pressed shoulder to shoulder, and not one of them looked at another.
Everything was loud.
Machines on two wheels screamed past her so close that her hair moved. Voices came out of boxes on the walls. People walked along talking to small flat stones in their hands, laughing at them, shouting at them, and the stones glowed.
Water came out of a metal pipe in a wall when a woman turned a handle. Pictures moved inside a sheet of glass. A cold wind blew out of a shop doorway on a hot afternoon, and she stood in it for a minute with her mouth open before she understood that the humans had made that too.
For the first week she flinched at everything.
Once she saw a thin, dirty girl looking at her out of a shop window, with a split lip and frightened eyes. She stepped back. The girl stepped back too.
It was a long moment before she understood. She had not seen her own face in four hundred years. She had not known that she still looked so young.
She did not understand money. She watched coloured paper go from hand to hand and could not see why anyone wanted it. On the third day she was so hungry that she took a mango from a pile.
She did not think of it as stealing. In her time, fruit on a tree had belonged to whoever was hungry.
The seller hit her across the back with a broom handle.
“Thief! Dirty little thief! Get away from my stall!”
It did not hurt. Very little hurt her, after the table. But people turned to look, and somebody laughed, and something old and black rose up in her throat. The words of the curse were still in her mouth. They would be in her mouth forever.
She looked at the woman’s red, shouting face. She thought how easy it would be. Not the whole curse. Only one word of it, for one person.
Her fingers curled.
One bad spoon spoils a glass.
She heard it in his voice. She saw him sitting in the chair with one finger raised.
She put the mango down. She walked away with her hands shaking, and she did not know whether she was shaking from rage or from how close she had come.
The worst thing was the light.
Not the sun. The other kind. The long white tubes the humans hung from their ceilings, in shops, in clinics, in the rooms where they ate. The first time she stepped under one, her legs stopped working. She stood in the doorway of a pharmacy with her back against the frame, unable to go forward or back, hearing straps and steel, until the girl at the counter asked if she was sick.
She could not answer. She could not breathe. She was lying on the table again, and the girl at the counter was wearing a white coat.
It took her an hour afterward, sitting on the kerb in the sun, before her hands were steady.
After that she slept outside. Under the eaves of the pagoda, where the only lights were candles.
She chose it because it was dark and because there was a wall at her back. She did not expect to be allowed to stay.
An old monk found her there. He looked at her for a while. Then he brought a mat and a folded orange cloth for a blanket, and said she should watch out for the dogs.
In the morning there was a bowl of rice on the step beside her. The next night the mat was already laid out.
He never asked where she had come from.
She lay awake for a long time that night, trying to work out what he wanted from her.
Every human who had ever come near her had wanted something. Her blood. Her magic. Her years. She went through everything she had, looking for the thing the monk was after, and she could not find it.

### The Bitter

She learned the paper. She learned that the paper came from work.
The noodle woman taught her that, without meaning to. She let Delta wash bowls behind the stall, and at the end of each day she put two small notes into her hand. Delta carried them to a fruit seller and held them out, and was given a mango, and nobody hit her.
So that was how it was done. You gave your hours, and they gave you paper, and the paper kept the broom handle away.
But there was not enough work at the stall for two. One morning a truck came through the town with a loudspeaker, looking for girls.
The work she found was in the city, in a building as long as a street, where four hundred women sat in rows at machines and sewed sleeves onto shirts.
Under white lights.
Row after row of the long white tubes, from one end of the roof to the other. There was not one corner of that building where they did not reach.
On the first morning she stood at the gate until the others had all gone in. Then she walked under the tubes with her nails cutting into her palms, and sat down, and sewed. She lasted the whole day. In the evening she was sick behind the building.
She went back the next morning.
She had to. She had learned what happened to a person in this world who had no paper.
The noise of the machines never stopped. The air was full of thread dust, and it got into her mouth and her eyes. Her fingers were clumsy at first, and the needle went through her thumb twice. She learned. A witch’s hands are quick, once they know what is wanted.
There was a man who walked up and down the rows and shouted. He shouted when you were slow. He shouted when you stood up. He took paper out of your pay for a crooked seam, for a minute lost, for looking at him.
She knew that voice. She had heard it for four centuries, in different mouths.
Once she saw him called into the glass office at the end of the floor. A man in a better shirt shouted at him for a long time, while he stood with his head down. When he came out, he shouted at the women twice as loudly.
Only people, all the way up, the old man had said. She had not wanted to believe him. She did not want to feel anything about the man who shouted. It was easier to hate him.
She slept in a room with seven other women, on a mat on the floor. In the second month, she came back and found that the paper she had hidden in her mat was gone. All of it. Nobody in the room would look at her.
She knew which of them it was. She could feel the guilt on the girl like heat, from across the room. It was the thin one in the corner, whose mother was in a hospital in the provinces.
She understood being cut open by enemies. She did not understand being robbed by someone who slept an arm’s length away and had shared her rice.
She said nothing. She did not know what a person was supposed to say. She lay awake that night, hungry, hating the girl, and knowing why she had done it. Both at once. She had not known it was possible to hold both.
And there was a night on the road home when a man said he had better work for her, easy work, and took hold of her wrist.
Her wrist.
His hand closed all the way round it, exactly where the straps had gone.
She did not decide anything. There was a sound, and he was on the ground three steps away, holding his arm and screaming, and she was running. She ran until she could not hear him. Then she sat down in an alley with her wrist pressed against her chest and shook until morning.
She did not know if she had broken his arm or only thrown him. She did not go back to find out. For weeks afterward she could not bear to have anyone walk behind her.
But the thing that was hardest was none of these.
It was the evenings. The other women would sit on the steps and talk about their mothers, their villages, a boy, a baby. They leaned on each other. They finished each other’s sentences.
They sent paper home. They showed each other small pictures on their phones. When one of them cried, another put an arm round her without being asked.
Delta sat at the end of the step and listened.
She had nobody to send paper to. She had no picture of anyone. If she had cried, she did not think any of them would have known what to do with her.
For four hundred years there had always been voices through the wall. Other witches, in other cells. She had never been touched, but she had never once been alone.
There had always been someone awake when she was awake. Someone who knew her name, and would say it in the dark.
Now she was in a room with seven people breathing, and the inside of her head was silent.
She did not know the human word for that. She only knew that it was cold, and that the sun did not reach it.
Bitter, she thought, every night, keeping count. Bitter. Bitter. Bitter.

### The Sweet

She kept another count as well. A count of the good things.
She had not meant to. Every night she lay on her mat and listed the bad things humans had done to her that day, the bitter ones, to remind herself why she hated them.
But the old man had told her that people are a mixture of sweet and bitter, and she could not get it out of her head. So every night, when she had finished the bad list, a second list began by itself. The kind things. The sweet ones.
The noodle woman, who fed her and said next time about the money, every time.
Delta had gone back, with her first wages, to pay for all those bowls of soup. The woman had looked at the notes and laughed in her face, and pushed them back across the counter, and given her another bowl.
The monk, who had wanted nothing.
She had worked that one out in the end. There was no trick in it. He had seen someone sleeping on stone, and he had owned a mat.
A small boy outside the factory gate who sold lottery tickets, and who discovered that she could not read the numbers on the paper. He was horrified. He sat her down on the kerb and taught her, shouting each number as if she were deaf, and refused to let her leave until she could count to a thousand.
He was eight or nine, with no front teeth and a voice like a crow. After that he waited for her every evening. He taught her the letters next, and then the names of all the football players in the world, which he considered equally important.
Rain on a tin roof, which was the best sound she had ever heard.
She would lie awake on purpose to listen to it. In the laboratory she had been too far underground to hear weather.
Mangoes, when she had paid for them.
And the woman on the next mat.
She was a few years older than Delta looked, with a loud laugh and a scar on her chin. One night, without asking, she sat down behind Delta and began to comb the knots out of her hair.
Delta went rigid.
Nobody had touched her in four hundred years except to hold her down. She did not know what to do with her hands. She did not know if she was permitted to move.
She sat with every muscle locked, waiting for it to hurt. It did not hurt. The comb went slowly. When it caught, the woman stopped, and worked the knot loose with her fingers.
“You’re like a cat nobody’s ever petted,” the woman said, and went on combing.
By the time she had finished, Delta’s shoulders had come down. She had not told them to.
It was the same woman who shared her rice on the night the money was stolen. She did not make anything of it. She only pushed half her bowl across the floor and said that thieves were everywhere, and that Delta was too thin already.
She shared her mosquito net. She showed Delta how to hide money where it would not be found. She showed her how to use the glowing stone, which was called a phone, and laughed until she cried when Delta held it to the wrong ear. She called her oun. Little sister.
At night she talked. She talked about her village, and the river behind it, and the man she was going to marry, whom she described as ugly but hardworking. She asked Delta about her family.
“Dead,” Delta said.
“All of them?”
“Yes.”
The woman was quiet. Then she reached across in the dark and patted Delta’s arm, twice, the way you pat a child.
“Well. You have me now. Go to sleep.”
Delta did not call her anything. She did not know what she was allowed to call her.
But one evening the woman did an imitation of the man who shouted, with a rice bowl on her head for his cap, and Delta made a sound that surprised both of them. It took her a moment to understand that she had laughed.
After a year the woman went home to her province to be married. She hugged Delta at the bus, hard, and said to eat more.
She was crying and laughing at once. She said she would send a message. She said Delta must come to the wedding, which they both knew would not happen. Then the door closed, and her face was at the window, and then it was not.
Delta stood in the dust after the bus had gone.
The heavy ache was back in the middle of her chest. It was the same one she had felt after the old man died. She pressed her hand to the place and frowned.
She had always believed it was an injury, something left over from ending the curse.
It could not be the backlash. That had healed as far as it was ever going to.
So it was something else. Something that came when a person you had sat beside was no longer there.
She had heard the humans use a word for it at the old man’s funeral. She tried it now, quietly.
Grief.
She turned that over for a long time. She had felt it for the old man. She had not felt it for four hundred years of her own dead, because for them she had felt only rage, and rage had left no room.
It seemed a strange way to build a creature. To make it so that the sweet things were the ones that hurt.

### Seventy-Five

With her first full month of paper, the first thing she bought for herself was a glass of coffee.
She did not need it. She needed sandals that fitted, and a second shirt. She went to the coffee stall anyway.
She had walked past that stall every day for a month, on her way to the factory. It was a cart like his, with a striped umbrella and a row of glasses. Every day she had slowed down beside it, and then gone on.
She had practiced the words.
She had said them over on her mat at night, under her breath, in the order he had said them. She was afraid of getting them wrong. She had never ordered anything in her life. For four hundred years, everything that went into her mouth had been decided by someone else.
She was more frightened walking up to that cart than she had been walking under the white lights. She did not know why. It was only a man with a spoon.
She stood at the counter with the notes folded small in her fist.
“Iced coffee with milk. Little ice. Seventy-five percent sweetness.”
It came out too quietly, and too fast. She had to say it twice.
The seller laughed at her. “Seventy-five? Who orders seventy-five?”
He was not being unkind. He held up a spoon. “Less sweet, normal, or extra sweet. That’s what I’ve got.”
“Seventy-five,” she said again.
He looked at her for a moment. Then he shrugged, and made something, and pushed it across.
She watched his hands while he did it. She had never seen the old man make a glass. She had only ever seen it arrive on a tray. She realized that she did not even know what went into it.
It was wrong. Too sweet, and thin. She knew it at the first mouthful.
She had a witch’s tongue. She could taste each thing in the glass separately, the way another person can pick out voices in a room. The beans had been burned. The milk was from a tin that had been open too long. There was too much water, and the sweetness sat on top of it all like a lid.
She stood by the stall and drank all of it anyway, to the bottom, with the same care she would have taken over the right one.
The next week she tried a different stall.
It belonged to an old woman who made the coffee so strong that it coated the glass. She clicked her tongue at the order. “Seventy-five, sixty, what is this, a bank? You’ll take it how I make it.”
That one was wrong too.
She tried every stall in that part of the city. It became the thing she did. On her one free afternoon, when the other women went to the market or slept, she walked. She learned the streets that way. She learned which sellers used fresh milk and which used the tin, which ones burned the beans, which ones watered the glass.
There was a boy who made it from powder and was ashamed of it. There was a man by the river who came close, very close, and she went back to him four weeks running before she admitted that it was not the same.
Some were too bitter and some were only sugar, and none of them were his, and she had begun to understand that none of them ever would be.
She had tasted his coffee once. One glass, on one night, with a curse spreading out from her bed. She was looking all over a city for something that had only ever existed in his hands.
But she kept ordering it. Every day. The same words, in the same order.
Some of the sellers came to know her. Here comes seventy-five, they would say, and start making it before she spoke.
“Who taught you to order like that?” the man by the river asked her once.
“An old man.”
“He sounds difficult.”
“He was,” she said. It was the first time she had spoken about him to anyone. Her voice did something strange on the second word.
She did not mind what they called her. She took the glass and found a place in the sun, and drank it slowly, and thought about a small man with a cracked lens holding up one finger.
Sometimes she wondered whether she had any right to do it. She had killed him. A person who kills a man does not usually get to mourn him.
But there was nobody else who would. He had no family. The market would forget him in a few years. If she stopped saying his words, there would be one place fewer in the world where he still was.
She had not been at his funeral, only across the road from it. She did not know where his ashes were. She had nothing of his. She had not even known his name until he was dead.
She had this. Seven words, and a glass.
It was the only grave she had for him.

### A Few Years

It took her a few years to get used to it.
The change began with a headache.
The woman at the next machine had them every afternoon, bad enough that she cried while she sewed. One evening Delta walked out past the last houses, to a ditch where the right leaves grew, and came back and made her a tea.
She did not think about it much. The woman was in pain, and Delta knew what would stop it. Her mother had shown her that plant before she could read.
It was nothing. Any witch child could have made it. But the humans had forgotten most of what grew at their own feet, and the next day there were three women waiting by her mat. A fever. A cough that would not leave. A baby who would not sleep.
