She made something for each of them. She went out to the ditches and the waste ground in the evenings, and came back with her skirt full of leaves that everyone else walked on.
They paid her in rice, then in paper.
Within a month, women were coming from the other dormitories. Within three, the man who shouted came to her himself, after dark, embarrassed, with a pain in his stomach that the clinic could not cure. She looked at him for a while. Then she made him a tea as well. He never shouted at her row again.
She had not known she owned anything the human world wanted. For four hundred years what she knew had been cut out of her by force. It was strange to have someone ask for it, and say thank you.
She learned quickly after that. She learned to read from the boy, and then from the phone, faster than he could believe. She learned what things cost and what they were worth, which were different. She learned to wear shoes, and to cross a road, and to stand under the white lights for as long as it took to buy something, if she counted her breaths.
She learned that humans lied constantly, and that most of the lies were small and meant to be kind.
You look well. It’s no trouble. I’m not hungry, you have it. At first she had thought these were tricks. It took her a long time to see that they were gifts.
Above all she learned to watch them. That was what she was best at. Most of her magic had torn away with the curse, but a little was left, enough to feel what a person was carrying when they sat down near her. Who was tired. Who was afraid. Who was smiling over something sour.
She left the factory when she no longer needed it. She rented a room of her own, with a door, and a window that faced the morning.
When she had saved enough, she went back to the old man’s town.
She had been putting it off. She was afraid the cart would be gone, sold for scrap, and that there would be nothing left of him at all.
The cart was still chained to its post. The noodle woman had kept the tarp on it through three rainy seasons, and had not let anyone take it.
“I was wondering when you’d come,” she said. She would only accept half the price.
“People wanted to buy it,” she said, counting the notes. “I told them no. I said it was already promised to someone. I didn’t know who. I only had a feeling that somebody would come back for it one day.”
She looked up at Delta.
“I think I knew it would be you. You sat beside it for a whole afternoon.”
Under the tarp everything was as he had left it. The tin of condensed milk, rusted shut. The long spoon. A row of glasses, with one missing.
It took Delta four months to make his coffee.
She roasted and ground and poured and threw away. She made hundreds of glasses in the room she rented, at night, tasting each one, with nothing to measure against but the memory of a single cup. She had a witch’s tongue. She could taste what was missing. She could not always find it.
She changed the beans. She changed the milk. She changed how long the water stood. Some nights she was sure she had imagined the whole thing, and that no coffee had ever tasted the way she remembered.
Then one evening she lifted a glass and it was his. Not too sweet. Still a little bitter.
She sat down on the floor with it. She drank it very slowly. The ache in her chest was there, and for the first time it did not feel like something to be got rid of.
She took the cart to the factory gate.
On the first morning she sold eleven glasses. On the second, thirty. By the end of the week there was a line.
The women came for the coffee and for the teas she kept in jars underneath. They came back because of something else. She never asked how sweet. She looked at each person, and made the glass they needed, and she was not wrong.
A girl who had been crying got it a little sweeter than she asked for. A man who talked too loudly got it stronger. She did not think of it as magic. It was only paying attention.
Within a year the cart was a stall with a tin awning. A year after that it was a shop.
It was a small shop. Six tables. A window that faced east, so the sun came in across the floor every morning. Yellow bulbs, never the white tubes. A chair by the door that was hers.
She kept the cart. It stood in the corner, polished, with the long spoon hanging on its hook.
The boy came after school to wash glasses and argue with her about prices. He had painted the sign himself, without asking. It said 75%, in crooked red numbers, and customers took pictures of it.
He had front teeth now. He still shouted. He told everyone who came in that he had taught the owner to read, which was true.
The woman with the scar on her chin sent a photograph of a baby to Delta’s phone. Delta looked at it for most of an hour. She did not know what you were meant to say. In the end she sent back a box of the tea for sleeping, and the woman called her, laughing so hard she could barely speak.
People said see you tomorrow when they left the shop. It took her a long time to understand that they meant it.
They noticed if she was not there. Once she closed for two days with a fever. On the second evening there was a knock at her door, and it was the noodle woman, who had heard from a customer and come in on the bus from her town with a pot of soup wrapped in a towel.
“You never eat properly,” she said, and came in without being asked, and stayed until Delta had finished the bowl.
She was not hungry anymore. She was not afraid of a hand on her wrist. She had a door that locked from the inside, and she was the one who held the key.
Some mornings she sat in her chair in the sun before the first customer, with her eyes closed and her face turned up, and did nothing at all. It was the doorstep. She had found her way back to it.
She knew it could not last. Her face did not change, and in a few more years someone would notice. That thought had a weight of its own.
One evening, after closing, she sat down in her chair and did something she had been putting off for years.
She went back through both of her lists. The list of bad things humans had done to her, and the list of good things. All of it, from the night on the road in the rain until now.
On one side: the broom handle. The money stolen from her mat. The man on the road who grabbed her wrist. The supervisor who shouted.
On the other side: the old man, who had saved her life. The soup. The monk’s mat. The woman who combed her hair. The boy who taught her to count. The sign he painted. The noodle woman on her doorstep with a pot wrapped in a towel.
She counted them honestly. She did not leave any of the bad things out, and she did not make the good things bigger than they were.
For every one bad thing, there had been about three good ones.
Three good to one bad. Seventy-five percent sweet, twenty-five percent bitter. It was exactly what the old man had told her, on his last night, with a glass of coffee in his hand: that people are mostly sweet, with some bitterness in them that never goes away.
She had built a curse on the belief that humans were all bitter. Part of her had hoped he was wrong.
He was right. She was annoyed.

### The Dead Are Fed

In the autumn of that year, the city went quiet for a festival.
The shops closed. The factories closed. The roads out of the city were jammed for two days with people going home to their villages, with bags of rice tied to the backs of their motorbikes.
She had seen it before and never looked closely. She had always kept the shop open, and been glad of the quiet.
This time the boy would not let her. “You can’t work today,” he said, scandalized. “Nobody works today. Come with us.”
His mother came to fetch them in the dark, with his grandmother riding on the back of the motorbike, holding a tray covered with a cloth. They put a second tray into Delta’s hands as if she had always been one of the family. Nobody asked whether she wanted to come.
She had brought a glass of coffee with her, out of habit. She had made it at the shop before dawn, the old man’s way, without thinking about why. She carried it in one hand and the tray in the other.
So she followed the crowds to a pagoda before dawn. Families were carrying trays of food. Rice, fruit, sweets wrapped in banana leaf.
The courtyard was packed. Candles were burning in every corner. Old women sat in rows on mats, chanting with their eyes closed. Children were asleep on their mothers’ shoulders.
The air was thick with incense smoke and the smell of rice and burning wax. At the far end, monks sat in a long row in their orange robes, chanting. Delta did not know the words, but she knew the sound. It was the sound of people speaking to someone who could not answer.
“Who is it for?” she asked a grandmother beside her.
The old woman looked at her as if she had asked what water was for.
“For our dead, child. So they aren’t hungry. So they can rest.”
She explained it slowly, as if to someone very young. For fifteen days each year, she said, the gates between the living and the dead stood open. The dead came back to look for their families. If they found food and prayers waiting, they were content. If nobody remembered them, they wandered, hungry, with nowhere to go.
“It’s our duty,” the grandmother said. “Who else will do it?”
“And the ones with no family?” Delta asked. “The ones nobody is left to remember?”
“That’s what this is for.”
The grandmother took a handful of rice from her tray and pressed it into a small ball between her palms. She put it into Delta’s hand.
“Throw it. Don’t look where it lands. Somebody will find it. Nobody should be forgotten completely.”
Delta held the little ball of rice. It was still warm.
Delta stood very still.
All around her, in the dark, people were throwing small balls of rice onto the ground for ghosts who had no one. They were feeding strangers’ dead. Just in case.
She watched a man throw a handful into the shadows by the wall. He did not know whose dead might be standing there. He fed them anyway.
Delta threw her ball of rice into the dark, the way the grandmother had shown her, without looking where it went.
Then she thought of the old man.
He had no wife now, and no daughter. He had no one at all. Nobody would be setting out a tray for him this morning. If anyone was going to feed him, it would have to be her.
She looked down at the glass in her hand. Seventy-five percent. Little ice. She had made it that morning without knowing why.
Now she knew who it was for.
And then she thought of the others.
She had three hundred and some dead of her own. She knew every name.
She had learned their names through a wall, in the dark, over centuries. She had built a curse out of them. She had never once done for them what these people were doing for strangers.
No one had ever fed them. They could not rest. The men had kept the one thing they needed to rest, and they were still there, awake, behind a wall somewhere, waiting.
That was the thing the men had taken from each body. A witch’s essence. Without it a witch could not rest when she died. She could only wait, awake, for as long as it was kept from her.
She had always been able to feel them. A faint pull under her ribs, in the hollow where her own essence had been. It pointed one way. It had pointed that way every day for years, toward the hills, and every day she had turned her back on it and gone to sit in her chair in the sun.
This morning it was stronger than she had ever felt it. It was as if the gates the grandmother spoke of were open for her dead friends too, and they were standing just on the other side, calling her.
On every other day she had been able to ignore it. She could not ignore it today.
She had told herself there was nothing she could do. That she had barely escaped. That nobody could ask her to go back.
Nobody had asked her. That was not the same thing.
Go. See it for us.
She heard it now as clearly as if the cold hand were still on hers, through the bars.
She had seen it. She had seen a great deal. The sun. The rain on a tin roof. A man swerving his bike in the rain. A comb going slowly through her hair.
She had seen it for them. And then she had kept it for herself.
She put down the cup she was holding. She set it on the ground by the wall, among the rice, for the old man.
The boy was tugging at her sleeve, saying something about breakfast. She did not hear him.
This time the weight in her chest had a name. She had learned it from the humans. It was shame.

### The Institute

The pull led her back to the hills. She had known it would.
There was a building over the place now. Glass and white stone, a flag, a guard at the gate, and a family name in steel letters above it. People in clean shirts went in and out with cards around their necks.
She saw them for the first time through the window of a shop, on a screen. A man and a woman, shaking hands with a minister. Pioneers, the screen said. Unknown phenomena. The woman was smiling.
Delta knew her hands.
She had been younger then, an assistant with a tray. She had been the one, near the end, who reached into Delta and took the thing that was not flesh.
The curse stood up in Delta’s mouth, whole, as if it had been said yesterday.
She wanted to kill that woman. The urge was so strong that she could feel the words of the curse waiting on her tongue.
She made herself walk out of the shop. She stood in the sun with her eyes closed and did what she had learned to do when the hatred came back. She went through her list of good things, one by one, the way the old man counted spoons of sugar into a glass. The soup. The comb. The boy shouting numbers. The noodle woman at her door.
She kept counting until the urge to curse had gone quiet again.
She had left the keys of the shop with the boy and told him she would be away for a while. He was fifteen by then, and did not believe her for a moment.
She could not walk in unseen, the way a witch walks past humans anywhere else. That was the one place in the world where it would not work. The men had spent four hundred years learning how witches hid, and they had built what they learned into the walls.
So she would have to walk in as one of them.
She watched the gate for a month from a tea stall across the road. She learned who went in and when. The researchers, with their cards and their cars. The cleaners, at night. The guards, who changed at six.
She learned that nobody was simply hired there. They checked everything. Schools. Families. Records going back years. She had no records. She had a coffee shop, and a face that did not change.
Then, in the second month, she saw the girl.
She was new. Delta could tell from the way she held her card against her chest, as if someone might take it away. She was about the age Delta looked. Slim, dark-haired, with glasses. A junior research assistant, straight from the university in the city, in her first week.
Delta followed her home that evening.
The girl lived alone, in a room above a pharmacy. Nobody at the institute had known her for more than a week. Her face was on a card, and a card could be changed.
It would be simple. A girl alone in a room, and a witch who still knew how to stop a heart. It would cost Delta a little of her own life. She would cut her hair the same way, and wear the glasses, and take the card, and walk in through the gate.
She told herself it was one life. One human life, against three hundred of her own dead.
She stood in the dark across the road from the pharmacy for three nights.
On the first night she watched the girl through the window, eating noodles alone at a small table, holding a book open with her elbow.
On the second night the girl’s mother called. Delta could hear only one side of it through the open window. “Yes, Ma. Yes, I’m eating. No, they’re nice to me. I’ll come home at New Year, I promise.”
On the third night Delta crossed the road. She climbed the stairs. She stood outside the door with her hand flat against the wood and the words ready in her mouth.
Inside, the girl was washing a cup and singing to herself. She sang badly. She did not know anyone could hear.
Delta thought of the old man’s wife, who had liked to sing when she thought nobody was listening.
She took her hand off the door.
She went back down the stairs and out into the street, and walked until morning. She could not do it. Not to that girl. Not to anyone. She had made a promise on the floor of a hospital room, with a dead man’s glass in her hand, and she found that she could not break it. Not even for her dead friends.
So it would have to be the slow way. If she could not take someone else’s place, she would have to earn one of her own.
It took her four years.
First, she needed a new face.
The institute would have photographs of her. She was sure of it. For four hundred years she had been one of the most valuable things in that building. They had measured her and drawn her, and later photographed her, from every side, year after year. Her face would be in their files. Since the night the lights went out, they would have been looking for it.
She could have changed it herself. A witch can reshape her own body, if she is willing to pay for it with some of her life.
But magic leaves a trace. It soaks into the skin and stays there, the way smoke stays in cloth. The institute had spent four hundred years learning to find it. If she walked through those gates wearing a face made by magic, she did not know what their machines would see. She could not take that risk.
So she would do it the human way.
She began with the name.
It came from a man in a back room who did not ask why. He did not sell made-up people. He sold real ones, who had stopped needing their papers.
The one he gave her had been a young woman from a province in the south. She had been twenty-two, a year out of school, when the river flooded her village. Her whole family had been lost with it, and so had the district office where her death should have been written down. On paper she was still alive. Nobody was left to say otherwise.
He slid her identity card across the table. There was a photograph on it. A round face, a small serious mouth, a fringe cut straight across, wire glasses.
Delta looked at it for a long time.
She learned everything about the woman that the papers could tell her. Her birthday. Her mother’s name. The name of her school, and the street it was on. She said them over at night until they came without thinking. It seemed the least she could do. If she was going to wear this woman’s life, she would at least remember her, when there was nobody else left to.
Then she went to find a face to go with it.
No proper surgeon would give a woman the face of someone else’s identity card. The man in the back room knew one who would.
He had been a surgeon at a hospital in the capital once, until something had happened that nobody talked about, and he had lost his licence. Now he worked at night, in a set of rooms above a closed-down garage on the edge of the city. He operated on people who could not go to a hospital. Men with bullets in them. Women running from husbands. People who needed to stop being who they were.
She went there after dark, as she had been told to. There was no sign on the door. A tired woman in a stained apron let her in and took the envelope of money without counting it. It was most of what the shop had saved in three years.
The surgeon was grey-haired, with very clean hands. Delta put the identity card on the table in front of him.
“I want to look like her,” she said.
He looked from the photograph to her face and back. He did not ask whose face it was, or why she wanted it. People who came to him never wanted to say, and he had learned not to ask.
“It can be done,” he said. “A rounder face. A smaller nose. The eyes will be the hardest.” He looked at her over his glasses. “You understand that if anything goes wrong in here, there is no hospital to send you to?”
“Yes,” Delta said.
She had been cut open by men who did not care whether she lived. This one, at least, wanted her to.
He drew lines on her face with a purple pen and explained what he would do. Then he asked her to lie down.
The table was steel. The lights above it were white.
She stood beside it for a long time. The woman in the stained apron asked if she was all right.
For four hundred years she had been strapped to a table like this one. Now she had walked to it on her own feet and paid for it with her own money. This time, no one was going to hold her down. She would have to lie down on it by choice.
She lay down. She would not let them strap her arms. She held on to the edges of the table instead, and counted the good things in her head, one by one, until the anaesthetic took her.
When she woke, her face was wrapped in bandages, and it ached as if it had been struck with a board.
It healed in eight days. It should have taken two months. At the end of the first week the surgeon unwrapped the bandages, and then stood there staring.
“This isn’t possible,” he said.
“My family heals quickly,” Delta said.
He did not write anything down. Men like him never did. But he looked at her for a long time, the way a man looks at something he has decided not to remember. She left that night and never went back.
When the last of the swelling had gone, she stood in front of the mirror in her room and held the identity card up beside her face.
The two faces were the same. A round face, a small serious mouth. The woman from the flooded village looked back at her out of the glass, alive.
She cut a fringe straight across her forehead, the way the woman had worn hers. She bought wire glasses like the ones in the photograph, with plain glass in them. Nobody who had ever seen her old face would look twice at this one.
Then she enrolled at the university in the city nearest the hills, to study biology.
She did not go back to the shop. She had left it to the boy, and she did not let herself think about it. There was only the plan now.
She hated the white lab coat. The first time she put it on, she was sick in the toilets. It was what the men had worn. She wore it anyway.
She learned faster than any student the university had ever seen.
A witch’s mind is built to last for centuries, and to keep everything it is given. She read a whole textbook in a night and remembered every page. She learned in a month the anatomy that the others took a year to learn. Before the end of her first term she could name every bone and every muscle in the body, and what each drug did to each of them. Her teachers were sure she must have studied somewhere before, and was too shy to say so.
She was not only quick. She was good. She had spent four hundred years on the other side of the experiment. She knew what their instruments were measuring, because they had measured it on her. She knew plants that the textbooks were only beginning to describe.
In her third year, an old professor set the class a problem he had been stuck on for ten years, half as a joke. She brought him the answer the following Monday. In her fourth year she finished at the top of her class.
That spring the institute asked the university to recommend its best graduate for a junior research post. The professor wrote her name first.
The interview was the most frightening twenty minutes of her life.
It took place in a white room on the first floor. A woman in a white coat sat behind a desk with Delta’s papers in front of her. She read every page. Then she looked up at Delta’s face, and kept looking, for much longer than she needed to.
Delta sat very still with her hands in her lap. She thought: she knows. She has seen my photograph a hundred times. Any moment now she will press a button under that desk.
The woman asked one question about her family. Delta answered it.
The woman nodded, looked down, and wrote something on the form.
“Welcome to the institute,” she said.
She had studied Delta’s face as closely as anyone ever had, and seen a stranger.
There was one more thing she had been afraid of. The institute had machines at the gate, built to find witches. They searched for what a witch carries inside her, her essence. But hers had been taken from her long ago, in that same building. There was nothing left in her for the machines to find. And her new face had been made with a knife, not with magic, so there was no trace of magic on her either. What they had stolen from her was what let her walk back in.
So she walked back in. Through the front gate, under the white lights, in a white coat, with a card around her neck that said she was a junior researcher. For four hundred years she had been the most watched creature in that place, and now she was no one.
Getting down took longer.
At first her card opened only the junior laboratories, the library and the offices on the upper floors. The lower levels were behind a steel door at the end of the ground floor. When she held her card to the reader beside it, the light glowed red.
So she did what she had done at the university. She was better than everyone else, and she was patient.
She did not try to make friends. She did her work, and she read.
At night, when the laboratories were empty, she read everything the institute had published, and every report she was allowed to see. She learned their science the way she had learned anatomy, in weeks instead of years. Chemistry. Genetics. The strange papers on “unknown phenomena” that had made the family famous.
Then she began to find their mistakes.
A drug trial in which the dose would have made half the volunteers ill. A set of results that could not be right, because the samples had been stored too warm for a week. A conclusion that contradicted the institute’s own data three pages earlier. She wrote each one down in a short, plain note and left it on the right desk.
She did not sign the first few. When they began asking who was writing them, she did.
After eight months, one of the senior researchers called her into his office. Her notes were spread across his desk.
“You were right about the samples,” he said. “And the dosage. And the rest of it. You saved us most of a year.” He looked at her for a moment as if he could not work her out. “You’ve been here eight months. Where did you learn to think like this?”
“I like to read,” she said.
He was moving her to his team on the lower levels, where the most important research was done and very few staff were allowed. She would be the youngest member of his team.
“I’ve never had a junior who reads our papers better than my senior staff,” he said. He held out a new card. “Don’t make me regret it.”
She took it. Her hand did not shake. She had had four hundred years of practice at keeping her face still.
That night, at the end of her rounds, she held the new card up to the steel door. The light turned green.
She went down.
The lower levels were still there. The corridor. The iron doors.
The cells were empty.
They had been washed. There was a drain in the middle of each floor and a smell of bleach. She stood in front of the first door, the one she had pulled at until her nails broke, and there was no voice on the other side.
Every witch she had left behind in those cells was dead. The last of them had died while Delta was out in the world. While she was learning to read, and selling coffee, and sitting in the sun. While she was feeling sorry for herself because she was lonely.
She had not come back in time to save a single one of them.
She put her forehead against the cold iron door and stayed there for a long time.
But they were not entirely gone. She could still feel them. It was the same faint pull she had felt under her ribs for years, in the hollow where her own essence had been taken. It was the pull of their essences, the part of each witch that the men had taken and kept. Their bodies were dead, but their essences were still somewhere in this building, awake, unable to rest.
The pull was not coming from the cells. It was coming from somewhere above her.
So she went back up the stairs, and followed it.
Night after night, after the others had gone home, she stayed late in the research offices, and she read. Nobody questions a junior researcher who works late. The humans wrote everything down. They always had.
One night, in a cabinet of old records, she found herself.
It was a thick folder with a number on it and no name. Inside were photographs, dozens of them, going back to the very first cameras. A girl in a white dress, from the front and from both sides. The same face in every picture, across more than a hundred years, never a day older. On the top of the pile was a newer sheet, stamped in red. Escaped. Do not approach. Report on sight.
She looked at that face for a long time. It was her own. She had not seen it since the day of the surgery.
Then she put the folder back exactly where she had found it, and walked out past a guard who said good night to her by her new name.
She learned that the men had never been able to keep what they took. In glass it faded. In metal it went wild. For centuries most of it had simply been lost, and those witches were lost with it.
Then the man and the woman had found the answer. Essence would only stay in something alive. Something built for it. Something strong enough to hold hundreds at once and not break.
So they had made one.
The files called it the vessel. They recorded its weight, its temperature, its blood. They noted with satisfaction that it showed no awareness of its contents.
She saw the woman once, late, alone in her office. She was on the phone. Her voice was different from the voice in the corridors.
“Has he eaten? …No, don’t wake him. Tell him I’ll call on Sunday.”
Then she hung up and wrote a number in a book.
Delta did not know what to do with that. She stood behind the door with her clipboard and could not make the two voices fit inside one woman.
No one is completely good, the old man said, in her head. No one is completely evil.
She had not expected it to apply here.

### The Vessel

For weeks she tried to find where the vessel was kept.
The files never said. They gave its weight and its temperature and the results of its blood tests, year after year, but never where it was. On her night shifts she searched the lower levels room by room. She expected a cage, or a tank. Some kind of animal, she thought, grown in a laboratory, with no mind of its own.
The pull was no help. Inside the building it was everywhere at once, faint and spread out, like a smell in a closed room. It did not lead to any vault, or to any door she could open.
Then, one afternoon, it changed.
She was at her bench in the laboratory when it came over her all at once, so strongly that she had to put down the glass in her hand. It was not spread out anymore. It was pointing. It was close, and it was moving, coming along the corridor toward her.
She went to the door.
A boy was walking down the corridor between two nurses. Sixteen, perhaps seventeen. He was in a hospital gown, with a plaster in the crook of his arm where they had taken his blood. He was complaining to one of the nurses about the food in the canteen, and she was laughing at him.
The pull went with him, every step. When he passed the door, it was so strong that Delta had to hold on to the frame.
They were all in there. She could feel them inside him. Every voice she had known through the wall. The one who had told her to go. And, very faintly, like hearing herself speak in another room, her own.
The vessel was not an animal. It was a boy.
That night she went up to the director’s office. On the desk, in a silver frame, there was a photograph of the same boy. He was laughing at whoever held the camera, with his chin up, as if nothing had ever gone wrong for him and nothing ever would. The director and his wife stood on either side of him, with their hands on his shoulders.
On the back, in the wife’s handwriting, was a name and a date. Zen, sixteen.
He was their son.
They had made a child to carry the dead. And then they had raised him, and sent him to school, and put his picture on the desk where they did their work.
Delta did not understand how anyone could do both of those things. She stood in the dark office for a long time, trying to.
She found his house in the city. On her next day off, she stood across the road and waited.
He came out in the morning with a bag over his shoulder. He was arguing cheerfully with someone on his phone. He stopped to let a dog cross in front of him. He was taller than in the photograph.
He was not a vault. He was not a wall. He walked and laughed and was kind to a dog.
She had come to take back what was hers. She had come for her dead friends.
And she understood, watching him, that she did not know how to do it.
The files had been plain about that. The essences were bound into him so tightly that nobody knew how to take them out. Not even his parents. If they were torn out by force, they would be destroyed. And so would he.
He turned his face up for a moment as he walked, into the sun, with his eyes half closed. The way she did.
Something moved in the hollow under her ribs. It was not the pull. It was not shame, or the weight she had learned to call grief.
She had no word for this one either.
She stood in the sunlight and watched him until he turned the corner.

## VIII. Arc 8 — Delta (The Original Loop)
@pov Delta

### Unseen

She still did not know how to get them out of him. Nobody did. The files had been clear about that.
So she did the only thing she was good at. She watched.
First she left the institute. She had read everything there was to read, and she could not bear to put on the white coat again if she did not have to. The senior researcher was sorry to see her go. He told her that if she ever wanted to come back, there would always be a place for her on his team. She thanked him. She did not think she would ever need it.
She rented a small room two streets from Zen’s house. She did not need to work. The shop was still running, and every month the boy sent her share of the money to the bank account. The share grew bigger every month.
The boy had turned out to be good at business, much better than she had ever been. A few lines always came with the money, telling her what he had done. He had taken over the empty shop next door and knocked through the wall. He had hired two girls from the factory to help at the counter. He had opened a second shop by the market, with the same crooked red sign, and then a third near the university. Every one of them made the coffee her way, seventy-five percent, little ice. He had taught the staff himself.
She read his messages more times than she needed to. She had left him a small shop with six tables. He was turning it into something she would hardly recognize, and she found that she did not mind at all.
For two years she watched the vessel, from across roads, from the back of buses.
She learned his life the way she had once learned the city. He lived in a large house with high walls and a housekeeper who went home at six. His parents were almost never there. Most nights the only light in the whole house was in his bedroom window, with the blue flicker of a television.
At school he was popular. He was clever and quick, and he never seemed to have to try. Girls waited for him at the gate. Boys copied his homework. He was kind to all of them, easily, the way people are kind when it costs them nothing.
She watched him grow taller.
She watched the dark car come for him four times a year. Each time it brought him back a day later with a plaster in the crook of his arm. Each time he went straight up to his room and slept until the next afternoon. Nobody in the house seemed to think that was strange. Neither did he.
Sometimes she thought about crossing the road and telling him. But she did not know what she would say. Your parents made you to hold the essences of hundreds of dead witches. And one of those essences is mine. He would think she was mad. And even if he believed her, she did not know what he could do about it.
So she only watched. She told herself she was guarding her dead friends. They were inside him, and wherever he went, she would go too.
Then he went away to university, in another town, and she followed.
A witch can go unseen.
It was the oldest thing they knew, older than any spell. It was how they had lived beside humans for so long. It was hardly magic at all. It was more a way of standing, a way of letting a person’s eyes slide past you to the next thing.
In the first months after the curse she had been too torn even for that. It had come back slowly, like feeling into a numb hand. And then she had not used it. In her shop, she had wanted to be seen.
She let the world’s eyes slide off her, and walked onto the campus at Zen’s side.
She went where he went. She sat on the steps of his lecture halls. She stood at the back of the room when he argued. He argued well, and nobody could beat him, and it bored him.
Most of all she sat in the cafeteria, at the empty table next to his, in the sun from the window.
On the first day he came in with a crowd around him. He was laughing. When he passed her chair, the hollow under her ribs turned over, the way it always did when he was near. It was like hearing her own voice from another room.
“Iced coffee,” he told the man at the counter. “Full sugar. Whatever’s fastest.”
A hundred percent. Of course.
He took it and turned around. Then he stopped. He looked straight at the chair where she was sitting, at nothing, with a small crease between his eyebrows, like someone who has walked into a warm room and can’t see where the heat is coming from.
Then somebody called his name, and he forgot.
He never finished the glass. Not once in two years.

### The Table

She had come to watch a vessel.
Within a month, without meaning to, she was watching a table.
There were four of them. They sat in the same place every day, by the window.
She pieced together how they had found each other, a little at a time, from what they said. Zen and Dan had been friends since they were small boys. Jess and Asa had met on their first day at the university, when Asa sat down in the empty seat beside her. Then Jess had fallen for Zen, and he had asked her out, and the two pairs had become one table.
The small one came to the counter first. On her first morning she reached the front of the line, opened her mouth, forgot every word she had ever known, and pointed at the lemonade.
The man at the till made it. The girl came back the next day and ordered lemonade again, firmly, as if it had been a choice. She ordered it every day after that. Her name was Jess.
Jess stirred her drink when she was nervous. She was nervous most of the time. She looked at Zen the way Delta had once looked at the end of a tunnel.
She was also jealous. Delta could feel it under the gentleness, small and hard.
When another girl stopped to talk to Zen, Jess’s straw went still in her glass. When Asa stayed too long at someone else’s table, Jess said nothing at all until she came back.
But it never showed on her face. Zen was hers. Asa was hers. As long as that was true, Jess felt in control, and she could afford to be sweet.
The sweetness was not a mask. That was what made the rest so hard to see. Jess truly loved Zen. She truly loved Asa as her friend. The jealousy lived in the same girl, underneath, and she did not look at it.
There was one more thing in Jess that Delta could feel, though it took her a long time to understand it. Jess knew about the heavy, hidden thing in Asa. Not its name, perhaps, but its shape. When Asa looked at her a moment too long, Jess went still and careful, and then she smiled and said something ordinary, and the moment passed. She never asked about it. She never moved her chair away either.
Jess never walked into the cafeteria alone. If she arrived first, she waited outside by the noticeboard, pretending to read it, until she saw one of the others through the glass. Only then did she come in.
But she remembered everyone’s birthday. She kept a list in the back of her notebook. On each one she left something small and carefully chosen on the table. A pen in Dan’s favourite colour. A keyring shaped like a cat for Asa. A packet of the sweets Zen had mentioned liking once, months before, and forgotten that he had said.
The bright one was Asa. She ordered something different every day, loudly, and made the people behind her laugh. And every day, at the end, in a much quieter voice, she said, “And a lemonade.”
She carried it to the table herself. She always put it down on Jess’s side before Jess arrived.
Delta could feel what people carried. That much of her magic was left. Most people carried small things, loosely. Asa carried one thing, and she carried it very carefully, every hour of the day. It was large and heavy and hidden, and all of it was turned toward Jess. Delta could feel the shape of it, but not what it was. She did not understand human feelings well enough to give it a name. She only knew that everything about the way Asa sat and talked and laughed was arranged so that nobody would ever see it.
When Jess looked at Zen, Asa looked at the window. Then she made a joke. She always had the joke ready.
The cheerfulness was not an act. Asa was truly bright, and truly confident, and she truly liked people. She teased Zen and argued with him and laughed at his jokes because they were funny. She was the only one who could make Dan talk. Those friendships were real.
But there was something in her toward Zen that was not friendly at all. Delta could not tell what it was. It only ever came when he was careless with Jess.
Now and then, when he let go of Jess’s hand to pick up his phone, Delta felt something hot and quick go through Asa. It was resentment. It was gone in a breath. Afterward Asa would say something especially kind to him, as if to make up for a thing he had never noticed.
Once, Delta saw her take a call in the stairwell. It was her mother. Asa stood with her back to the wall and said, “Yes, Ma. Sorry, Ma. I’ll try harder,” in a small flat voice that Delta had never heard her use. Her face went completely empty. Then she hung up, and stood for a moment with her eyes closed. When she walked back into the cafeteria she was laughing at something before she even reached the table.
The quiet one was Dan. He thanked the man at the counter, and looked at him while he said it. He looked at everyone that way. Carefully. As if he were learning them by heart in case they were taken away.
Once or twice his eyes stopped on the empty chair where Delta was sitting. They stayed there a moment before they moved on.
He sat at the end of the table. He noticed when a glass was empty. Every few minutes his eyes went round the four of them, one, two, three, four, and only then did his shoulders come down.
One day Zen was late. Ten minutes, then fifteen. The others hardly noticed. Dan noticed. He stopped eating. He looked at the door, and at his phone, and at the door again, and his knee began to jump under the table.
When Zen finally came in, with his hair wet from the rain and a story about a broken-down bus, Dan said nothing at all. He only let out a long breath, and picked up his spoon again.
Delta did not understand it then. Nothing had happened. Nobody had been hurt. She thought he was simply a nervous boy.
She would understand it later.
And Zen sat in the middle, tipped back in his chair, spinning a pen.
He was a good friend. Delta had not expected that, from something the men had made. He let Asa make fun of him. He was the one Dan talked to, when Dan talked to anyone. When one of them was in trouble he was there before he was asked, and he never mentioned it afterward.
He was gentle with Jess. He walked her back to her dorm. He minded when she was sad.
But Delta could feel what people carried, and what Zen carried for Jess was not what Jess carried for him. She could feel that the two were different. She did not know why. She did not understand human love well enough to tell one kind from another.
But she had watched humans for years, through the window of her shop, and she had learned to match what she felt to what she had seen.
There had been a boy who came into the shop every afternoon with his little sister. He always bought her a drink before he bought his own. He held her hand when they crossed the road, even when she was too old to need it. When she was upset he went quiet and stayed close to her until she was better. Delta had stood behind the counter and felt what he carried for that girl. It was warm and steady and protective.
What Zen carried for Jess felt exactly the same.
Delta did not have a word for it. But she knew where she had felt it before. It was the way that boy had loved his sister.
He did not know that. He had never been in love, so he had nothing to compare it with. He believed this was what love was, and he was doing his honest best at it.
He was not unhappy. He simply seemed to be waiting for something, without knowing what.
Delta had watched humans for years. She had never watched the same four for so long.
She began to see how it was built.
Dan was looking at all of them. Asa was looking at Jess. Jess was looking at Zen. Zen was looking at nothing.
Four people at one table, and no two of them facing each other.
And yet they came. Every day. They saved each other seats. They argued about where to eat on Fridays. When Jess had a fever, the other three took turns leaving soup outside her door. When Dan’s grandmother died, nobody said anything about it at all, but for a week Asa talked twice as much, so that he would not have to.
It was a fragile thing. Delta could see the cracks in it from across the room. Each of them was holding one end and pretending not to feel it pulling.
She did not understand why they kept holding.
In the laboratory, the witches had been bound together by the wall and the dark. They had no choice in it. These four had a choice every morning, and every morning they chose the same table.
Without meaning to, she had begun to care about them. Every morning, when the cafeteria doors opened, she found herself looking for all four of them, not only Zen. She noticed when Jess looked tired, or when Dan was quieter than usual. She told herself that she only watched because Zen was carrying her dead friends, and that the others did not matter to her. It was not true. She did not know that yet.

### The Sea

In the spring of the second year, they went to the sea.
It was Asa’s idea. She announced it at lunch, and planned all of it herself. The bus, the cottage, the food. All that week Delta could feel something building in her, stronger every day. Asa laughed more than usual, and talked more, and twice Delta saw her stop in the middle of a sentence and look at Jess, and then look away.
She was working up her courage. Delta did not yet know for what.
She followed them, and sat in the shade of the trees at the top of the beach. Nobody could see her. She had made sure of that.
The cottage was old, with a sagging veranda and a roof held up on thin posts eaten by the salt. Dan had not slept on the bus. He went inside to lie down for ten minutes.
Zen sat on the sand with his phone.
Jess and Asa went into the water.
Jess could not swim. Asa held both her hands and walked backward, laughing, pulling her along in the shallows. Jess was shrieking every time a wave came, and laughing too. Delta could hear them from the trees. It was a good sound. She had come to know it well.
Then the laughing stopped.
Asa was standing very still, waist-deep, still holding Jess’s hands.
The wind was blowing in from the sea toward the trees, and a witch’s ears are sharp. Delta heard every word.
“Jess. I need to tell you something. And you have to let me finish, okay? Don’t say anything until I’m done.”
“Okay,” Jess said. She was still half laughing. “What?”
Asa took a breath.
“I’m in love with you.”
Jess stopped laughing.
“Not as a friend. I mean it. I have been since the first day. I asked you for a pen, and I had three in my bag. I just wanted a reason to sit next to you.” Her voice was shaking, but she kept going. “I know you love Zen. I know. I’m not asking you for anything. I just couldn’t keep carrying it by myself anymore. I didn’t want to lie to you every day for the rest of my life.”
She had kept it hidden for two years. And on this bright afternoon, in the sea, with nobody near, she had finally said it out loud.
So that was what it had been. The heavy, hidden thing Delta had felt in Asa for two years, all turned toward Jess. It was love. Now Delta knew its name. And she understood, too, why Asa had never been able to forgive Zen for letting go of Jess’s hand.
Delta felt the relief go through her, and the terror right behind it.
She saw Jess’s face.
It wasn’t disgust. It wasn’t anger. It was only a person who had been handed something far too heavy without any warning, and did not know where to put it down.
It was not surprise. Delta could feel that much. Jess had known. Perhaps not in words, but she had known for a long time. What was in her face was fear. As long as Asa never said it, Jess could keep her, as a friend, beside her every day, and nothing had to change. Now it had been said out loud, in the bright water, and it could not be taken back.
Jess pulled her hands away. She took a step back, away from the words. Then another. She was shaking her head. “No. Asa, no. You can’t— why would you say that? Why would you say that to me?” She was not looking where she was going.
Asa reached for her. “Jess, wait—”
The seabed dropped.
The wave came in sideways and took her.
One moment Jess was there, and the next there was only water where she had been, and then her head came up further out, and her arms, and she was screaming.
Asa went after her at once.
She didn’t call for help. She didn’t stop to think. She swam out into the deep water, caught Jess around the waist, and shoved her, hard, back toward the shallows with everything she had.
The same water that gave Jess back closed over Asa.
Zen was running. He went into the sea up to his chest, and caught Jess by the arm, and dragged her out onto the sand. She was not breathing. He knelt over her, pressing on her chest, breathing into her mouth, shouting her name.
Delta was on her feet. She did not remember standing. Her fingers had curled. She could have run down to the water. She could have let them see her. She could have gone in.
But she did not. She told herself it was not her place. She had not followed them here to protect them. She had come only to keep watch over Zen, because her dead friends were inside him. Asa and Jess were just two humans who happened to sit at his table. Why should she save them? Humans had cut her open for four hundred years, and not one of them had ever tried to save her.
She told herself that. She said it twice, in her head, very clearly.
She stood under the trees and did not move.
Out in the water, there was no sign of Asa at all.
On the sand, Jess coughed up seawater and began to breathe. Zen was on his feet, shouting Asa’s name at the sea. The cottage door opened and Dan came out, with his hair flat on one side from the pillow, into the screaming.
The sea was flat and bright. There was no sign of her.
Delta pressed her hand under her breastbone.
The weight was there. She did not understand it. She had never sat beside that girl. She had only watched her carry a lemonade across a room, seven hundred and thirty two times.

### After

They did not find Asa.
The coast guard searched for three days. Boats went up and down the bay, and a helicopter flew low over the water, and men walked the rocks at low tide with long poles. Jess sat on the cottage steps the whole time, wrapped in a blanket, and would not go inside. Zen stood at the edge of the water. Dan walked the beach from one end to the other, again and again, as if he could find her by counting the sand.
On the third evening, the search was called off.
Asa’s mother came to the university a week later to collect her things. She was a small, upright woman in a dark suit. She did not cry. She stood in Asa’s room and looked at the photographs of the four friends stuck around the mirror. “She never told me she had so many friends,” she said. Then she packed everything into boxes, and left.
Delta watched her go. Asa had never told anyone about her mother either.
The table by the window had three people at it, and then two, and then it was empty.
Dan had counted to four every day for years. He had slept through the one moment it mattered. He could not live with that, so he put it on Zen.
It started at lunch, a few days after the memorial.
“You were right there,” Dan said. His voice was quiet, which was worse than shouting. “You were on the sand. You were ten steps away.”
“I went for Jess—”
“You were looking at your phone.”
Zen had no answer to that.
Delta heard it from the empty table beside theirs. Everyone in the room heard it. After that day, Dan and Zen did not sit together again.
Jess never told anyone what Asa had said in the water. Delta was sure of that. When the coast guard asked what had happened, Jess said they had been playing in the shallows and a wave had come. When Zen asked, she said the same thing. She said it in a flat, careful voice, looking at the floor.
Delta understood why. Jess believed it was her fault. She had stepped back from Asa’s words without looking, and Asa had died pulling her out. If Jess told anyone what Asa had said, she would have to tell them the rest. So she said nothing, and carried it alone, the way Asa had carried her secret.
And there was a second thing Jess could never tell anyone. She had not been surprised in the water. She had known what Asa felt, and she had kept her close anyway, because Asa was her friend and she could not bear to lose her. If she told them what Asa had said, she would have to tell them that too.
Jess held on to Zen’s sleeve and did not say Asa’s name, and Zen, who could not bear to see her hurt, let her not say it. He helped her forget. He thought that was what a good boyfriend did.
But he couldn’t look at her. Every time he did, he saw the sea. And he heard Dan’s voice. You were ten steps away.
Jess came to the counter alone now.
“Lemonade,” she said, and then stood there a moment too long, as if waiting for someone to add something.
Nobody did. For two years, someone always had.
Delta was standing where Asa used to stand. She found that her own lips had moved.
And a lemonade.
A few months later, Zen told Jess he was going home for a while.
He told her on the steps of her dormitory, kindly and quickly, the way he told her everything. He said he needed some time. He said it wasn’t her. He said he would call. Jess nodded and nodded, and did not cry until he had turned the corner.
He did not tell Dan at all.
The dark car came for him that evening.
Delta watched it pull away from the gate with Zen in the back seat, looking out at nothing. The pull under her ribs went with it. Every one of her dead friends, going out along the road toward the hills.
Delta went after it.

### The Room Below

She knew something was wrong before she reached the hills.
The pull under her ribs had changed. For years it had been steady, a faint, even ache that pointed always toward Zen. Now, once a day, it gave a sudden sharp jerk, as if something were being torn loose. Then it was weaker than before.
She did not understand it yet. She only knew that it had never done that in four hundred years.
She could not go unseen in that place. It was the one building in the world that had been made to stop her. So she went in the way she had before, as a researcher.
She had left the institute two years earlier, once she had read everything she needed. They had been sorry to lose her. When she came back and asked for her old place on the team, they gave it to her the same day.
“You’ve come back at the right time,” the senior researcher told her. He was happier than she had ever seen him. “We’ve had a breakthrough. The biggest in the history of this place. Come and see.”
He took her down to the lowest level himself.
The room was new. It was where the cells had been. They had knocked down the old walls and tiled over the stone, and filled the space with machines. The lights were white. In the middle of the room there was a steel table.
Zen was on it.
He was strapped down at the wrists and ankles, with a broad strap across his forehead, so that he could not turn his head. The straps did not have buckles. They had a small red light that held them shut.
Delta stood in the doorway in her white coat and could not breathe. For one moment she did not know which of them was lying there. Him, or her.
Zen’s mother was standing beside the table in a white coat. His father was reading numbers off a screen.
Zen’s eyes were open. He had been told he was coming home for a rest. He had fallen asleep in the car. He had woken up here.
“Mum?” His voice was thick. “Mum, what is this? What are you doing? Mum—”
“Don’t answer it,” his father said to his mother, without turning round. “It doesn’t know what it is. It never has. Begin.”
He meant that Zen had never known the truth about himself. That he was not really their son. That he had been made, on purpose, to hold the dead. And his father did not think he was worth explaining it to.
Delta had been called that for four hundred years.
His mother put her hand on Zen’s hair. She smoothed it back from his forehead, once, the way you would for a child with a fever.
Then she switched on the machine.
Delta made herself stay and watch. She made herself read the notes on the screen. Then, that night, alone in the office, she made herself read everything else.
The breakthrough had come while she was away. For years they had known the essences were inside Zen, but they had not been able to get them out without destroying them. Now they had found a way.
An essence would only come loose from him at the very edge of death. So every day they brought him to that edge. They used drugs and the machine to slow his heart until it was barely moving. At that moment, the one essence that sat nearest the surface came free, and the machine caught it before it could escape. Then they brought his heart back, and let him rest until the next day.
Each essence went into a small steel case. Each case had a name written on it. Not a witch’s name. A buyer’s. A general. A minister. An old man with a great deal of money who did not want to die.
That was what the essences were for. The buyers would take them into their own bodies, and live as long as witches lived. Her dead friends were not going to be set free. They were going to be sold, and used up, by the same kind of men who had caged them.
And now she understood what the pull was doing. The sharp jerk under her ribs, once a day, was the machine. It was one of her dead friends being pulled out of Zen and shut in a box.
Each day, after the machine had finished, Zen came back a little less far. A little weaker. A little greyer. There were three hundred and some essences inside him. Nobody in the room expected him to last until the last one.
Once, Delta could have torn that room apart. Not anymore. Most of her magic had been torn away when she ended her curse. All she had left was a little warmth in one palm. And if she used even that, in this building, the alarms would find her within a minute. Then she would be on the next table.
Nobody stayed with him at night. Nobody thought they needed to. The machines watched his heart, and an alarm would sound upstairs if anything went wrong. At the end of each day the others wrote up their notes, switched off the main lights, and went home. They left him alone down there, strapped to the table, until morning.
Delta went home with them that first evening. She sat in her room and could not stay there.
She still had her card. A little before midnight, she went back.

### The Chair

The first night, she only meant to look.
The corridor was dark. The room was empty except for him. One white lamp had been left on above the table, so that anyone who came in could read the machines. They were quiet now, only blinking. There was a chair beside the table, where his mother sat in the daytime to write in her book.
Delta stood in the doorway for a long time.
Then she sat down in the chair.
He was awake. He could not turn his head. The strap across his forehead saw to that. He was looking straight up into the white lamp, and his eyes were wet, and he was nineteen years old.
He looked very different from the boy in the cafeteria. His face was grey, and wet with sweat, and his lips were cracked. There were tape marks on his arms where the needles went in, and a bruise on the inside of his elbow. He had been crying earlier. The tears had dried in lines down the sides of his face, into his hair, because he could not lift a hand to wipe them.
He was shivering. The room was kept cold for the machines, and he had nothing on but a thin paper gown. Nobody had thought to give him a blanket. Nobody had thought of him as something that could be cold.
There was a cup of water on the trolley at the end of the table, with a straw in it. It was well out of his reach. Even if it had been closer, he could not have turned his head to drink.
His voice, when it came, was hoarse. He had been screaming earlier, for most of the afternoon. Now he was too tired to scream. He was humming instead, under his breath, very badly and out of tune, the way a child hums in the dark to keep from being afraid.
She knew exactly what he was seeing, because she had seen it herself for four hundred years. The white light, so close that it filled everything. The tiles of the ceiling around it, every one of them the same. And when you closed your eyes, the light was still there, a red glow through your eyelids, so that there was no real dark to escape into. Nowhere to go at all.
In all those years on that table, nobody had ever sat down beside her. Not once.
Only once in her whole life had anyone pulled up a chair beside her bed. A small man with a cracked lens in his glasses, in a hospital that smelled of medicine. And she had killed him.
She had expected to feel nothing tonight. This boy was the son of the woman who had cut her essence out of her. He was the box her dead friends were kept in. She had come down here telling herself that.
It did not feel like nothing.
“Mum?” he whispered.
Delta did not answer.
“No,” he said, after a moment. “You’re not her. She doesn’t come at night.”
Then, so quietly that she almost did not hear it:
“I’m sorry, Mum. Whatever I did. I’m sorry. I’ll be better, I promise. Just tell me what I did.”
Delta went very still.
He thought it was his fault. They had tied him to a table and were draining him a little more each day, and he was lying there in the cold trying to work out what he had done to deserve it. He had been raised to believe he was loved. He could not believe they would do this to him unless he had earned it somehow.
In four hundred years, Delta had never once thought it was her fault. She had hated them too much for that. It had kept her alive.
This boy did not know how to hate anyone. He only knew how to blame himself.
She got up. She found a blanket in a cupboard by the door, and came back, and laid it over him, carefully, without touching him. She tucked it in around his shoulders. Before dawn, she would have to take it away again, so that nobody would know.
He stopped shivering.
“Is someone there?”
She didn’t answer. She did not know what she would have said.
He listened for a while. He could not see her. But he could hear that someone was breathing near him, softly, and he could smell something faint and warm, like coffee and soap. He thought it was a girl. He did not know why he thought that.
Then his breathing changed. It slowed. His hands opened a little in the straps.
She stayed until the corridor lights came on at dawn. Then she went upstairs, and washed her face, and put on her white coat, and came back down with the others to watch the machine take one more of her dead friends out of him.
She came back the next night. And the next.
She told herself she was guarding her dead friends. That was true. They were in him.
It was not why she sat down.
On the second night he heard her come in.
“Are you one of them?” he asked the ceiling. His voice was rough. “Are you here to write things down?”
She said nothing.
He was quiet for a long time. Then he said, “No. I don’t think you are. They don’t sit down.”
On the third night, he started to talk.
He talked to the ceiling, because that was all he could see. He did not ask her to answer. He talked about his mother, and how she used to measure him on the kitchen door when he was small. He talked about Dan, who had never once in his life needed him to be impressive. He talked about Jess, and said he did not know how to look at her anymore.
Sometimes he asked questions. Not to her. To nobody.
“Why are they doing this? What do they keep taking out of me?” And once, so quietly she almost did not hear it: “What am I?”
Delta knew the answer to every one of those questions. She sat with them in her mouth, and did not say them. There was nothing he could do with the truth, strapped to that table. It would only be one more thing to carry.
And he talked about Asa.
“I was ten steps away,” he said to the ceiling. “Dan’s right. I was looking at my phone.”
Delta sat with her hands in her lap and listened. She had been there. She had been closer to the water than he had. She said nothing.
On the fourth night he cried, for a long time, without making much sound. His hands kept opening and closing in the straps, as if he were trying to hold on to something.
Delta’s hand came up off her lap. It stopped an inch from his.
She did not know what would happen if she touched him. She did not know if she was allowed. Nobody had ever taught her how to comfort someone. Nobody had ever done it for her, until it was too late to matter.
She put her hand back in her lap. But she moved her chair a little closer, so that he could hear her breathing. After a while, he stopped crying.
“You’re here again,” he said, on the fifth night. He was weaker now, and his voice was thin. “I can’t see you. That’s all right.”
And later, almost asleep: “It’s warm when you’re here.”
She looked down at her hand. She had not been doing anything with it. There was no magic in it. She did not know where the warmth was coming from.
Perhaps, she thought, it was the part of her that was inside him. Her own essence, knowing she was near.
She never spoke to him. She did not touch him. She sat in the chair until the corridor lights came on, and then she went back upstairs.
For hundreds of years, she had lain on a table just like this one, in this same building. It had been cold, and she had been alone on it. The men came and went. They did their work and wrote their notes and left. Nobody had ever stayed. Nobody had ever pulled up a chair beside her and waited with her until the pain was over.
Now she was seeing it from the outside. Zen was lying where she used to lie. And she was the one in the chair, doing for him the thing that nobody had ever done for her.
She did not have a word for what that felt like. She only knew that she could not leave him. Nothing in the world could have made her get up from that chair.

### The Anchor

It was the files that gave her the answer. The men had found it for her, and never noticed what they had found.
She read the notes on the machine again and again, night after night. An essence only came loose from Zen at the very edge of death. The machine did not pull it out. It could not. It only waited at the edge, and caught the essence as it came free by itself. Then, in the same moment, it pulled him back from the edge, so that he would live to be used again the next day.
So there was a moment, she saw, between the two. First the essence came free. Then the machine caught it. In between, for less than a breath, it belonged to no one. It was out of him, and it was not yet in the machine.
If something happened at that exact moment, so that the machine never caught it, the essence would simply go. Whole. Unbroken. Free to go wherever witches go when they are finally allowed to rest.
One essence. For one moment.
But that would only save one of them. The next essence would still be caught, and the one after that, until there was nothing left of him.
Unless.
There was a kind of magic that every witch had heard of, and almost none had ever done. It could hold a stretch of time in a loop.
You set two anchors in time. The first anchor was a moment you chose to go back to. The second anchor was a moment further on. When the world reached the second anchor, time would turn back to the first, and everything in between would happen again.
It was the same kind of magic as the curse. Her mother had spoken of both in the same voice, the voice for things that must never be done. Neither of them drew on a witch’s power. They drew on her life.
And this one was paid for by the day. Every day inside the loop had to be bought with a piece of the witch’s own life. The longer the loop, the more it cost. A loop of a few days might cost a witch a few years. A loop of many months could cost her most of the life she had left.
And the price was not paid only once. It had to be paid again every time time turned back.
That was why almost no witch had ever done it. Even a witch with a very long life could only afford a few turns, and then she would be dead.
Delta did not have a very long life left. She had already spent part of it on the curse, before she pulled it back. What remained might pay for one turn of the loop she needed. It would only leave her a few more years to live, which isn’t enough for the second loop.
But she had something else.
Every essence inside Zen had belonged to a witch who died before her time. They had been caught and kept and killed on tables like the one in front of her. Each of them had died with hundreds of years still unlived. Those years had not gone anywhere. They were still held inside her essence.
When an essence is finally set free, it lets go of everything it has been holding, all at once. All those unlived years would be released in a single moment. That would be enough to pay for one more turn.
So Delta would only have to pay for the first turn herself, with her own life. After that, each witch who was freed would pay for the next turn with the years she had never been allowed to live. And the next. Her dead friends would carry each other out, one by one.
She began to build it in the chair, night after night, a word at a time, under her breath. She had built the curse the same way once, in a hospital bed. It was the same kind of magic, and it would take the same price. But the curse had been made to kill. This one was made to set them free.
While she worked, she remembered something her mother had told her once, in the same low voice she had used for the curse.
A spell that holds time listens, her mother had said. It listens to the one who pays for it. But it also listens to anyone caught inside it who wants something with their whole heart, at the moment it turns. Whatever they want that much, they may keep.
Delta had not thought about those words in four hundred years. She did not think about them for long now. The only people inside this loop would be humans, and she did not believe any human could want something that hard. She had watched them for years. They wanted things loosely, and changed their minds, and let go.
The second anchor had to be here, on this table. Not at his death. At the exact moment an essence came free from him, when he was at the very edge of death and the machine had not yet caught it. In every turn, the first time an essence came free on this table, time would turn back. That essence would already be out of him, and out of the machine’s reach. Nothing would ever catch it.
But the machine would never let that moment stand. It was built to do two things in the same instant: catch the essence, and pull him back. As long as it did that, no essence would ever be free, and Zen would never be allowed to go.
So she would have to change the machine.
She had read its files for two years. By now she understood it better than the men who used it. At night, after they had gone home, she worked out exactly what to change. One part, so that at the edge it would not reach for what came loose. One more, so that it would not pull him back. Instead of saving him, it would simply stop, and let him go.
He would go over the edge. The essence would come free, with nothing waiting to catch it. That was the moment of the second anchor. And time would turn back.
There was one more problem. When time turned back, the machine would turn back too, to the way the men had built it. Any change she made with her hands would be undone the very first time.
So she made the change part of the spell. She wrote it in with the anchors, word by word, in the chair. Every time the world went back to that day in the cafeteria, her change to the machine would come back with it, the same way her memory would. In every turn, the first time an essence came loose on this table, the machine would let him die.
The first anchor she thought about for a long time. The further back she set it, the longer the loop would be, and the more every turn would cost. So she wanted it as close to the end as she could make it.
But it could not be too close. It had to be before the machine had taken anything from him. On that day, every essence would still be inside him, and every one of them could be set free in its turn. If she set the anchor even one day after the machine began, the essence taken that day would already be shut in its case when time turned back. It would stay in that case through every turn, forever, and no death of his could ever reach it.
And it had to be a day when he was well. Far from this room, with his strength back and his whole life still in front of him. If time only ever turned back to him lying here, broken, he would never get to live at all between one death and the next. She would be giving him nothing but this table, over and over.
The latest day that worked would have been some grey afternoon after the sea, when Asa was already gone and Zen was still at the university, carrying her death. That day would have cost the least.
She did not choose it.
She chose a day in the cafeteria. An ordinary one. The four of them at the table by the window. Asa in the middle of a story, Jess stirring her drink, Dan counting, Zen spinning his pen.
She told herself she chose it because it was a strong moment, easy to hold on to. That was true. It was also the last day she could remember when all four of them had been happy.
She worked out carefully what would happen each time. When time turned back, everything would return to the way it had been on that day in the cafeteria. Zen would be well. Asa would be alive. The essences the machine had taken and shut in their cases would be back inside him, because on that day they had never been taken.
But the essence that had come free at the second anchor would stay free. It had never been caught. It would have left the world entirely. Time could not reach it to bring it back.
So each time, there would be one fewer.
Then she did the sum. She was a witch. She did not look away from numbers.
Three hundred and some essences.
He would lie on this table three hundred and some times. The girl would go under the water three hundred and some times. The quiet one would walk out of that cottage into the screaming, again, and again, and again.
And she would let it happen. Every time. She would stand under the trees.
She was going to use a body on a table to get what she wanted. She knew what that made her. She had four hundred years of knowing.
The bitter is in the glass already, the old man said.
She put it in the glass. She kept building.

### The First Loop

On the last night, he did not ask if anyone was there.
For months he had asked it every night, into the dark, as soon as the door closed behind the men. Is someone there? She had never answered. He had talked to her anyway.
Tonight he did not ask. He turned his eyes toward the chair, as far as the strap across his forehead would let him, and looked at the place where she sat, as if he could see her.
“Stay,” he said. It was hardly a sound.
She stayed.
His breathing was very slow. The numbers on the screen were small, and getting smaller. No one else was in the room. The men had gone home at seven, as they always did. Before they left, they had looked at his numbers and written them down, and his father had said there were a few more weeks in him yet. They had not expected it so soon.
The machine was still fastened to him, as it always was. Wires ran from it to his chest, his arms and his temples. Every night it waited beside him in the dark. If he ever slipped to the edge on his own, it would wake, catch whatever came loose from him, and pull him back.
Tonight it would do neither.
She had changed it an hour ago, after the men had gone. Two small parts, the two she had found in the files. Her hands had been steady while she did it. Then she had sat down in the chair to wait, and her hands had not been steady since.
She moved the chair closer. She did not decide to.
She looked at him properly. He seemed smaller than he had ever been. His wrists had gone thin under the straps. His lips were cracked, because nobody remembered to give him water at night. On the inside of his arm, where they put the needle every morning, there was a bruise that never had time to heal, yellow at the edges and dark in the middle. The blanket she had brought him had slipped down to his waist. She pulled it back up to his chin.
He had been on this table for months. In all that time he had never once asked them to stop. He had come home on his own, because he believed a girl had drowned because of him, and he had lain down here as if this was what he owed.
She laid her hand flat on the steel, beside his, not touching. She could feel the cold of the table through her palm. She had forgotten how cold it was. She had lain on a table like this one for hundreds of years, and somehow, in the years since, her body had let her forget.
His fingers moved a little toward hers. They did not reach.
“Warm,” he said.
He smiled, a very small smile, at nothing. At the ceiling. At the chair. Then his eyes closed.
She sat with him and listened to every breath. Each one came a little later than the one before. In the gaps between them, the room was so quiet that she could hear the white lights hum.
She thought, very clearly: I could stop this.
She could put the machine back the way the men had built it. It would wake. It would catch the essence and pull him back, and tomorrow he would open his eyes, and they would take another one out of him, and he would still be alive. For a few more weeks. On this table.
And every essence still inside him would end up in a steel case with a buyer’s name on it, and none of them would ever rest.
She did not move.
A little later, his chest went down and did not come up.
The screen made one long sound.
The machine did not move. Its small lights stayed dark. It did not reach for him. It did not pull him back. The steel case at its side, with the next buyer’s name already on the label, stayed empty.
And something rose out of him.
It was not light. It was more like the first second outside the tunnel, when the sun had touched her skin before her eyes could open.
She knew who it was. It was the witch from the cell next to hers. For three hundred years they had never seen each other’s faces. They had only known each other’s voices, through the stone wall.
