On the day the men took the other witch away and she did not come back, Delta had pulled at her own iron door until her nails broke.
It stayed beside her for a moment. There were no words. There was only something like a hand laid briefly against her face.
You saw it.
She meant the way out. In all their years in the cells, none of them had ever found one. Delta had.
Then it was gone. Not behind a wall. Not into a box. Gone the way a breath goes when you finally let it out.
Rest.
Delta sat in the chair with her hand on the cold steel beside a dead boy’s hand. Her face was wet.
She understood why, this time. She was crying for two things at once. One half was for the witch from the next cell, who was free at last. The other half was for the boy on the table, who had paid for it. She could not separate the two halves. She did not try.
The moment had come. An essence was out of him, and nothing had caught it. This was the second anchor.
In every turn after this one, time would turn back in the very instant an essence came loose. But this first time, the spell was not finished. She had held back its last word until the moment was real. Now all it needed was that word, and the price.
There were footsteps in the corridor, running. The long sound from the screen had gone to some other screen upstairs, and somebody had heard it. She could not hide in this building. If they opened that door, they would find a researcher in a chair, a dead boy on the table, and a machine that had been changed.
She said the last word.
She felt the price taken at once. It was the same as the curse: a thread drawn out from the middle of her chest, long and thin. This time she did not pull it back. Years went with it. She could not count them. She only felt them go, the way you feel the warmth leave your hands in the cold.
The white lights went out, all of them, at once.
Somewhere, a cafeteria was loud. It always had been.

## IX. Arc 9 — Delta (The Last Loop)
@pov Delta

### The Last One

She had stood under the trees three hundred and some times.
There was a line of trees at the top of the beach, where the sand ended and the grass began. Every turn, on the day of the sea trip, she stood in their shade, unseen, and watched. She watched Asa swim out to Jess. She watched Jess step back into deeper water. She watched Asa go under, and not come up.
She never once went into the water. If she saved Asa, Zen would not go home with that guilt on him. He would not lie down on the table. The turn would never reach the second anchor, and none of her dead friends would be freed. So every time, she stood under the trees with her hand pressed flat against the bark, and let the girl drown.
She had sat in the chair beside the laboratory table three hundred and some times, and watched Zen die.
Each time it went the same way. A few months after the sea, he went home. They strapped him down. The first time the machine took him to the edge, an essence came loose from him, and the machine she had changed let it go, and let him go with it. One witch. One of her dead friends, free at last. It touched her face and was gone. And the years that witch had never been allowed to live paid for the next turn.
She had said every name aloud as it left, so that someone would have said it. Some of those names she had heard only once, called down a corridor hundreds of years ago. She had kept them all.
Now there was only one essence left inside him.
Ever since the men took her essence from her, there had been a hollow under her ribs, in the place where a witch’s essence is meant to sit. The hollow had always pulled a little toward Zen, because so many of her people were inside him. Now it pulled toward one thing only. It was faint, like hearing her own voice from another room.
Hers was the last.
There was nobody left to pay for another turn. This time, when the essence came free on the table, time would not turn back. The world would simply go on, with a dead boy in it, and a drowned girl. And Delta would be whole again, with her own essence back under her ribs, where it belonged. When she died, at last, she would be able to rest.
All she had to do was stand under the trees one more time. Sit in the chair one more time. Do nothing, the way she had done nothing three hundred times before.
She sat at the empty table beside theirs, unseen, in the sun from the window.
Every turn began here. This was where she had been sitting, invisible, on the morning she chose for the first anchor, so this was where she found herself each time time turned back. The same chair. The same light on the same scratched table. The same smell of fried eggs and floor cleaner.
Asa was in the middle of a story. Jess was stirring her lemonade. Dan was counting. Zen was spinning his pen.
She had watched this morning three hundred times. She knew every word of the story. She knew where Asa would stop to laugh, and that Jess would laugh a moment after her, a little too late. She knew that Zen would drop the pen on the same word every time, and pick it up, and start spinning it again.
She still did not know why they came back to that table every day.
They argued. Jess went quiet when she was hurt and would not say why. Asa laughed louder when something was wrong. Dan worried about all of them until it made them tired. Zen did not understand half of what was happening around him. And still, every morning, all four of them carried their trays to the same table by the window and sat down together, as if there were no other table in the room.
She had watched them hold it together through more deaths than they would ever know about. And she did not understand what they were holding.
She wanted to know. Before it ended.
She did not have a word for that wanting. It was not a plan. It was not for her dead friends. It was the first thing she had wanted for herself since the day she came out of the tunnel and felt the sun on her skin.
And there was only one way to find out. She could not learn it from under the trees, or from an empty table one step away. She would have to sit at theirs.
Delta stood up and walked out of the cafeteria.
In the corridor, where nobody was looking, she let go of the magic that kept her unseen. It was like stepping out of cold water. The air touched her differently. A boy came round the corner and nearly walked into her, and said sorry, and looked at her face as he said it.
Her face. She had nearly forgotten.
For years she had worn the researcher’s face, the one the surgeon above the garage had cut to match a dead girl’s photograph. That face belonged to the institute. She did not want to bring it to this table.
So, there in the corridor, she let her own face come back. She used magic for it. Out here, far from the institute’s machines, nobody would ever catch the trace. She felt the bones of her cheeks shift, and her eyes go back to their own shape, and the fringe she had cut to match the photograph fall away from her forehead. It took less than a minute.
It was the face she had been born with, four hundred years ago. The only place it still existed in the world, apart from on her, was a photograph in a folder at the institute, stamped Escaped. Do not approach. Nobody at this university had ever seen that folder.
It was a risk. She took it. If four people were going to look at her, for once she wanted them to see her own face.
Being a student would need papers, and a name on a list, and a room. She could buy all of that later. This morning she only needed four people to believe her.
Then she pushed open the doors.

### Seen

The noise did not stop. But heads turned.
Not many. A boy at the next table looked up from his phone. Two girls by the drinks machine glanced at her and went back to their talk. The woman behind the counter lifted her chin, the way she did for every customer. That was all. To them it was nothing. A student they did not know had come in late for lunch.
To Delta, it was like being struck.
She had forgotten what it was like to be looked at. For years she had walked through this building unseen. Before that, the only people who had looked at her were the men in the laboratory, and in the laboratory, being looked at meant that someone was coming toward you with a tray of instruments and a blade.
Nobody watching her would have guessed any of that. She walked in as if she had done it every day of her life. Her back was straight and her steps were unhurried. She did not look at the floor, and she did not look around for somewhere to hide. Her face was calm, almost bored. She had kept a calm face in front of the men for hundreds of years. She knew exactly how it was done.
Inside, it was a mess. Her heart was going far too fast. Her body still remembered the tray and the blade, and every pair of eyes that turned toward her made something in her flinch. A voice in her head kept telling her to go back out into the corridor, to let the magic close over her again, to be unseen, now, before it was too late. She did not listen to it. She walked to the counter.
She had never ordered here. She had only stood beside the counter, invisible, and watched other people do it, three hundred times over. She knew how it went. You looked at the board. You said what you wanted. You held out the money. She had heard Asa do it in every kind of mood.
“Iced coffee with milk,” she said. “Little ice. Seventy-five percent sweetness.”
The woman did not even look up. She just made it, as if it were an ordinary thing to ask for. Delta watched her hands: the coffee, the milk, the sugar going in. It was a strange feeling, to be served by someone who did not know what she was.
She tasted it at the counter. It was not right. It was never right. It never tasted the way the old man had made it, in the shop, in the morning sun. She had stopped expecting it to, years ago. She ordered it anyway, everywhere she went, because it was the closest thing to him she had left.
She took it to an empty table in the corner and sat down with both hands around the cup. Then she waited, to find out what happened to a person who could be seen.
What happened was Asa.
Delta heard her before she saw her. Across the room, Asa’s chair scraped back. Asa crossed the cafeteria in her easy way, stopping once to say hi to someone on the way, and leaned on the back of the empty chair opposite Delta as if she had known her for years.
“New student?”
Delta turned. There was the smile, close up for the first time. It was a very good smile. Delta had watched Asa put it on every morning in the corridor outside, three hundred times, the way other people put on a coat before going out into the cold. She had never been on the other side of it before.
“Yes. I’m Delta.”
Asa asked her the usual questions. Which course. Which dorm. Where she was from. Delta answered them. She had made up the answers on the walk across the cafeteria, and they came out smoothly enough.
Then came the next part, the part she did not know how to do. She had watched humans do it for years. After the questions, they said small untrue things to each other until they felt comfortable. I love your bag. This place is so nice. We should get coffee sometime. Delta could always hear which words they meant and which they did not. She had never learned how to say a thing she did not mean.
So she said a true one.
“You seem tired.”
She had not meant it to land the way it did. She watched the smile stop. She felt the secret this girl carried so carefully tremble inside her, like something nearly dropped. And she understood that nobody had ever said that to Asa before. Not her friends. Not her mother.
For a moment Asa said nothing at all. Then she laughed, a beat too late. “Wow. Okay. Hi to you too.”
But she did not walk away. She stayed where she was, leaning on the chair.
Delta thought of the phone calls she had heard three hundred times. Every Sunday night, Asa walked up and down the corridor outside the dorm with the phone pressed to her ear, saying yes, Mum and no, Mum and I know, I know. Then she hung up, and stood still for a moment with her eyes closed, and came back to the others louder and brighter than before.
“People who grow up without warmth,” Delta said, “learn to become warm for others.”
Asa stared at her.
Delta was not only talking about Asa. She was also talking about a boy on a steel table who had said warm to a chair he could not turn his head to see. And she was talking about herself. She did not realize that until much later.
Asa looked at her for a long moment, as if she were deciding something. Then she turned and waved across the whole cafeteria, with both arms, as if she had found something wonderful and wanted everyone to come and see.
Jess came first. She was holding her lemonade in both hands, so that her hands would have something to do. She bowed her head a little. H-hi. Her eyes went from Asa’s face to Delta’s, and back to Asa’s, very quickly. Delta felt something in her go still and careful. Then Jess smiled, and it was gone.
Then Zen, and Dan a step behind him.
Zen looked at her. The crease came between his eyebrows, the one he got when he was trying to remember a word.
He did not know her. He could not. In every turn she had been invisible to him, or a silence beside a table he could not turn his head on. But Delta could feel something in him reach toward her, faint and puzzled, the way a person half turns at a voice he thinks he knows. He did not know what it was. He only looked at her a moment longer than people look at strangers.
She looked back, a moment too long herself. She was checking. Her own essence was still there, inside him, exactly where she had last felt it. It was the only one left. Sitting this close to it, she could feel it more clearly than she had in years, like hearing her own voice from the next room.
Dan said, “Nice to meet you,” and looked straight at her while he said it. It was the way he looked at anything new: carefully, as if he were counting it and checking it was safe.
Four people, facing her.
She had been cut open by a hundred men and had never felt as exposed as she did at that table. None of it showed. She lifted her coffee, and looked back at them, and waited.

### Four Months

For three hundred turns she had watched what the four of them were holding from one table away. In the next four months, she learned what it was from the inside.
The papers took a week. She bought them the same way she had once bought a dead girl’s name, from a man who did not ask questions. By the second week she had a student card, a room on the third floor of the girls’ dorm, and a seat at the table by the window that Asa saved for her every day without being asked.
Asa was the first.
It began on the back stairs behind the library, where Asa went after her mother’s calls, when she did not want anyone to see her face for a while. Delta found her there one Sunday evening, sitting on the cold step with her phone face down on her knee.
“My mum just called to tell me my cousin got engaged,” Asa said, before Delta could ask. She laughed. “And then she asked me if I’d put on weight, because I sounded heavier. On the phone. You can hear weight now, apparently.”
It was a joke. Delta could tell it was meant to be a joke, from the shape of it. But it was not a joke. Underneath it was something raw and very old.
So she did not laugh. She did not know that she was supposed to. She sat down on the step beside Asa, and waited.
Asa looked at her, waiting for the laugh. When it did not come, something in her face gave way. And the rest came out. The mother who had never once said she was proud of her. The father who was always too busy with work to care, who came home after she was asleep and left before she woke, and who still did not know which year of university she was in. The way she had learned, very young, that if she was funny enough and bright enough and easy enough, people would stay. She talked until the light on the stairs went off, and then she kept talking in the dark.
After that, Asa talked to her for hours, most nights. Sometimes on the stairs. Sometimes on the phone at two in the morning, in a small flat voice that nobody at the lunch table had ever heard.
Delta listened. And sometimes she told Asa what she had learned from years of watching humans through her shop window. She had watched thousands of conversations through that glass. She had seen which ones turned ugly and which ones did not, and why.
“When your mother starts,” she said once, “change the direction of the conversation. Ask her about something she is proud of. Her garden. Your cousin’s wedding. People cannot criticize and boast at the same time. They have to choose.”
Asa tried it the next Sunday. She came back up the stairs afterward shining, and threw both arms around Delta, and held on.
Delta did not go rigid. She noticed that afterward, lying awake in her new room. A human had held her, without warning, and her body had not flinched. It had never happened before.
It was real. Delta could feel what people carried, and those hours were real. Asa was her friend. Delta had never had a friend. She did not have a word for it yet, but she knew the thing itself when she felt it.
And also, when they sat together at lunch, Asa laughed a little louder than the thing deserved. She leaned in close to Delta, closer than she needed to. And every time, her eyes went to Jess. Quickly. To see if Jess was looking.
Delta noticed it in the first week. She knew by now what Asa felt for Jess. She had heard it said out loud in the water three hundred times. What she did not understand was this. Asa was using her to make Jess jealous. The loud laughs and the leaning close were not really for Delta at all. They were for Jess to see. Asa wanted Jess to look at the two of them and feel left out. She wanted Jess to hurt. Delta could not see how hurting someone could make them love you.
She did not mind being used. She found that strange. Both things were true of Asa at once: the small voice on the phone at two in the morning, and the quick glance at lunch. Neither one made the other false.
Jess was harder.
Delta helped her with mathematics, in the library, on Tuesday afternoons. Jess was not stupid. She was quick, quicker than she knew. But halfway through every problem she stopped trusting her own answer, and rubbed it out, and wrote something worse. Delta learned to put a hand flat on the page before she could reach for the eraser. “You were right,” she would say. “Leave it.”
Jess was gentle with her, and thanked her every time, and once, after a test came back with a mark she had never had before, left a lemonade on Delta’s desk without a word.
But Delta felt something else in Jess, too.
Jess was jealous. It was not new. In three hundred loops Delta had felt it under the gentleness, small and hard, like a stone held in a closed fist.
Jess needed to be the one Zen looked at. She needed to be the one Asa sat beside.
And this time Delta understood something she had felt in every loop without knowing what it was. Jess knew what Asa felt for her. Perhaps not in words. But she knew. When Asa looked at her a moment too long, Jess went still and careful, and then smiled and said something ordinary, and let the moment pass.
She did not love Asa that way. She never would. But she never pushed her away either. She let Asa save her seat, and carry her drinks, and walk her back to the dorm, and look at her across the table, and she never once said don’t. If she said it, she might lose her. Asa was her friend, the first one she had ever had, and Jess could not let her go. She could not let Zen go either.
As long as she had both, she felt in control, and the jealousy stayed hidden. She could afford to be sweet.
That did not make the rest of her false.
Jess loved Zen. Delta could feel that too, steady and plain, the truest thing in her. And she loved Asa, as her friend, with her whole heart. The lemonade she left on Delta’s desk had been meant kindly.
Both things lived in the same girl. It was the same with Jess as it was with Asa.
Now a new girl was sitting at the table. Zen looked at her. Asa laughed with her, and leaned close to her, and went with her to the back stairs. For the first time, Jess could not be sure of either of them. She was not in control anymore, and the fist had begun to tighten.
Delta had done that. Just by sitting down.
Dan was polite to her from the first day, and careful, but with a quiet warmth. He watched her the way he watched the clock when Zen was late: steadily, without saying anything, as if he were waiting to find out what she would cost them.
Delta did not hold it against him. She understood it. Dan looked after Zen. He worried when Zen was late. He noticed when Zen went quiet. He checked every new person who came near him, and Delta was a new person.
She was looking after Zen too, in her own way, because her own essence was inside him. So the two of them were doing the same thing, from opposite sides of the table. But Dan did not know that. He did not know what Zen really was, or what was waiting for him at home, or that the new girl he was watching so carefully had sat beside Zen three hundred times while he died.
And there was Zen.
She argued with him. Nobody had ever argued with her. For four hundred years people had done things to her without asking what she thought. He asked. He disagreed. He lost, and smiled as if losing were a present.
When he sat near her, the hollow under her ribs was warm. That was her own essence, she told herself. It was only that. A thing recognizing itself.
She did not know why, then, she looked for him first when the doors opened.
He was confused too. She could feel it.
He still walked Jess back to her dorm. He still minded when she was sad. That had not changed, because it was real. But when he sat near Delta he went quiet, with that crease between his eyebrows. He looked like someone who has called one thing by a name all his life, and has just met the thing the name belongs to.
For three hundred loops, Asa had carried her love for Jess in silence. She had held it as carefully as a bowl filled to the brim, until the day at the sea when she could not hold it any longer, and said it aloud.
This time she was not holding it that way. She had found another way to make Jess look at her. So when the spring came, there would be no confession in the water.
But the sea would still be the sea.
“To the seaside!” Asa said.
Delta had stood under the trees three hundred times and watched Asa drown. She had always been able to do it, because until now Asa had been a stranger to her, a girl at someone else’s table.
Now she was not. Now Asa was the girl on the back stairs, and the small voice on the phone at two in the morning, and the friend who had thrown her arms around her and held on. Only the night before, Asa had made her laugh on those stairs.
Delta found that she could not stand under the trees and watch that girl go under the water again. Not this time. Not after that.
“I’ve never seen Angkor Wat Temple,” she said.
It was the first time she had ever changed anything.
She told herself it cost her nothing. One more turn of the road. Zen would go home in the end. He always did.
She was not thinking like a witch. She did not notice.

### Angkor Wat - Siem Reap, Cambodia

They left the guesthouse at four in the morning, in two tuk-tuks, half asleep. Dan had set three alarms. Asa had promised everyone a proper breakfast afterward, if they could stay awake that long. Jess fell asleep against Zen’s shoulder on the bumpy road, and Zen sat very still so as not to wake her.
It was still dark when they reached the water in front of the temple. Hundreds of people were already standing along the edge of it, whispering, holding up their phones. The five of them found a place near the front. Then they waited.
She saw the sun come up behind the towers.
First the sky went grey, then pink, then a deep, clear gold.
The temple came out of the dark slowly, a little at a time. First the five towers, black against the light, each one shaped like a lotus bud just about to open. The tallest stood in the middle, and the other four stood around it like guards. Then the long galleries below them. Then the whole great body of the temple, wide and low and perfectly balanced, with every line of it leading the eye up to the centre tower.
In the still water at their feet, the same temple appeared again, upside down, so clear that for a moment there seemed to be two of them, one standing in the sky and one in the pool, touching at the base. A breeze crossed the water. The second temple trembled, broke into ripples, and came back.
The sky behind it turned from gold to orange. The stone changed colour as the light reached it, from black to grey to a soft, warm brown, like old honey. Birds rose out of the trees on either side, hundreds of them, circled once over the towers, and were gone.
The whole crowd went quiet at once, as if somebody had asked them to. Even Asa did not say anything. Dan forgot to take a photograph. Jess put her hand over her mouth.
Delta stood very still.
Humans had made this. Nine hundred years ago, with no machines, they had cut this stone by hand, and carried it here, and set it piece by piece into something so beautiful that strangers from every country in the world still got up in the dark to stand in front of it and fall silent. The same kind of creature that had built the white room and the steel table had built this.
She had known that, in her head. She had never seen it before.
Delta had watched four hundred years’ worth of dawns in her head, in a cell with no window. She had watched a few thousand real ones since, from her shop doorway, alone. This was the first one she had ever seen with people.
Afterward, Asa arranged them all on the old stone steps for a photograph. She propped her phone on a ledge, set the timer, and ran back to squeeze in at the end beside Delta. Her arm came round Delta’s shoulders.
Delta let it stay.
Breakfast was at a row of wooden stalls by the road, just outside the temple grounds. Smoke from the charcoal grills drifted across the road in the early sun. They sat on low plastic stools around a metal table, and the woman who ran the stall did not give them a menu. She just looked at them, and nodded, and started cooking.
She brought kuy teav for Dan and Zen: big bowls of clear pork broth with soft rice noodles, slices of pork, bean sprouts, and fried garlic floating on top. There was a plate of fresh herbs and lime in the middle of the table for everyone to tear up and squeeze in.
For the girls she brought bai sach chrouk. Thin slices of pork that had been soaked in coconut milk and garlic and grilled over the coals until the edges went dark and sticky, laid over a mound of broken rice. On the side there were pickled cucumber and carrot, and a small bowl of clear soup with green onion in it.
Asa ate as if she had never eaten before. Jess stole the pickles from Asa’s plate, and Asa let her. Zen burnt his tongue on the soup, twice, and did not learn.
Delta ate slowly. The pork was sweet and salty and smoky all at once. She had lived in this country for years, but always alone, and always in a hurry, and she found she had never really tasted it.
The woman brought iced coffee with condensed milk without being asked, in tall plastic cups full of ice. Delta tasted hers. It was strong and very sweet, sweeter than seventy-five percent.
It was the closest she had ever come to the old man’s coffee. She did not say so. She drank all of it.
Then they went inside.
They crossed the moat on a long stone causeway. Great stone serpents ran along both sides of it, and at the ends their seven heads rose up and spread out like open fans. Beyond the gate, the walls of the galleries were carved from end to end. Kings rode to war on elephants. Gods and demons pulled on the body of an enormous snake to churn the ocean. There were armies and battles, heavens and hells, all cut into the stone so finely that you could see the patterns on their clothes.
And everywhere, on every wall and pillar and doorway, there were women carved in stone. Dancers. Hundreds of them. Each one had a different crown, a different necklace, a different way of wearing her hair. Some of them were smiling. One of them, beside a doorway where the light came in, had her head tilted a little to one side, as if she were listening to something. Asa stood in front of her for a long time without saying anything.
They climbed to the top. The steps were so steep that Jess went up them on her hands and knees, laughing at herself, with Zen behind her saying, “You’re fine, you’re fine.” At the top, a cool wind blew through the stone windows. Below them the jungle went on in every direction, green and endless, with the morning mist still lying in it. Three young monks in orange robes sat on the steps of the central tower, sharing a phone and laughing at something on it.
Delta laid her hand flat on the stone. It was already warm from the sun. It was nothing like the table.
Later they went to Ta Prohm, where the forest had been allowed to stay. Giant trees grew out of the roofs and walls there. Their roots were as thick as a person’s body, and they poured down over the stones and split them apart, so that you could no longer tell where the temple ended and the tree began. Dan read every sign aloud.
At the Bayon, great stone faces were carved into the towers, more than Delta could count, all of them smiling the same calm smile with their eyes half closed. Asa stood under each one and copied its smile until even Dan laughed.
All day, Zen kept falling back to walk beside Delta and ask what she thought of things. He listened to every answer as if it mattered.
At lunch they sat on plastic stools by the road and shared everything, and argued about the bill, and Asa won.
So this was it. This was what they were holding. Not one big thing. A hundred small ones. A shoulder to sleep on. Pickles stolen off somebody’s plate. Somebody reading the signs aloud, and somebody making the others laugh, and all of them waiting for each other at the bottom of the steps.
She understood it for exactly one day. By the next morning, it would be gone.
That night they went to a club on Pub Street, because Asa said you could not come to Siem Reap and not go.
The club was loud, and the lights were white and broken into pieces. The music came up through the floor and into her ribs. Delta sat very straight on a high stool with a glass of something she did not drink, and looked calm. She looked as if she went to places like this every week.
Inside, she was counting her breaths. One, and the lights flashed. Two, and somebody pushed past her. Three. She stayed because they were staying.
At the bar, Zen bought a round of drinks. He handed Delta hers first, without thinking about it, and smiled at her when she took it.
Jess saw.
After that, Jess was drinking. She did not usually drink. That night she emptied one glass after another, quickly, without seeming to taste them. Zen was looking at Delta. Jess was looking at Zen. Asa was looking at Jess. Nobody was looking at Dan, who was watching all of them and holding everyone’s phones.
The boys went to the bathroom.
Jess turned to Delta at once. She had been waiting for it. She was drunk. Her eyes were wet and very bright, and her words ran into each other.
“Do you like him?”
Delta did not know how to lie. She was only beginning to learn why humans did.
“I don’t know,” she said.
It was the truth. It was the worst thing she could have said. A no would have been a kindness, and a yes would have been a fight. I don’t know meant that it was possible.
Jess laughed. It was a horrible sound.
“You don’t know.” She leaned in close, so close that Delta could smell the drink on her breath. “He looks at you. Did you know that? He looks at you like you’re a question he wants to answer. Two years, and he has never once looked at me like that. Not once.”
“Jess,” Asa said. “You’re drunk. Let’s go back to the—”
“And you.” Jess turned on her. “You used to look at me like I was the only person in the room. Do you think I never noticed? Now you look at her. Now you go off to the stairs with her.” She turned back to Delta, and her voice cracked. “You have Asa. You have everyone looking at you, all the time, and you don’t even want them. He’s the only thing I have. He’s the only—”
She could not finish. She stood up so fast that her stool went over against the wall. Then she was gone, through the crowd, toward the door.
Asa was already on her feet. For one second, the bright smile she always wore was gone, and Delta could see what she was really feeling underneath it. It was not happiness that her plan had worked. It was fear. For four months Asa had been trying to make Jess jealous. She had wanted Jess to feel left out, and to look at her again. She had never once thought about what Jess might do when it finally hurt enough.
“Jess!”
Asa ran after her.
Delta went too, but the crowd closed between them at once. Someone stepped back into her with a tray of drinks. A group of boys pushed past, shouting a song. By the time she reached the door, both of them were gone.
Outside, Pub Street was full of light and noise. Music came out of every doorway. Tuk-tuk drivers called to her from the kerb. Delta stood on the step, outwardly calm, and turned slowly, looking for a white dress. Inside, her heart was going so fast she could feel it in her throat.
Then she heard Asa’s voice. It was coming from the corner at the end of the street, where the lights stopped. Delta followed it.
They were standing under a broken street lamp at the mouth of a narrow lane. Jess had her back to the wall. Her face was wet and her mascara had run. Asa was in front of her, holding her by both arms, as if she was afraid Jess would fall, or run. They were both talking at once.
Then Asa’s voice rose above Jess’s, hard and shaking, and every word came clear.
“You know that I love you, right?”
Jess stopped talking.
“Not the way a friend loves a friend,” Asa said. “The way a boy loves a girl. That way. That’s what this is. That’s what it’s always been.”
She had said it to stop Jess. Delta could feel that. It was the only thing left that Asa could think of that would make Jess stand still and listen to her. She had thrown it at Jess the way you throw cold water at someone to shock them.
It worked. Jess stood absolutely still.
Delta stopped too, a few steps away, in the dark. She had heard those words before, three hundred times. She had heard them in the bright water at the sea, in Asa’s soft, careful voice, with the waves coming in. Now she heard them here, under a broken lamp in Siem Reap, shouted, with the music thumping behind them.
They were the same words. She had moved the five of them hundreds of kilometres away from the sea, and the words had found them anyway.
She felt what was in Jess. It was not surprise. It was the same thing she had felt in Jess in the water, every time: fear. Jess had known. She had always known. She could live with knowing, as long as nobody ever said it. Now it had been said out loud, in a street, and it could never be taken back.
“No,” Jess said. She was shaking her head. “No. No. Don’t. Why would you say that? Why would you say that to me now?”
“Jess—”
Jess pulled her arms out of Asa’s hands. She backed away one step, and another. Then she turned and ran, into the lane, away from the lights.
“Jess!” Asa went after her.
Delta ran after both of them.
The lane was dark and narrow, with high walls on both sides. It smelled of rubbish and wet concrete. Their footsteps were very loud in it. Somewhere ahead, Jess was crying as she ran, with one hand on the wall to keep herself steady.
At the far end of the lane, where it opened onto an empty patch of ground, a van was parked with its engine running and its side door open.
There were men.
What happened after that, Delta never put into words. Not then, and not later.
She only ever let herself keep a few things from it.
She could have gone unseen. It would have taken one breath. She could have walked out of that place between their hands.
She did not. Asa’s fingers were locked in hers. Jess was screaming a name, and the name was Delta’s.
On the table in the laboratory, over four hundred years, she had learned one way to bear what was done to her. She let her mind leave. She went back in her head to her mother’s doorstep, and stayed there, until the men were finished with her body. It was the only place they had never been able to follow her. She could have done that now, too. She did not. Asa and Jess had no doorstep to go to. They could not leave, and she would not have them be there alone.
It was the first thing she ever did as one of them.

### The Wrong Anchor

A ceiling. Her own bed, in her room on the third floor of the dormitory. Dark.
She lay without moving for a long time. Her body was whole. There was no blood on it, no bruise, nothing broken. But it remembered everything. Her hands remembered Asa’s fingers locked in hers. Her ears remembered Jess screaming her name. She lay on her back in the dark and shook, and could not stop.
Outside, it was quiet. Somebody’s music was playing very softly through a wall. A motorbike went past on the road.
She made herself get up. She went out into the corridor in her bare feet and walked to the end of it, to Asa’s door, and stood there in the dark with her hand flat against the wood. After a long time, she heard Asa turn over in her sleep, and mumble something, and go still again.
Alive.
Delta slid down the wall and sat on the floor outside the door until her legs stopped shaking.
Then she tried to understand what had happened. She reached inside herself for her spell, the way you put your hand out for a wall in the dark, to find out where she was.
The spell had turned. Time had gone back. But it had gone to the wrong place.
It was not the cafeteria. It was not that ordinary morning four months ago, with Asa in the middle of a story. It was a night in March, two weeks before the trip.
The first anchor was in the wrong place.
And there was something worse. She could still feel her own essence inside Zen, faint, like her own voice in another room. It had not come free. So Zen had died, because only his death could make time turn back. But he had not died on the table. He had died somewhere else, some other way, in the days after Siem Reap, while she was already dead in that lane and could not see it.
No essence had come free. So nothing had paid for this turn. Her own life had paid for the first turn, long ago, and every turn since then had been paid for by one of her dead friends as she was set free. This time there was nobody to pay.
The spell should not have been able to move at all. Time should simply have gone on, with all of them dead.
Somebody had made it turn.
In the morning she went to breakfast and sat down at the table by the window and watched them.
It took her less than a minute to find out who. Dan was looking at the other four as if he could not believe they were really there, as if he had just watched every one of them die. He kept counting them with his eyes. One, two, three, four, five. His hands were shaking on the table, and he hid them under it. When Asa said, “So, about Siem Reap, I found a guesthouse—” he said no before she had even finished the sentence.
He remembered.
Delta turned it over slowly. Dan was not a witch. He had no magic. He had no spell of his own.
What he had was an obsession.
She had watched it for three hundred loops without giving it that name. Every day he counted his friends. One, two, three, four. He needed all four of them alive. He could not live in a world in which even one of them was missing. He had never been able to.
That obsession was what had turned time back.
Her spell was still there, hanging over all of them. It was still built to turn back whenever Zen died. It had no strength left of its own, because nobody had been freed to pay for it. But a witch’s spell does not only answer to the life that pays for it. It also answers to how much someone wants something. And nobody at that table wanted anything as hard as Dan wanted this.
She remembered her mother’s warning then, the one she had brushed aside in the chair. It also listens to anyone caught inside it who wants something with their whole heart. She had been so sure that no human could ever want anything that hard. She had been wrong about humans before. She had never been this wrong.
So when Zen died in the days after Siem Reap, Dan’s refusal to lose them had grabbed hold of her spell and pulled time backward with it. Not to the cafeteria, where she had set the first anchor. To the last night Dan could bear to remember. A night in March, two weeks before the trip, before any of it had happened.
And it would go on. As long as Dan could not let any of them go, every time Zen died, anywhere, the world would be pulled back to that same midnight in March. Over and over. Until Dan let go.
She should have stopped it. She should have told Dan everything, there and then, and made him let go.
She looked across the table. Asa was alive, and telling a story. Jess was stirring her lemonade.
She did not say anything.
That was a bitter spoon, and she knew whose glass it went in.

### The Seaside

They went to the sea after all.
It was Dan’s idea this time. He would not hear a word about Siem Reap. He said the bus were too expensive, and the city was too far, and anyway, wouldn’t it be nice to just go somewhere quiet? A cottage by the sea. A few days of nothing. He said it lightly, but his hands were shaking, and he had already found the cottage and paid for it before anyone could argue.
He did not know about the sea. He had slept through it, in every one of Delta’s turns. To him, the sea was the safest place in the world.
Delta said nothing. Outside, her face was calm. Inside, something went cold. The sea was where Asa had drowned three hundred times.
But she told herself she could manage it. She knew exactly how it went. She knew the hour, and the place in the water, and the wave. And this time was already different. Asa had not been carrying her secret the same way in this turn. She had been using Delta to make Jess jealous instead. So she would have no reason to take Jess into the water and tell her. There would be no confession.
And Delta would be in the water herself, in Asa’s place, holding Jess’s hands. If anything went wrong, she would be right there.
On the second afternoon, it was hot and bright and the sea was flat and blue. Asa said she had a headache and stayed on the veranda in the shade with a book over her face. Zen and Dan sat on the sand. Delta took Jess down to the water.
Jess could not swim. Delta held both her hands and walked backward into the shallows, the way she had watched Asa do it three hundred times, pulling Jess gently along. Jess shrieked when the first wave hit her knees, and then laughed, and then shrieked again. The water was warm. The sand was soft under their feet.
They stood in the shallows, up to their waists, rocking a little with the waves. Jess was almost happy. Delta could feel it in her, a small, light thing, like a held breath let go.
Then Jess looked back at the beach.
Delta did not need to turn around. She knew where Zen was looking. He was looking at her. He had been looking at her all afternoon.
She felt Jess’s jealousy tighten like a fist.
Jess let go of Delta’s hands. She turned round in the water, slowly, so that she was facing the shore, standing side by side with Delta. She lifted one hand and waved at the beach. She was smiling.
“Do you know what I’ve always known?” she said.
Her voice was very soft. Gentle, even. She did not look at Delta while she spoke. She kept her eyes on Zen the whole time, and kept smiling, so that anyone on the sand would have thought the two of them were talking about the waves.
“I know Asa loves me,” Jess said. “Not the way friends do. The other way. I’ve known since first year. The way she looks at me when she thinks I’m not looking. The way she always sits on my side of the table. The way she walks me home when it’s not even on her way. I’m not stupid, Delta.”
Delta said nothing.
“And I know Zen doesn’t love me. Not the way I love him.” The smile did not move. “He loves me like a sister. Like someone he’s supposed to look after. When I tell him I love him, he says I know, because he can’t say it back. I know that too.”
A wave came in. Jess rocked with it and kept her balance, and kept smiling at the beach.
“And I didn’t care,” she said. “Do you understand? I didn’t care about any of it. Asa loved me. Zen looked after me. I sat between the two of them at that table every single day, and they were both mine. It was enough. I had everything I needed, and nobody had to say anything, and nothing ever had to change.”
She waved at the beach again. Zen did not wave back. He was not looking at Jess. He was looking at Delta.
“And then you came,” Jess said softly. “You just sat down. And now Asa goes to the stairs with you, and tells you things she never told me. And Zen looks at you like you’re a question he wants to answer. He has never looked at me like that. Not once. Not in two years.”
“Jess—”
“I’m not angry.” Jess’s smile got wider. Her eyes were wet. “I just want him to look at me.”
Delta kept her face still. Inside, she was cold all over. For four months she had helped this girl with her mathematics, and felt her kindness, and taken the lemonade she left on the desk. All of that had been real. This was real too. Jess had known everything, all along, and she had held on to both of them anyway, quietly, because it suited her.
Then Jess took one step backward, away from Delta, and another, still facing the shore. Her face was calm. It was the face of someone doing a small, reasonable thing.
Delta could feel what she wanted as clearly as if she had said it out loud.
Look at me.
If something happened to her, Zen would have to look. He would have to run to her, and hold her, and be afraid for her, the way he had never once been afraid for her before.
“Jess, don’t—”
The seabed dropped away. Jess went under. A wave came in sideways and pulled her further out, and her head came up, and her arms, and she was screaming.
Delta went after her. She was not a swimmer. She had never needed to be. In four hundred years nobody had ever let her near the sea. The water was suddenly cold, and her feet could not find the bottom, and every wave filled her mouth with salt.
She got her arms around Jess anyway. She held her up, and turned her toward the beach, toward the figure already running down the sand, and pushed her with everything she had.
She saw Zen go into the water up to his chest. She saw his hands catch hold of Jess’s arm.
Then the same water closed over her.
Drowning was slow.
The light went first. It was bright above her, and then it was further away, and then it was green, and then it was dark. The noise of the beach went away too, until all she could hear was the water in her ears, and her own heart, slowing.
It was nothing like the table. On the table, someone was always doing it to you. There were hands, and voices, and men who knew your name. The sea did not even know she was there. It was not cruel. It simply did not care.
The last thing she thought was that now she knew what Asa had felt, three hundred times, while Delta stood under the trees and watched and did nothing.
Then the ceiling.

### The Bus

That time, Dan would not let them go anywhere.
There was no trip. No sea, no temples, no cottage. When Asa brought it up at lunch, Dan said he was sorry, he really was, but he could not afford it this year, and he did not want them going without him. He said it so firmly that even Asa dropped it. Everyone would just go home for the break instead. Home was safe. Dan was sure of that.
On the last morning of term, Asa, Jess and Dan took the bus home together. They lived along the same road, a few towns apart, and they always went back that way.
Delta said she was waiting for her parents to pick her up. It was easier than saying she had none.
Zen was waiting for his.
They stood together at the bus stop outside the gate, with their bags at their feet. Asa did an impression of their statistics lecturer. Dan checked the timetable three times. When the bus came, Jess turned round and hugged Delta, quickly and awkwardly, with her bag still on her shoulder. Then she stepped back and looked surprised at herself, as if she did not know why she had done it.
“Have a good break,” Jess said, and climbed on.
Delta watched the bus pull away. Asa’s face was in the back window, pulling a face at her. Then it turned the corner, and was gone.
The dark car was already outside Zen’s dormitory.
Delta knew that car. She had watched it come for him three hundred times. It was long and black and very clean, and the driver never got out. In the back, behind the dark glass, there was room for three more people.
Zen stood beside it with his bag, looking down the road where the bus had gone. For a moment it seemed as if he was about to say something. Then the back door opened from the inside, and he got in.
Delta sat on the bench in the courtyard, in the sun, with her bag beside her, waiting for parents who did not exist.
The campus was emptying around her. Doors banged. Suitcases rattled over the paving stones. A cleaner went past with a trolley and nodded at her. Then it was quiet, the particular quiet of a place that everyone has left.
She did not go anywhere. Her shop could wait. She liked the courtyard when it was empty, and she liked the sun on her face, and she was tired in a way that sleep did not help.
The news came in the afternoon, on her phone.
First a message in the class group chat, from a girl she hardly knew: is this the bus jess and asa were on?? Then a link. A road in the hills. Two buses. One of them had drifted across the middle of the road on a bend, into the other. A passenger said the driver had looked round for a second, because someone on board was screaming. There was a photograph of twisted metal lying in a field, and people standing at the edge of the road with their hands over their mouths.
An hour later, there was a list of names.
Jess. Asa. Dan.
She read the list four times. She read it slowly, every letter, as if the names might change if she read them carefully enough.
She had seen them die more often than she could count. Asa in the water, three hundred times. Jess and Asa in a lane in Siem Reap. Herself, under the sea. She knew how this worked now. When Zen died, time would turn back, and all of them would wake up in March, whole, with no idea that anything had happened. The morning would give them back. It always had.
She waited for the weight in her chest to be smaller, because of that.
It was not smaller.
She sat on the bench with the phone in her lap until the sun went off the courtyard and the stone grew cold under her. Far away, in the hills, she could feel the pull of her own essence inside Zen. He was at his parents’ house by now. He would hear the news soon, if he had not heard it already. She knew exactly what it would do to him.
She could not go to him. The institute knew this face. It had a photograph of it in a folder, stamped Escaped. Do not approach. She had given up the researcher’s face to sit at their table, and she could not use magic to put it back on, because the machines at the gate would catch the trace before she reached the door.
So she sat, and felt the pull, and could do nothing at all.
Zen never reached the laboratory table.
Delta did not see what happened to him. She only put it together afterward, from what she knew of him, and from what she had felt through the pull that night. But she was sure she was right.
The news reached him at his parents’ house, the same way it had reached her: on his phone, sitting on the bed in his old room with the door shut. Jess, Asa and Dan. All three of them, gone in one afternoon.
He blamed himself.
There had been a car. A large car with a driver, sent to take him home, and the whole back seat empty. It could have carried all four of them. Their towns were on the way. He had stood beside it and watched his friends climb onto the bus, and for one moment he had almost said something. Then he had not said it. He had waved, and got into the back seat alone.
It was such a small thing. One sentence he had not said. Come with me, there’s room.
If he had said it, they would be alive. He could not get past that. He went over it again and again: the bus stop, the empty seats, his own hand waving goodbye. Everyone he loved had been on that bus, and he had been sitting in a car with room for all of them.
The guilt was more than he could carry. That same night, he took his own life.
Delta was still on the bench when it happened. It was after midnight, and the courtyard was dark. She felt the pull inside her go suddenly, terribly still.
Then time turned back, and she knew that he was dead.
She knew why, too. She had watched guilt work on him three hundred times. She had watched it walk him home to that table, every single time, and lay him down on it.

### The Roof

Dan followed them everywhere after that. He stood under their windows at night. Jess and Asa were frightened of him.
“Let him be,” Delta said. She looked at him as she said it. She wanted him to hear that someone knew.
He only looked tired.
At the party, Jess drank too much, and the jealousy she had been hiding for four months finally broke out.
She turned on Asa first.
“Why are you so close to Delta?!”
She was on her feet, shouting across the table at her best friend. Her voice shook, but it was not the small voice any of them knew.
“I was your friend first!”
Delta watched Asa’s face. There it was. Relief, bright and helpless. For four months Asa had used Delta to make Jess jealous, and at last Jess had turned around.
“Oh, now you notice me?” Asa said. Her voice was high and shaking. “Have you ever noticed that I don’t want to just be your friend?”
For a second nobody breathed.
Delta felt Jess hear it. Every word of it, exactly as Asa meant it. Something in Jess went tight and frightened. Then Delta felt her push it away, the way you shove a drawer shut with your hip. Jess had decided not to have heard the second half. She answered only the first.
“Notice you? I sat next to you for two years!” Jess laughed, and it was an ugly sound. “I laughed at every joke. I waited at every table. And you never told me one real thing. Not one. Then she walks in, and in a month you’re on the stairs with her, whispering. Did you think I didn’t see?”
“You never asked,” Asa said. “You never asked me anything. You were too busy staring at him.”
“You were supposed to be mine.”
The room went quiet.
Asa heard those words one way. Delta could feel it, the sudden wild hope in her.
But Jess did not mean what Asa wanted her to mean. She meant my best friend. The one person who had sat down beside her when nobody else did. She meant it with all her heart, and she was terrified of losing her. And she knew exactly how Asa would hear it. She had known what Asa felt for two years. She said it anyway.
It was real. It was only not the thing Asa had waited two years to hear.
Then Jess turned to Delta.
Delta had been cut open by men who hated her. She had never been looked at like this by someone who had once left a lemonade on her desk.
“And you. You just sit there. With your coffee and your calm face.” Jess’s eyes were wet and perfectly steady. “You took my friend. You took my seat. You’re taking him, and you don’t even want him. You don’t want anything. You just take things, to see what happens.”
“Jess,” Zen said.
“Do you know what I think about, when you’re helping me with my homework? When I’m saying thank you, Delta?” She smiled. It was her gentle smile. That was the worst of it. “I think about you being gone. I think about it all the time. An accident. A fall. And I’m not even sorry.”
She swept Delta’s cup off the table. It broke against the wall.
Nobody moved.
Delta understood then that she had been wrong about Jess’s jealousy. She had thought it was small. It was not. It had been there long before Delta came, kept quiet for as long as Jess had what she wanted. Now that she was losing it, there was nothing gentle left on top.
“Everyone thinks I’m so sweet,” Jess said. “Poor quiet Jess. I am so tired of it.”
Then she turned on Zen.
“And you. You think I can’t see where you look?” Her voice cracked at last. “I told you I loved you. Every day. You said I know. You let me stand there like a beggar. So say it now. Say it in front of her. Say you love me.”
Zen opened his mouth. Nothing came out.
Jess was crying now, openly. She loved him. That had never been a lie. It was the reason all of this hurt her so much.
“Fine,” Jess said softly. “Maybe you’ll look at me when something happens.”
She ran. The door hit the wall. Asa went after her, and then Zen.
Dan started to follow them. Then he stopped, and looked back at Delta, and sat down beside her instead.
Neither of them spoke for a moment.
“Why didn’t you follow them?” Delta asked quietly.
“I’ll keep you company.”
“Why?”
“I don’t want you to be alone.”
She looked at him. He had died for his friends more times than he could count, and he still thought to sit down beside the one who was left behind.
Delta smiled slightly.
“I’m fine.”
She wanted to tell him the truth. That she knew about the loop. That she remembered every death he remembered. She did not know how humans said a thing that large.
She looked directly at him.
“I won’t die.”
Dan’s expression darkened.
“You have no idea.”
He thought she was a girl who did not know what was coming. He had been shown her body in Siem Reap. He had lost her to the sea.
So she gave him a hint instead. She smiled, suddenly and brightly.
“Have you ever seen me die with your own eyes?”
She watched it reach him. His face went still. She could see him going back through every loop, one by one, and finding that she was right. Each time, she had been missing, or already dead when he found her. He had never once watched it happen.
He was beginning to ask himself whether she was the key to the loop.
Before he could say it aloud, a loud noise echoed outside.
They ran outside.
Jess and Asa were lying on the ground below the building. They had fallen from the roof. Neither of them was moving.
Delta stood beside Dan and looked at her two friends.
Dan had fallen to his knees. His eyes were closed. He was waiting for time to turn back. For the white ceiling, and March.
Delta knew it would not come.
She had built the loop. She knew its one rule, the rule Dan had not yet worked out. Time only turned back when Zen died. And Zen was alive. He was standing on the roof above them with his hand still stretched out.
So the morning would come instead. And another after it.
She had always known this could happen. She had never had to live it. In three hundred loops and all of Dan’s, Zen had always died in the end, and she had never once had to wake up the day after.
Asa did not call at two o’clock. Nobody left a lemonade on her desk. She bought one herself and sat with it at their table until the ice was gone, trying to understand how a thing could go on being finished.
This was what the humans had. Every time. No wheel. Only the next morning, and the one after.
She understood the old man’s funeral now. She understood why they stood together.
There was nobody to stand with.
Months later she came round a corner and heard two voices. Zen’s came first, low and unsure.
“I think I like Delta.”
She stopped walking.
She did not know what the hollow under her ribs was doing. She had no time to find out. Dan’s voice came over the top of it, shouting.
“Because of her, Asa and Jess died!”
“That’s not her fault,” Zen said.
But it was her fault. Dan was right. He did not know how right.
Jess had always been jealous. But she had kept it hidden, because she had always felt in control. Zen was hers, and Asa was hers.
Delta had taken that control away, simply by sitting down at their table. After that, there was nothing to hold Jess’s jealousy back. And Asa, for the first time, had someone to make Jess jealous with.
Jess had begun to hate her. Asa had begun to use her. Those two things had led both girls up to that roof.
She had not pushed anyone. She had not meant any harm. They were dead because of her all the same.
She walked away silently, with her coffee in her hand. After that she avoided both of them.
A few weeks later, Zen stopped her in the courtyard. She had not slept.
“Delta. Wait. Please.”
He was thinner. He had that crease between his eyebrows.
“I have to tell you something. I’ve been trying to understand it for months, and I think I finally do.”
She knew what he was going to say. She had already heard it, from around a corner.
“I like you,” he said. “Not the way I liked Jess. I thought that was love. It wasn’t. I didn’t know the difference until—”
He stopped there. The rest of the sentence had two dead girls in it.
Delta looked at him. He was warm. He was alive, and they were not. He had stood on that roof with his hand out, an arm’s length short. And now he was standing in the sun, telling her that Jess had been right to be afraid.
Something rose in her that she did not understand. There was grief in it, and guilt. Under both there was a third thing, which wanted to say yes to him.
She could not bear that. Not when they were dead because of exactly this.
She did not know where the words came from.
“I wish the one who died that day was you… not Asa and Jess.”
Her voice shook. She heard it shake and did not recognize it.
It was the dark part of her, the part she had tried so hard to keep small. It came out whole.
She saw his face, and understood what she had done one second too late to take it back.
Zen had not been well for months. She had seen it and looked away. He had stopped sleeping. He had stopped arguing. He went over that night on the roof again and again. The slap. The low wall. His own hand reaching out, and closing on nothing.
He had been holding on by one thin thread. The thread was the thought that it might not have been his fault.
Her sentence cut it.
She watched that thought leave his eyes. If Delta, of all people, wished he had died in their place, then it was true. He had been too slow. One step faster, and he would have caught them. Jess and Asa were dead because of him.
And if that was true, he knew only one way to pay for it.
Zen turned and ran.
She understood too late where he was going. It was the same building. The same roof. He jumped from it, and he died.
He had paid with his life for a debt he did not owe. And her sentence was what had sent him.
It was the second time her words had killed someone.
The first had been years ago, at midnight, in a hospital bed. She had spoken the last word of her curse against all humans, and the old man who sold coffee, the man who had saved her life, had walked into it and died. She had ended the curse that same night. She had sworn she would never use words to kill again.
Now she had done it to Zen.

### The Bench

The ceiling. March. Midnight.
She lay in the dark with the sentence still in her mouth. The one she had said to Zen in the courtyard. I wish the one who died that day was you. She could still taste it. She had watched his face empty out when she said it, and then she had watched him run.
She made herself look at the truth, the way she made herself look at numbers.
The loop was Dan’s now. His need to save them was what turned it. But she had known that since the morning after Siem Reap, and she had never once tried to stop it. She had let it turn, again and again. Every time time went back, she got to sit at that table with the four of them again. She got Asa on the back stairs, and Jess in the library, and Zen walking beside her asking what she thought of things. She had not wanted to give that up. So she had said nothing.
And they had died of it. Every time. Not of fate. Not of bad luck. Of each other. Jess’s jealousy in a club. One step backward in the sea. A scream on a bus. A slap on a roof. And last of all, a sentence of her own, said in a courtyard to a boy who was already falling.
She had told herself she was only watching. She had not been only watching for a long time.
In the morning, she bought an iced coffee at the cafeteria counter, little ice, seventy-five percent sweetness, and took it out to the bench in the courtyard. She sat in the full sun, facing the flower bed, and waited.
She had seen Dan’s face at breakfast. He had looked at Zen across the table the way a person looks at a cliff edge. Something had changed in him overnight. She thought he would come.
He came. He walked across the courtyard like someone who had stopped running, because there was nowhere left to run to. He sat down on the bench beside her and did not say anything for a while.
Outside, Delta was calm. She kept her eyes on the flowers and her hands still in her lap. Inside, her heart was going very fast. She had never told anyone the truth about herself. Not in four hundred years.
When he spoke, it was about nothing.
“Are those flowers beautiful?”
She hummed. She did not know. She had never been able to tell what humans meant by beautiful, only what made them stop and look.
“Do you like them?” he asked.
“I’m sitting here because I like the sunlight.” She held her hand out into the light and turned it over, and watched the warmth sit in her palm. “I like the warmth of sunlight. I can’t get enough of it.”
Dan laughed, lightly, as if it had surprised him. “Most people try to avoid sunlight. But not you.”
“I used to live in a place that had no sunlight for a very long time.”
She said it plainly. It was the first true thing she had ever told him about herself. She turned her head and looked straight at him. “So I really yearn for it.”
He teased her. Something about skin cancer, and how sunlight could kill you.
She could have let it go. She could have laughed, the way Asa would have. Instead she told him the next true thing, to see whether he would understand it.
“Cancer isn’t as painful as drowning.” She paused. “Or being raped… and then killed.”
She watched his smile freeze.
She tilted her head and smiled at him, as if she had said nothing at all. She wanted him to know, without her having to say it in so many words, that he was not the only one who remembered.
He understood. She saw it go through him. “How do you know that?” Then he shook his head, and his voice changed. “No. That’s not the right question. Who are you exactly… Delta?”
“If I tell you I’m a witch… would you believe me?”
He did not laugh. A week ago he would have. But for him, a week ago was several deaths back.
“Then explain something to me,” he said. “Why are you trapping us in this loop? Delta… what do you want?”
So he thought she had built the cage. Of course he did. She was the witch.
“I didn’t trap you in this loop,” she said quietly. “You trapped yourself. This loop should have ended a long time ago. But your obsession keeps it alive.”
He did not understand. She could see that he did not, and that he was trying very hard to.
So she picked up her coffee and turned it in the light, and told him about the old man. Not about the shop, or the curse, or the hospital. Only about the coffee, because it was the truest thing she owned. A man had once explained the whole world to her with a glass in his hand, and in all the years since, she had never found a better way to say it.
Zero sweetness was honest, but too bitter to finish. A hundred was only sugar. Fifty was a fight, and bitter always won a fair fight. Seventy-five was right: three parts sweet, to carry one part bitter. The bitterness had to be there. The bitterness was the coffee.
“Humans are the same,” she said.
He still did not understand. She had not understood either, the first time. She had sat on the floor of a hospital room with a dead man’s glass in her hands and cried, and not understood it for days.
“What have you been ordering, Dan?” she asked him. “Every time you wake up. A hundred percent. Everyone alive. Nothing lost. Not one bitter drop anywhere in the glass.” She put the cup down on the bench between them. “That isn’t an ending. That’s why it never ends.”
She stood up and stepped in front of him, and leaned down a little, so that he had to look at her.
“Dan. Do you think humans are purely evil?”
He said nothing.
“Or purely good?”
He looked completely lost. So she asked him the question she had really come to ask.
“Dan… do you believe you are a good person?”
She watched him try to say yes. She could feel it in him, the word almost coming. He had spent every life believing he was the one trying to save them. He was the only one who had ever tried.
The word did not come.
She let him sit with that. She did not help him. Nobody had helped her.
Then she straightened up. “Maybe you need time to process this,” she said. She turned to go. “If you figure it out… you can come find me.”
She walked away across the courtyard, with the sun on her back. She did not look round. She left her coffee on the bench beside him, with the ice melting in it.
She did not tell him that she had asked herself the same question every midnight since the roof. Do you believe you are a good person?
She did not have an answer either.

## X. Arc 10 — Dan (Part 3)
@pov Dan

### The Answer He Brought

It took Dan three days.
He went to class. He took notes he did not read. He walked past the bench in the courtyard twice a day and did not look at it. It was empty every time.
At lunch he sat at the end of the table by the window and watched the four of them eat and argue about nothing. Asa wanted to try the new noodle place by the market. Jess said it was too far to walk in this heat. Zen said he did not mind either way, which made both of them cross with him. Delta ate her rice and said nothing, and did not look at Dan once.
All the while, he turned her question over.
Do you believe you are a good person?
The first night, he told himself that of course he was.
He lay on his bed in the dark and made a list, the way he used to make lists before exams. He had tried to save them in every life. He had changed the trip from Siem Reap to the sea. He had cancelled the trip altogether. He had kept them on campus. He had checked the wiring on the grill, and latched the window, and put a bucket of water by the door. He had stood under their windows at night until the lights went out. He had stopped eating until he saw them eat. Nobody else had done any of that. Nobody else had even known there was anything to do.
By the end of the list he felt better. He fell asleep with it still in his head.
The second night, he made the same list again, and it started to sound different.
He had kept them on campus. He had not asked them. He had stood under their windows at night. Jess had looked out once and seen him there, and gone white, and pulled the curtain shut. Asa had stopped him in the corridor and said you’re scaring her, you’re scaring me, and he had not been able to answer. Zen had said talk to me, whatever it is, and Dan had looked him in the face and said he was fine.
He had never once asked any of them what they wanted. Not in any life. He had decided what was safe for them, and made them do it, and called that love.
He did not fall asleep easily that night.
The third night, he did not make a list at all.
He lay on his back and thought about a beach, and about the weeks after it. About waiting outside Zen’s door in the corridor at night, so that he could say it again. About his own voice saying you chose, you chose, you chose to a boy who was already on his knees. He thought about Zen’s face when he said it, the way it had gone very still and then very far away.
He had wanted to hurt him. He remembered that clearly, and he made himself keep remembering it. In that moment, on that beach and in that corridor, he had wanted to hurt Zen more than he had wanted anything. Because if it was Zen’s fault, then it was not his.
He did not sleep at all that night. He sat on the floor with his back against the bed until the window went grey.
On the fourth morning, she was on the bench again, in the sun, with a cup of iced coffee beside her, as if she had never left it.
He stopped at the edge of the courtyard when he saw her. For a moment he thought about walking on, the way he had for three days. Then he made himself cross the grass.
He sat down.
For a while he did not say anything. He watched the light on the flower bed. A gardener was watering the far end of it with a hose, and the water made small rainbows in the air. He had practised what he was going to say all night, sentence by sentence, and now he could not remember any of it.
“No,” he said.
Delta did not turn her head.
“No?”
“I killed Zen.”
It was the first time he had said it aloud, in any life. His voice came out rough, and too loud, and he made himself go on.
“Once with a knife. I didn’t mean that one. He was trying to take it off me.” He swallowed. “And once with words. After the sea. I said it to him every day for weeks, until he believed it. I meant every single one of those.”
He looked down at his hands. They were shaking again. He pressed them flat against his knees until they stopped.
“I told myself I was the one trying to save them,” he said. “The good one. The only one who cared enough to try. Every life, I told myself that.” He was quiet for a moment. “But I don’t think that’s what it was. I think I just couldn’t stand to lose them. I couldn’t stand being the one who’s left behind. So I held on, and I held on, and every time I held on harder, somebody died.”
He swallowed again.
“That isn’t the same thing as saving someone. Is it.”
Delta was quiet for a long time. A leaf came down out of the tree behind them, turning over and over, and landed on the bench between them. She did not brush it off. She looked at it.
“No,” she said at last. “It isn’t.”
Then, more quietly, still looking at the leaf: “But that’s a start. Most people never get that far.”
Dan let out a breath he had not known he was holding. It did not feel like being forgiven. It felt like putting down something very heavy, and finding out how much his arms hurt.
“Then tell me the rest.” His voice was steadier now. “I changed the place. I changed the day. I cancelled everything. I kept them in one room. Every time, I did more. And every time, they still died. Why can’t I stop it?”
“You changed the place,” Delta said. “You never changed the people.”
“What does that mean?”
“It means you were guarding the wrong thing.” She picked up her cup and turned it a little in the light. “You guard the door, Dan. Every time. You watch for the stranger, the driver, the wave, the fire. You look outside for the danger. You have never once looked at the table.”
“I look at them every day.”
“You look at them the way you look at something you’re afraid of dropping. You check that they’re there. You count them. One, two, three, four.” She said it gently, but she did not stop. “You see where they are. You never see what they’re doing to each other.”
Dan opened his mouth to argue, and stopped.
He was thinking about the bus. He had watched the driver’s hands for two hours. He had watched every passenger who got on. And in the seat right in front of him, Asa and Jess had been tearing each other apart in low voices, and he had decided it was about plans for the break, and looked back at the road.
He was thinking about the party. He had checked the wiring on the grill, and latched the window, and put a bucket of water by the door. He had been watching for a fire. He had not been watching the five people in the room.
She was right. He had been so busy guarding them from the world that he had never once looked at them.
A bicycle went past. Somebody laughed by the cafeteria doors.
“Tragedy doesn’t walk in from outside,” she said. “It’s never that large. It’s a step. A glance. A sentence somebody almost doesn’t say. Somebody wanting, very badly, to be looked at.”
“Who?”
“If I tell you, you’ll guard that one, and miss the next.”
“Then what do I do?”
Delta looked at him at last.
“Nothing. For once. Don’t save anyone. Just watch.”
Dan felt his chest go cold.
“You’re asking me to let them die.”
“They have died every time you held on,” she said gently. “I’m asking you to see it. Once.”
He wanted to refuse. Every life he had lived was a refusal of exactly this.
“And you?” he said instead. “Do you have one too? A dark side you hide?”
She picked up her coffee.
“Twenty-five percent,” she said. “On a good day.”

### The Table

A few days later, at lunch, Asa brought up the trip.
Dan knew it was coming. It always came, on this day or the next, in every life. He felt it coming the way you feel a wave pulling back before it breaks.
“Okay. Spring break. I’ve decided.” Asa slapped both hands flat on the table so that the cups jumped. “The sea. A cottage. Five days of doing absolutely nothing. No lectures, no assignments, no Dan telling us to go to bed. Who’s in?”
Jess laughed and said yes at once. Delta put down her spoon.
“No,” Zen said.
He said it too fast. It came out loud, louder than he meant, and the table went quiet. Everyone looked at him.
He looked down at his tray. His ears went red. “Sorry,” he said. “I just—not the sea. We shouldn’t go. Anywhere else.”
Asa stared at him. “Since when do you hate the sea?”
Zen did not answer.
Dan saw his face, and understood.
Zen remembered the sea. Not a feeling, not a bad dream. All of it. Dan could see it in the way his hands had gone still on the tray, and the way his eyes went, just for a second, to Delta, as if to check that she was really there. The beach. Delta going under. Six days of boats and helicopters. The man from the coast guard with his cap in his hands. Jess in a blanket on the sand. And a voice in the corridor, every day for weeks, telling him you chose.
Dan understood what that meant. When time turned back, the sea loop had been wiped away for everyone else. For Jess and Asa and Delta, it had never happened at all. But Zen still had it, every day of it, the same way Dan did.
He had watched a girl drown. Then he had woken up in March and found her sitting at their table, alive, and nobody else remembered a thing. He must have thought he was going mad. And he had not said a word about it to anyone. Not even to Dan. If Zen had told anyone, it would have been Dan.
Then Dan understood the rest of it, and it went through him like cold water.
Zen remembered the corridor, too. He remembered Dan standing outside his door at night, saying it was his fault. He remembered every word. And for weeks now, in this life, he had been sitting across the table from Dan every day, eating lunch with him, walking back to the dorms with him, knowing exactly what Dan had said to him in a life that only the two of them had lived. And he had never once said anything about it.
And it had not started today.
Now that Dan knew to look, he went back over the last two lives and saw it everywhere. In the bus life, when Asa had suggested the sea and Dan had said no, Zen had let out a breath, very quietly, as if he had been holding it. In the roof life, when Dan was falling apart, Zen had come to him and said, Talk to me. Whatever it is. I’m not going to laugh. He had said it like someone who was carrying something of his own that he could not explain.
It had been coming up in Zen for two whole lives, Dan thought, a little clearer each time, like something rising slowly through deep water. In the bus life it had only been a dread he could not name. By the roof, it had been something he could almost say. Now it was all there.
