And Dan had never seen any of it. In every other life, he had been the one to say no to the sea, loudly, before anyone else could speak. This was the first time he had kept quiet long enough for Zen to say it himself.
Then Dan did the hardest thing he had ever done.
Every part of him wanted to agree with Zen. Yes. Not the sea. Never the sea. In every life before this one, that was exactly what he would have done. He would have fought. He would have argued for the safest place he could think of, or cancelled the trip, or found a reason to keep them all inside. He would have taken the choice out of their hands, the way he always did, and told himself it was for their own good.
He could feel the words in his mouth, ready.
He did not say them.
He made himself breathe out, slowly, and say something ordinary instead.
“If not the sea,” he said, “we could go camping in the mountains.”
Zen’s head came up. He stared at Dan across the table.
Dan knew what he was seeing. The Dan who had begged him not to go to the sea. The Dan who had said it isn’t safe and I changed it and what if the outcome is the same. That Dan would never have suggested another trip, anywhere, ever.
Dan made himself meet his eyes. Calmly. He held them for a long moment, and did not look away first.
Asa was staring at him too. “You? Camping? You don’t even like walking to the bus stop.”
“People change,” Dan said.
Jess laughed. Asa said fine, the mountains, but if a single spider got into her tent she was going home. Zen looked down at his tray again, and slowly his shoulders came down. Not the sea. That was enough for him.
At the other end of the table, Delta did not look up from her rice. But Dan saw her spoon stop, for one second, halfway to her mouth. Then it went on.
He chose the mountains because he had never been there, in any life. He had no memory of them at all. He did not know which path was steep, or which river was deep, or who might be waiting in the trees. He would not know where the danger was, so he could not guard against it. He would have to do what Delta had told him to do.
Nothing. Just watch.
And for the two weeks before the trip, that was what he did. He watched the table.
It was the hardest work he had ever done. Every time one of them crossed a road, his whole body wanted to grab them. Every time Jess picked up a knife to cut her food, he wanted to take it from her. He made himself sit still, and keep his hands under the table, and look.
And for the first time, he saw.
He saw Asa laugh with Delta. He had seen that in many lives. This time he saw the rest of it. Every time Asa laughed, her eyes went to Jess, quickly, to see whether Jess was watching. When Jess was looking at Zen, Asa laughed louder. When Jess looked back at her, Asa looked away, and her whole face changed, just for a moment, before the smile came back.
He had always thought that glance was Asa being kind, checking that Jess was all right. It was not kindness. He did not know yet what it was. But it was not kindness.
He saw Jess. Quiet Jess, gentle Jess, stirring her drink. He saw her eyes follow Zen’s eyes to Delta, and come back smaller. He saw her move her chair an inch at a time over the course of one lunch, until it stood between them. He saw her drop a book at the exact moment Zen leaned over to hear what Delta had said, so that he had to stop and pick it up for her.
And he saw something stranger. He saw Jess catch Asa looking at her. Jess did not look away, and she did not look back. She only smiled a small, soft smile, as if she had been given something she was owed, and turned back to Zen.
Small things. So small that any one of them was nothing. A glance. A chair. A dropped book. A smile.
He saw Zen not seeing any of it. Zen looked at Delta the way he looked at a hard question in an exam, puzzled and careful and unable to stop. He had watched her drown. Now she was sitting across the table from him, eating rice, and he could not stop checking that she was still there. He was kind to Jess and kind to Asa and kind to Dan, and he did not notice a single thing that was happening around him. When somebody at the next table mentioned the beach, he went quiet for the rest of the meal, and Dan knew exactly why.
He saw Delta, too. She was watching them as well. Once she caught him watching, across the table, and held his eyes for a moment. Then she gave him the smallest nod, and went back to her rice.
And last of all, he saw himself. Dan, at the end of the table, where he had sat for years and called it love. He had been looking at them all that time. Every day. Every life. He had counted them a thousand times.
He had not seen one thing.

### The Step

They took a bus up into the hills early in the morning, with their tents and sleeping bags piled on the seats behind them.
On the bus, Asa sat with Delta. Jess sat beside Zen, who put his earphones in and fell asleep before they had even left the town. Dan sat across the aisle from her. He watched her look out of the window at the fields going past, and then at Zen asleep beside her, and then out of the window again.
He peeled an orange and held half of it out to her without saying anything. She looked surprised. Then she took it, and gave him a small smile. Later he saw her slip the peel into her pocket instead of throwing it away. He did not know why. He did not ask.
From the last stop, they walked up the mountain along a trail through the pine forest. The air was cool and smelled of resin. Asa sang badly for the first hour and refused to stop, even when Zen begged her. Jess walked beside her, and on the steep parts Asa held her hand and pulled her up. Zen and Delta walked a little ahead, arguing about whether you could ever really know what another person was thinking. Dan walked at the back, where he could see all of them.
He kept his hands in his pockets the whole way.
It was harder than he had expected. The trail was steeper than any of them had thought, and every time it went close to an edge, his whole body wanted to run forward and grab someone by the arm. Halfway up, the loose stones slid under Jess’s shoe. Dan had already taken two steps toward her before he stopped himself. But it was Delta who caught her, by the elbow. “Careful,” Delta said, and let go as soon as Jess was steady, and walked on. Jess stood there for a second, looking after her. Dan made himself put his hands back in his pockets, and keep walking, and look.
He looked at the way Jess kept glancing ahead at Zen and Delta, and the way her laugh went thin each time she did. He looked at the way Asa’s hand stayed on Jess’s a moment longer than it needed to, after the steep part was over. He looked at Zen, who turned round now and then to check on all of them, and whose eyes always went to Delta last, and stayed there longest.
They reached the clearing near the top in the late afternoon. This was where they were going to camp. There was soft ground covered in pine needles, and a fallen log, and a view down the whole valley between the trees. Zen and Dan fought with the tent poles for half an hour, and Asa took pictures of them losing. For a while, it was exactly the kind of day it was supposed to be.
Then Asa hooked her arm through Jess’s and turned her toward the view. “See? Who needs the sea.” The whole valley lay below them, turning gold.
Dan noticed how quiet it was. There were no other campers. No voices on the trail. He realised he had not heard a bird in a long time. Delta had noticed it too. She was standing at the far side of the clearing, not looking at the view but at the trees, very still. When Asa called her over to help with the fire, she looked at the trees a moment longer before she came.
The sun was setting when the three men came out of the trees, not from the trail, shouting. “Hands up!”
They were not much older than Dan. Their clothes were dirty, and one of them had no shoes. The youngest was thin, and he held a gun out in front of him with both hands, as if he was afraid of it. His hands were shaking so badly that the end of it wandered back and forth across the five of them. Dan could see it in his face. He was more frightened than any of them.
Asa grabbed Jess’s hand. Zen stepped slightly forward. Delta did not move at all.
“Phones. Money. Now.”
They all did as they were told. One of the men went down the line with an open sack. Jess’s hands trembled as she handed over her bag, with her phone in it, and the orange peel. Dan dropped his wallet in without a word.
It should have ended there. The other two men were already backing toward the trees with the sack.
But the one with the gun did not move. His eyes kept jumping from one face to the next, as if he could not make himself believe it was over.
Dan’s whole body knew what to do.
He had lived enough lives, and seen enough frightened people, to know what a frightened hand does with a gun. He could have stepped forward, slowly, with his own hands up. He could have spoken to the boy in the right voice, calm and low. It’s okay. You’ve got what you came for. Go. He could have told the others to sit down and look at the ground. He had a dozen lives of practice at saving people. Every part of him was already leaning forward.
“Don’t move!” the boy shouted. His voice cracked on it. “Nobody move!”
Dan didn’t move.
It was the hardest thing he had ever done. Harder than the bus. Harder than the roof. Harder than keeping quiet at the lunch table. He stood in the clearing with his hands at his sides and his heart going so hard that he could hear it in his ears, and he did nothing at all.
Just watch, Delta had said.
He watched.
Zen stepped in front of Delta.
He did it without thinking. Dan saw that. One moment Zen was standing beside her, and the next he was between her and the gun, with one arm held a little out from his side, the way you put your arm across someone when a car brakes too hard. He did not look round to see where anyone else was. He did not need to. He had already decided, somewhere far below thinking, who he would stand in front of.
Jess saw it.
She had been staring at the ground, at her own shoes and the pine needles. Then she looked up, only for a second, to find Zen. Dan was watching her face, the way Delta had told him to, and he saw it cross her face: the same small shrinking he had watched at the table for two weeks, only much worse. Zen had not stepped in front of her. He had not even looked to see where she was. He had chosen, without a second’s thought, and he had not chosen her.
Asa saw it too.
Dan watched her see it. Asa was still holding Jess’s hand, and she saw where Jess was looking, and what Jess was feeling. Something went across Asa’s face, hot and quick, the same look Dan had seen at the lunch table every time Zen let go of Jess’s hand. Asa’s fingers tightened. She held on harder. She did not want to let go. Not now. Not for him.
Jess did not care.
She shook her hand out of Asa’s, hard, the way you shake off something that is holding you back. She did not even look at Asa while she did it.
And then she moved.
It was not a careful movement. It was not a thought at all. It was jealousy, all of it at once, everything she had been holding down for four months, taking over her body for one second. She pushed forward across the clearing, fast, into the gap between Zen and Delta, so that she was the one standing pressed against Zen’s arm. So that he would have to feel her there. So that he would have to turn round.
It was a big movement, sudden and quick, in a clearing where nobody else had dared to breathe.
The boy with the gun saw it.
His shaking hands swung toward the movement, fast, the way a frightened animal turns toward a noise. Asa screamed Jess’s name.
The shot was very loud. It went up off the hills and came back.
Jess fell. Delta fell beside her.
For a moment nobody moved at all. Then the three men were running, crashing back into the trees, shouting at each other. Nobody went after them.
Nobody had pushed Jess. There had been no wave. No bus. No stranger in a van. No fate.
There had been a step.
Zen had Delta in his arms before the echo was gone. He was saying her name. Then he was up on his feet with her, running back down the trail toward the road and the village, shouting for help, and he did not look back once.
Asa made a sound Dan had never heard come out of her. It was not a scream. It was lower than that. She started toward Jess, and her knees went.
Dan’s arms caught her before he had decided anything.
He knew why, even as it happened. There was a second on a beach that he had never stopped counting. One second, standing between Asa and the water, deciding. He had turned toward the sea that time, and the roof had come down on her. His body had been waiting many lives to take that second back. Now it had, without asking him.
So that was his own small movement. He held Asa up while she shook, and he understood that he was no different from any of them. Zen had chosen Delta. Jess had chosen to be seen. And Dan had chosen Asa, with his arms, before his head could stop him.
Over Asa’s shoulder, he could see Jess.
She lay on her back on the pine needles, with her eyes open, looking up at the sky through the tops of the trees. Her hand was still turned a little toward the place where Zen had been standing.
Dan had let it happen. He had stood still, the way he had promised, and watched, and seen everything he had never seen before. And Jess had died anyway, of one rush of jealousy, one step across a clearing, that he could have stopped with a single word.
He would have to carry that. It was in the glass now, the bitter part, and it was not coming out.

### The Door

Zen reached the road with Delta in his arms just as it got dark. A man in a pickup truck stopped for him, and drove them down the mountain to the district hospital with his hazard lights flashing the whole way.
Asa had fainted in Dan’s arms. He laid her down on the pine needles, with his rolled-up jacket under her head, and checked that she was breathing. She was. She did not wake.
Then there was nothing to do but wait.
Dan sat in the clearing between the two of them, Asa on one side and Jess on the other, and did not move. It was getting cold. The stars came out over the valley one by one. For a long time nobody came. Then there were torches on the trail, and voices, and men from the village with two stretchers, and a policeman who kept asking Dan questions he could not answer.
It was after midnight when they reached the district hospital at the bottom of the mountain.
Delta was already in surgery. Zen was sitting on the floor outside the doors, with his back against the wall, still in his torn shirt, with dirt and pine needles on his trousers. He did not look up when Dan came in.
For Jess, there was nothing anyone could do. A doctor with tired eyes told Dan so, in a flat, careful voice. Then he told him that Asa was only in shock and would sleep it off, and that the girl in surgery was going to live. He said all three things in the same voice, one after the other, as if they were the same kind of news.
They gave Asa a bed in a small room at the end of the corridor. Somebody folded her jacket over the back of the chair. There were still pine needles caught in the sleeve.
Dan sat down in the chair beside her bed.
He sat there all night while she slept, and went over everything one more time. And this time, at last, he understood it all the way through.
It had never been the place. Not Siem Reap, not the sea, not the bus, not the roof, not the mountain. It had been the five of them, all along. Each of them hiding one bitter part, one quarter of the glass, and smiling over the top of it.
Jess’s jealousy. It had walked her out of a club into a dark lane. It had stepped her back into deep water. It had screamed on a bus. It had pushed her across a clearing toward a gun.
Asa’s secret. It had come out in the water, and on a bus, and on a roof, every time at exactly the wrong moment.
Zen’s mistake about his own heart. He had called what he felt for Jess love, and so he had never understood why she hurt, and he had never understood what he felt for Delta until it was too late. Tonight he had chosen without thinking, and Jess had seen him choose.
And Dan’s own. His need to hold on. One second on a beach. Weeks of blame in a corridor. A pair of arms that caught Asa instead of Jess.
And Delta, sitting quietly among the four of them, not understanding any of it, and somehow always in the middle.
You could not guard against that from outside. You could change the place a hundred times, and the same five people would carry the same five things into it, hidden, and those things would come out at the worst possible moment, the way they always did. In a club. In the sea. On a roof. In a clearing at sunset.
The only way to stop it was to start again, knowing exactly where to look. Not at the road. At the table.
And this time, Dan thought, he would not wait for the bitter parts to come out on their own. He would bring them out himself. Somewhere safe. Somewhere with no water and no roof and no strangers. He would make all five of them say it, out loud, to each other’s faces, before it could kill anyone.
Once more. The last time.
It had to be the last time. He knew he could not do this again. He did not think there was enough of him left.
And there was only one way to get back to March.
Zen had to die.
Dan sat very still in the plastic chair and let himself think it, clearly, all the way to the end. He did not look away from it. His best friend, who had carried a girl down a mountain in the dark, who was sitting ten metres away holding her hand. Zen had to die, and it had to be soon, and Dan was going to have to be the one who made it happen.
Then he got up and went to find a coffee from the machine at the end of the corridor, and his hands did not shake at all. That frightened him more than anything else that night.
When he came back, the window was going grey, and Asa was awake.
She lay looking at the ceiling. The light was so dull that it could have been morning or evening.
“You fainted,” Dan said softly, and sat down again.
Asa sat up slowly. She put a hand to her head.
“Where is everyone?”
“Delta’s out of surgery. They say she’ll be all right. Zen is with her.”
He stopped there.
“And Jess?”
Dan hesitated. He had known this question was coming all night. He still had no way to say it.
“Jess is dead.”
Asa stared at him. “No.”
Dan looked down. “They couldn’t save her.”
For a long time the room was silent. Asa looked at her own hand lying on the blanket, as if she could still feel something in it.
Then she said, “Why did you help me?”
Dan made his face look confused. “You fainted.”
“Jess needed help more than me.” Her voice was very quiet and very steady, which was worse than crying. “She was right there, Dan.”
Dan’s fingers tightened on his knees.
“I… didn’t think,” he said.
It was the last lie he bothered with.
He could see that Asa did not believe him. She had spent her whole life saying things she did not mean in a voice that sounded as if she did. She knew exactly what it sounded like. But she said nothing. She only looked at him a moment longer, and then turned her face to the wall.
They buried Jess four days later, in her mother’s village. There were white flowers, and a monk, and a great many people Dan did not know. Jess’s mother held on to Asa’s arm through the whole thing, as if Asa were the only thing keeping her standing. Delta was not well enough to come. Zen stood at the very back, and left before the end.
He came to find Dan the day after, on the back stairs behind the library. He did not sit down. He stood over him, with his hands shaking at his sides.
“Why did you help Asa instead of Jess?”
Dan looked up at him calmly. “For the same reason you helped Delta.”
He watched it land. He watched Zen understand what that meant, and go white.
“You remember too,” Zen said.
Dan nodded.
“Then why take us there? Why the mountains? You knew. You must have known something would happen.”
“You wouldn’t understand,” Dan said.
It was true. There was no way to explain it that would not end with the words I need you to die. So he did not explain. He started instead.
“You left her.”
He had said things like this to Zen before, in another life, after the sea. That time he had said them out of rage, because he could not stand his own guilt and needed somewhere to put it. This time he was not angry at all. He chose every word. He knew exactly where the bruise was, because he had made it himself once already.
“I was getting help,” Zen said. His voice was hoarse. “Delta was bleeding, I had to—”
“You chose.”
“I didn’t choose anything. I didn’t see—”
“She was your girlfriend. She was right there.” Dan did not raise his voice. He never raised it, this time. “You could have saved her.”
Zen did not answer. He turned and went down the stairs, fast. Dan let him go. There would be tomorrow, and the day after.
“You knew the future. You remembered the sea. You still couldn’t change it.”
“People died because of you. Again.”
He said it on the stairs. He said it in the corridor outside the lecture hall. He said it quietly, in a reasonable voice, as if he were only pointing out the facts. He watched each sentence go into Zen and stay there.
Doing it calmly was worse than doing it in anger. He knew that. He did it anyway.
Asa tried to stop him once. She caught him alone outside the lecture hall after the last class. “Dan… maybe you should stop.” He looked at her calmly, with the same gentle face as always, and said, “Truth shouldn’t be comfortable.” Then he picked up his bag and walked away. He was not sure, even as he said it, which of the two of them he was saying it to.
Delta said nothing. She did not try to stop him. But she had started sitting where she could see Zen in every room.
One afternoon Dan caught her watching Zen across the lecture hall. She had one hand pressed flat under her ribs, and she was frowning slightly, like someone trying to work out where a pain was coming from.
She did not look sad. She looked puzzled, as if the pain did not make sense to her.
Then she noticed Dan looking. She took her hand away, and met his eyes, and held them. He could not read her face at all. She did not stop him. He never found out why.
A month later, Zen’s seat was empty.
Dan sat at the back of the lecture hall, looking out of the window at the courtyard. The restlessness had gone out of him. He felt very calm, and very tired, and very far away from everything. He thought of the bench in the sun, and the cup of coffee with the ice melting in it, and her question.
Do you believe you are a good person?
No.
But one more time.
The room faded.
White ceiling.
12:00 AM. March twenty-first.
Dan did not smirk this time.
“The last one,” he said.

## XI. Arc 11 — The Last Loop

### Dan’s Room
@pov Dan

**Part One — Dan**

Dan woke on March twenty-first for the last time.
He did not refuse any trips. He did not follow anyone home. He did not stand under their windows.
He had learned, on the mountain, what killed them. It was never the place. It was what each of the five of them carried and did not say.
Asa’s love for Jess. Jess’s jealousy. Zen’s mistake about his own heart.
And the other two as well. His own obsession with keeping all of them, which had dragged them through one death after another. And Delta’s ignorance. She could see everything people did, but she did not understand human feelings well enough to know where they would lead, and she had sat down at their table anyway.
Every time, those five things had come out at the worst possible moment. In the sea. In a club. On a roof.
So this time he would make them come out at the best possible moment. In daylight. Sober. With all of them sitting down.
And in private. These were private things. They did not belong in a cafeteria with two hundred people listening.
He asked the four of them to come to his dorm room on a Sunday afternoon. He did not say why. He had never invited them anywhere before, so they came.
It was a small room. Zen took the desk chair. Jess and Asa sat on the bed. Delta sat on the floor by the window, in the sun. Dan had made tea. There was no alcohol in the room. He had checked twice.
He closed the door.
“I need to say something,” he said.
All four of them looked at him. In two years, Dan had hardly said ten sentences in a row.
“We’re not all right,” he said. “None of us. We keep pretending, and it’s going to hurt someone. So I’m going to say what I’ve seen. And then I’m going to ask you to say it yourselves.”
“I’ll go first,” he said. “I watch all of you too closely. I count you. I follow you. I’ve been so afraid of losing any of you that I stopped treating you like people who get to choose for themselves. That’s an obsession. It isn’t love, or it isn’t only love. It’s mine, and I’m sorry.”
Asa laughed. It was the bright laugh, the one she used on strangers.
“Wow. Okay. Dan’s having a moment. Does somebody want to get him some water?”
“I’m not finished.” He turned to her. His heart was going very fast. “You put a lemonade on Jess’s side of the table every day before she gets here. You’ve done it for two years. And since Delta came, you laugh louder whenever Jess is close enough to hear.”
Asa’s smile stayed on her face a second too long.
“So I buy my friend a drink. Arrest me.”
“Tell her, Asa.”
“Tell her what?” She was still smiling. But she had gone white. “You’re being weird, Dan. You’ve been weird for weeks. Everyone’s noticed.”
“Tell her why you sat next to her on the first day.”
“Stop it.”
“Tell her why you look at the window every time she looks at Zen.”
“I said stop.”
The smile was gone. Her voice dropped, low and furious.
“You don’t get to do this. You don’t get to sit in the corner for two years, saying nothing, watching everybody, and then decide one day to shut us in a room and take people apart. Who asked you? Who do you think you are?”
“Asa,” Jess said. “What is he talking about?”
“Nothing. He’s talking about nothing.” Asa grabbed her bag. Her hands were shaking so badly that she dropped it. “I’m going. I have things to do.”
“If you go,” Dan said, “you’ll end up telling her some other way. Somewhere worse. And it will cost more than you can imagine. I’m begging you. Here. Now.”
She stood there, halfway to the door. He had never seen her frightened before. She was looking at him as if he had put a knife on the table.
“You can’t know that,” she whispered. “Nobody knows. I never told anyone.”
“Asa?” Jess said again, in a smaller voice.
Asa looked at her.
Dan watched her try to do it the way she did everything. Lightly. With a joke. Her mouth opened, and nothing light came out. Two years of it were in her face, and she could not smile over it anymore.
“I’m in love with you,” she said.
It came out loud, and cracked. It filled the small room.
“Okay? There. I’m in love with you. Not like a friend. Since the first day. I asked to borrow your pen, and I had three in my bag. I know how you take your lemonade. I know you bow your head when you say hello. I’ve watched you look at him for two years, and every single time I made a joke, so that nobody would see what it did to me.”
She was crying now, angrily, wiping her face with the back of her hand.
“And I used her.” She jerked her head toward Delta. “I sat with her and laughed with her so that you’d finally look at me the way you look at him. It was a rotten thing to do. I knew it was rotten, and I did it anyway. Because I was so tired of being invisible to you when I was sitting right there.”
Jess had not moved. She looked as if she had been slapped.
“You made me jealous on purpose?”
“Yes.”
“I cried about that. I thought I was losing you. I thought I wasn’t enough for you anymore. And you were doing it on purpose?”
“I wanted you to feel one tenth of what I felt!” Asa shouted.
Then her face crumpled.
“I’m sorry. I’m sorry. That’s the truth, and it’s ugly.”
Nobody in the room moved. The tea was going cold.
“I can’t,” Jess said at last. Her voice was shaking. “Asa. I can’t love you like that. I’m sorry. You’re my best friend. You’re the only person who ever sat down next to me. But not like that.”
Asa nodded. She had known. Dan could see that she had known for two years what the answer would be. It was the reason she had never asked.
“I know,” she said. She sat down again, slowly, like someone very tired. “God. I know.”
Then she looked up at Jess.
“And you knew too. Didn’t you?”
Jess opened her mouth. She closed it again.
“You knew,” Asa said. Her voice had gone very quiet, which was worse than the shouting. “Every time I looked at you. Every seat I saved. Every time I walked you home. You knew, and you let me. You let me sit there loving you for two years, because it was nice to have someone who would.”
“That’s not—” Jess’s voice broke. “You’re my friend. You’re my friend. I didn’t want to lose you.”
“So you kept me.”
Jess did not answer. That was the answer.
Dan saw it then, and could not believe he had missed it for so long. Jess had never pretended to love Asa back. But she had never let her go, either. She had kept her close the way she kept everything she was afraid of losing, quietly, without ever asking what it cost.
Dan made himself go on. He turned to Jess. This was the one he was most afraid of.
“Jess. You’ve been jealous of Delta since the day she came.”
“Don’t.” Jess rounded on him. “Don’t you start on me. Haven’t you done enough?”
“You have been. I’ve watched you move your chair. Say it, so it stops growing.”
“You want me to say it?”
Her hands were shaking around her glass. For a moment Dan thought she would run, and his whole body got ready to go after her.
She did not run. She turned to Delta instead, and her gentle face was not gentle at all.
“I hate you. Is that what he wants? I hate you. You walked in here and took everything, and you didn’t even try. My friend. My seat. Him. Sometimes I think about you just being gone. I lie in bed and think about it. I know that’s horrible. I know you helped me. I still think it.”
“I know,” Delta said quietly.
Jess stared at her. “You knew?”
“Yes.”
Delta put down her cup.
“I should say mine too. I saw all of this in the first week. Asa using me. You hating me. I can see what people do. I don’t always understand what they feel, or what it will turn into. I didn’t know it would hurt you this much. I stayed anyway, because I wanted to be here. That was my part.”
“That’s worse,” Jess said. “That is so much worse. You knew, and you kept sitting there being nice to me.”
Then Zen spoke, without being asked. He had been looking at Jess the whole time. He looked ill.
“It’s my turn, isn’t it.”
“No,” Jess said quickly. “No. Not you. Don’t.”
She knew. Some part of her had always known what he would say.
He took her hand. He was gentle about it, the way he always was.
“I care about you more than almost anyone. I want you safe. I want you happy. I thought that was what love was. I really did.” He swallowed. “But I think it’s how you love a sister. I didn’t know there was a difference. I’m so sorry. I should have known before I asked you.”
Jess pulled her hand away as if it had burned her.
“A sister.” She laughed, and the laugh turned into a sob. “I told you I loved you every day. Every single day. And you let me.”
“I thought I did too. I swear to you. I thought that was what it was.”
“Then what was I for?”
“You were the person I most wanted to be okay,” Zen said. “You still are.”
“I’m not okay!”
She was on her feet. Her cup went over and spilled across the floor.
“None of this is okay! You’ve all been lying to me. All of you. For years.” She looked round the table at the four of them, and her face twisted. “And so have I.”
She pulled the door open and ran. They heard her footsteps going away down the corridor.
Dan was on his feet.
Then he sat down again.
It was the hardest thing he had ever done. He let her go, and he did not know if she would be alive in the morning.

### Four
@pov Dan

Jess was alive in the morning.
Dan had not stood under her window. He had not waited outside her door. For the first time in more lives than he could count, he had gone back to his own room, and lain down on his bed, and let the night happen without him. He had not slept. But he had stayed where he was.
In the morning he saw her crossing the courtyard with her bag on her shoulder and her eyes swollen, walking very fast, looking at nobody. Alive. He stood at the window and watched her until she was out of sight, and then he sat down on the floor, because his legs would not hold him.
She had cried all night in her room. Everyone on her corridor had heard it. Asa had not knocked. But late that night she had left a bowl of soup outside Jess’s door, the way the three of them used to when one of them was ill. Jess had not opened the door while Asa was there. But when Asa came back for the bowl in the morning, it was empty, and it had been washed, and put back exactly where she had left it.
Nobody said anything about a trip. Nobody suggested the sea, or the mountains, or Siem Reap. The day when they would have left came, and went, like any other day. It rained a little in the afternoon.
Nobody died.
Dan woke up the next morning and looked at the date on his phone for a long time. It was a date he had never seen. Then there was another one. Then another. The calendar went on into April, and then into a month he had no memory of at all, and every morning the world was still there.
But the table by the window was empty.
Jess sat on the far side of the cafeteria now, by herself, with a book open in front of her that she did not read. She nodded to Asa when they passed in the corridor. She could not look at Zen at all. When he came into a room, she found a reason to leave it.
Asa ate with people from her class, and laughed at their jokes, and her eyes were tired. She no longer tried to hide that they were tired. Sometimes Dan saw her alone on the back stairs, not crying, just sitting, with her phone face down on her knee.
Zen ate standing up, or not at all. He went to his lectures and sat at the back and did not speak. He looked like someone who was waiting for something, and did not know what.
Delta sat in the sun on the bench in the courtyard, every day, alone, and did not come into the cafeteria.
They were not enemies. Nobody hated anybody. They were only five people who now knew too much about each other to sit down at the same table and pretend they did not.
Dan still counted them every day, out of habit. He could not stop. One, two, three, four. All alive. In four different places.
He had wanted to save all of them and keep all of them. Every life, he had tried to have both. He understood now that he could only ever have had the first. Saving them had meant letting them go. Letting them say the bitter things out loud, and walk away from each other, and be angry, and be honest, and be alive.
It was not what he had wanted. It was nowhere near what he had wanted.
He found, to his surprise, that he could live with it.

### What Delta Told Him
@pov Dan

A week after the day of the trip, Delta was waiting for him on the bench in the courtyard, in the sun.
She did not wave him over. She did not need to. He had known for days that she was waiting for him, the same way he had known, in another life, that she would be on that bench the morning after the roof.
He sat down beside her.
“You did it,” she said.
Dan looked across the courtyard at the cafeteria doors. Through the glass he could see the table by the window, with nobody at it.
“It isn’t what I wanted.”
“No,” Delta said. “But they’re alive. And they’re honest with each other, for the first time. That’s more sweet than bitter.”
She did not have a coffee with her. He noticed that at once. In every life, on every morning he had found her on this bench, there had been a plastic cup beside her with the ice melting in it. Today there was nothing. Her hands were empty in her lap.
“I’m leaving,” she said.
He turned to look at her.
“I came to find out what it was like to have friends,” she said. She said it plainly, the way she said everything. “I had watched the four of you for three hundred lives, and I did not understand what you were holding on to. So I sat down at your table to find out. And I did find out. It was the best thing that has ever happened to me, in four hundred years.”
She looked down at her empty hands.
“And then I broke it. Just by being there. Jess saw Zen look at me. Asa used me to make Jess jealous. Everything that was already hidden in the four of you came out faster, because I was sitting in the middle of it. Now there is no table any more. There is nothing left for me to sit down at.”
“That wasn’t your fault,” Dan said.
“It was partly my fault.” She said it calmly. “That’s all right. Someone taught me once that there is always some bitterness in the glass, and that the bitter part is still part of the coffee. You don’t have to pretend it isn’t there. You only have to say it.” She almost smiled. “It took me a very long time to learn how to say so.”
Then she told him everything.
She told it in order, plainly, without stopping, the way someone reads out a list they have known by heart for years. Who she was. How old she was. The laboratory under the ground in the hills, where she had been kept for four hundred years with hundreds of others like her. The tables. The white lights. The essences taken out of the dying, and the one taken out of her.
She told him about the night the lights went out, and the tunnel, and the sun. About a curse she had built in a hospital bed, and an old man who had sat down inside it and taught her how to drink coffee. About a shop by a road, and a boy who swept its floor.
She told him what Zen was. What his parents had made him to be. What was done to him on the table when he went home, every time, in every life Dan had never seen.
She told him about the chair. About the anchors, and the machine she had changed, and the three hundred and some times she had sat beside Zen and watched him die so that one more of her friends could be free. About the morning she had woken up and found only her own essence left inside him, and decided, for the first time in four hundred years, to want something for herself.
And she told him about the night in a lane in Siem Reap, and waking up in March, and finding out that someone else had taken hold of her spell. That the one who had kept time turning, all those lives, had been Dan.
It took almost the entire morning. Dan did not interrupt once. Somewhere in the middle of it, he realised he was holding on to the edge of the bench with both hands.
When she had finished, he sat for a long time without saying anything. Then he asked the only question he could think of.
“Zen remembers the sea,” he said. “Not the others. Only the sea. Why?”
“You remember everything because of what you wanted,” Delta said. “My spell listens to the person who pays for it. But it also listens to anyone inside it who wants something with their whole heart. You wanted all of us alive more than anyone has ever wanted anything. So the spell listened to you. And you had been wanting it for three hundred lives before you ever remembered one, every time you lost them. That kind of wanting does not vanish when time turns back. It stays, and it grows. In my other turns it did not matter, because my dead friends paid for every turn. But in my last loop nobody was left to pay, and by then your wanting was strong enough to turn time back by itself. That is how you took it over. That is why you remember every life since.”
“And Zen?”
“Zen didn’t want anything. With him it was different. It was how many times.” She looked down at her hands. “In my loops, the sea happened to him three hundred times. Always the same way. The beach. Someone lost in the water. Jess in his arms on the sand. Someone blaming him. Then it happened once more, in yours. When something happens to a person that many times, it wears into them. Time can turn back. But it cannot wipe out something that deep. Everything else only happened to him once. The sea happened three hundred times. So the sea is the only thing he kept.”
Dan thought about that. Then he said, “He remembers losing you.”
Delta looked up.
“He’s never said so,” Dan said. “But I’ve seen it on his face, every time someone says the word sea. In my sea loop, you were the one who drowned, Delta. You pushed Jess back to him, and then you went under. He watched it happen. He watched them search for you for six days, and then stop. He remembers all of it.”
Delta went very still.
“Me?” she said. “Are you sure it’s me he remembers? In all my loops, it was Asa who drowned. Not me. Never me.”
“It was you, that time.”
She was quiet for a moment. “You’re sure?”
“I’m sure, Delta. It was you he lost.”
She pressed one hand flat under her ribs. Dan had seen her do that once before, across a lecture hall, in the life after the mountain. She frowned at nothing, like someone trying to work out where a pain was coming from.
“I don’t understand why it hurts so much to hear that,” she said.
Dan thought he knew what it was. He thought it was the same thing that made Zen look at her across the table as if she were a question he could not stop trying to answer. But he did not say so. He did not think she would believe him. And he did not think it was his to say.
“Why are you telling me all this?” he asked instead. “Why me?”
“Because he will come and ask you where I went,” Delta said. “He will not stop until somebody tells him. When he does, tell him the truth. All of it. Then tell him to run. Tell him he must never go home. Not for his parents, not for anything.”
She looked across the courtyard toward the gate, as if she could already see the dark car waiting there.
“You let go of the loop,” she said. “Time will not turn back for you any more. And my spell can only finish one way, on that table. If he never goes home, it never finishes. My essence stays inside him, and he lives. That is the end I want.”
“And you?” Dan said.
“I’ll manage without it.”
She stood up. She looked at him for a moment.
“You asked me once to explain why you couldn’t save them. You were a good friend to them, Dan. You were also the reason it went on so long. Both.”
“I know,” he said.
She walked away across the courtyard. He watched until he couldn’t see her. He did not count.

### Where Did She Go
@pov Zen

**Part Two — Zen**

Delta’s seat was empty on Monday.
Zen noticed it the moment he walked into the lecture hall. He always noticed where she was. He had stopped pretending to himself that he didn’t on the night in Dan’s room, when he had told Jess that he loved her the way you love a sister, watched her face break, and understood at last what the other feeling was, the one he had for Delta.
Since that night he had not spoken to Delta once. He had not known how. He had sat at the back of every lecture and watched the side of her face, three rows ahead, and told himself that he would find the words tomorrow.
On Monday, her seat was empty. He told himself she was ill.
It was empty on Tuesday too. At lunch he went out to the courtyard, to the bench where she always sat in the sun. There was nobody on it. A plastic cup lay on its side underneath it, empty, with the straw still in it. He did not know whether it was hers.
He sat down on the bench for a while. It was warm from the sun. But the other warmth, the one he had felt every time he sat near her, the one he had never been able to explain, was not there. He had not known until it was gone how much he had come to depend on it.
On Wednesday he went to her dormitory. He climbed the stairs to the third floor and found her door. There was a different name on it, on a strip of white card, in someone else’s handwriting. A girl he did not know opened it, with a towel around her hair, and told him she had been in that room since the start of the year.
“But it’s Delta’s room,” Zen said. “I’ve been here. I’ve stood in this doorway.”
The girl looked at him as if he might be dangerous, and shut the door.
He went to the student office. The woman behind the desk typed the name into her computer. Then she typed it again, slowly, letter by letter, while he spelled it for her. She looked through a printed list. She looked through it twice.
“There’s no student here called Delta,” she said. “There never has been.”
Zen stood at the desk with both hands flat on it. For a moment he thought he was going mad again, the way he had thought it every morning since March. Then he thought, very clearly: no. She had been real. She had sat at their table. She had helped Jess with her mathematics and stood on the back stairs with Asa, and on the night in Dan’s room she had heard every word he said.
And Dan had known.
Zen had been thinking about it for days, without letting himself think it properly. On that night, Dan had known every one of their secrets. Asa’s. Jess’s. His own. Things none of them had ever said out loud to anyone. Dan had not guessed. He had known, the way you know something that has already happened.
If anyone knew where she had gone, it was Dan.
He found him in his room, sitting on the floor with his back against the bed, looking at nothing. Dan did not seem surprised to see him. He looked as if he had been waiting.
“Where did she go?” Zen said.
Dan looked at him for a long moment. Zen saw something in his face that he had never seen there before. It was not worry, or fear, or the restless watching that Dan had done for months. It was something calmer and much sadder than that, as if he had already made up his mind to do something very hard.
“Sit down,” Dan said. “This is going to take a while. And you’re not going to believe the first part.”

### What Dan Told Him
@pov Zen

Zen sat down on the edge of Dan’s bed. Dan stayed where he was, on the floor, with his back against the wall and his knees pulled up.
For a long time he did not say anything. He opened his mouth twice, and closed it again. Zen watched him rub his hands over his face, hard, the way he did before exams. It was the first time in months that Dan had looked frightened. Not watchful. Frightened.
“I’ve been trying to work out how to say this for a week,” Dan said. “I still don’t know how. So I’m just going to say it, and you’re going to think I’ve gone mad. Please just let me finish.”
“Okay.”
Dan took a breath.
“Delta isn’t a student. She was never a student. She isn’t human, Zen. She’s a witch. She’s more than four hundred years old.”
Zen laughed. It came out of him before he could stop it, short and shocked and too loud in the small room.
Dan didn’t laugh. He didn’t even look up. He just sat there on the floor with his arms around his knees and waited for Zen to stop.
The laugh died in Zen’s throat. Something in the way Dan was sitting made the back of his neck go cold.
“For four hundred years,” Dan said quietly, “she was kept in a laboratory under the ground. In the dark. There were hundreds of witches down there. Some of them had been there since before she was born. They kept them in cells, and they took them out one at a time, and cut them open, and put them back. Over and over. For years. To find out how their magic worked.” His voice shook, and he waited until it stopped. “Almost all of them died down there. She used to hear them through the walls.”
“Dan—”
“The laboratory is under your parents’ institute, Zen.”
Zen stopped.
“In the hills,” Dan said. “The place you go home to. Your parents run it now.”
Zen could not speak. He could not even breathe properly.
He thought of the institute. The long white building at the end of the road, behind the gate with the guard. The lift that went down further than the buttons showed. The doors he had never been allowed through when he was a child. He had pressed his face to the glass once, when he was very small, and his mother had picked him up and carried him away and said that’s just work, sweetheart. That’s not for you. He had believed her. He had always believed her.
“No,” he said. It came out very small.
“Every witch has something inside her called an essence,” Dan went on. He was speaking very carefully now, slowly, like someone walking across ice. “It’s where her magic is. It’s also what she needs in order to rest when she dies. A witch can go on living if it’s taken out of her. But when she dies, she can’t rest. She stays awake, somewhere, alone, forever. Delta told me that’s the worst thing that can happen to a witch. Worse than the tables. Worse than anything.”
Zen said nothing. His hands had found the edge of the mattress and were holding on to it.
“The people in that laboratory took the essence out of every witch who died there. They wanted to use them. Witches live for hundreds of years, and the people who paid for that place wanted to live that long too. But they couldn’t keep the essences. In glass they faded in a few days. An essence would only stay whole inside something alive. Something built to hold it.”
Dan looked up at him then, for the first time since he had started. His eyes were wet. His voice went very soft, the way you speak to someone who is about to fall.
“So your parents built one.”
The room was completely silent.
Zen did not move. He did not say anything. Somewhere inside him, something very small and very old had gone still, the way an animal goes still when it hears a sound in the dark.
“Zen. Think about it.” Dan’s voice broke on his name. “They measure you every single time you go home. They take your blood. You’ve never once been ill. Not a cold. Not a fever. Not once, in all the years I’ve known you. And you don’t remember being small. Do you? You told me once. You said it was as if you started at about ten.”
“Stop.”
“You were made, Zen. To hold them. Every witch who died in that place is inside you. Hundreds of them. You’ve been carrying them your whole life, and nobody ever told you.”
“Stop. Please. Dan, please, stop.”
Dan stopped. He was crying now, openly, without wiping his face. He came up off the floor onto his knees, in front of the bed, and put both his hands on Zen’s arms, and held on.
“I’m sorry,” he said. “I’m so sorry. I’d give anything for it not to be true. But listen to me. Listen. You’re still my friend. You’re still you. You’re the one who used to tip his chair back until it fell over. You’re the one who sat with me at my grandmother’s funeral and didn’t say a single word for three hours because you knew I couldn’t stand it. Whatever they made you for, that’s who you are. That part hasn’t changed. It’s never going to change.”
Zen sat on the edge of the bed with Dan’s hands on his arms. He was looking at the floor, but he was not seeing it. He was not seeing anything at all.
He was thinking about the book his mother wrote numbers in.
A thick black notebook with a red spine. She brought it out every time he came home, while his father took his blood at the kitchen table. She wrote down figures in small, neat columns, and never let him see the pages. When he was little he had asked once what she was writing, and she had smiled and said, how much you’ve grown. He had been so pleased. He had stood up very straight for her, every time after that, so that the number would be bigger.
He had seen that book every holiday of his life. He had never once asked about it again. It had never seemed strange to him. It had just been home.
He thought of his mother’s hand smoothing his hair back from his forehead when he was ill. Except he was never ill. He tried to find the memory and it was not there. He tried to find any memory of being very small, a birthday, a first day at school, a fall from a bicycle, and there was nothing. Only a soft grey blank, like a wall that had been painted over.
He had never noticed it before. He had never once looked.
He thought about the warmth he felt near Delta, the one he had never been able to explain. He thought about how cold the bench had been that morning without her. And for the first time he wondered whether the warmth had ever really been his at all.
His hands had started to shake. He pressed them harder against his knees, until it hurt.
“Go on,” he said. His voice did not sound like his own.
Dan looked at him for a long moment, as if he wanted to stop, and knew that he could not.
“Delta escaped,” he said. “A long time ago, before you were born. Years later she came back and found you, because she could feel her dead friends inside you. All of them. People she had heard crying through the walls for hundreds of years. She wanted to set them free. But she couldn’t get them out of you without destroying them.”
Dan stopped. He wiped his face with the back of his hand, and made himself go on.
“Then she found out how your parents were doing it. When you went home, they strapped you to a table. They take you right to the edge of dying, with a machine. When you’re at the very edge, one of the essences comes loose by itself. Just one. The machine catches it and shuts it in a case, and brings you back to live, and they sell it before it can fade. Then they do it again the next day.” His voice cracked. “But if nothing catches it—if you go over the edge and the machine doesn’t bring you back—then it doesn’t go into a case. It goes free. That witch gets to rest.”
“When I die,” Zen said. His voice sounded very far away to him, as if someone else were saying it in another room.
“Yes.” Dan could hardly get the word out. “When you die on that operation table.”
He made himself look at Zen while he said the rest. Zen could see what it cost him.
“So she made a spell. A loop. Every time you died there, and one of them came free, time turned back to an ordinary day in the cafeteria. The four of us at the table by the window. Asa telling a story. Jess stirring her lemonade. You spinning your pen. And everything started again. You, alive and well. The rest of them back inside you. Except the one that had gone free. Each time, there was one fewer. Each time, one more witch got to rest.”
Zen’s lips moved before the words came.
“How many times?”
Dan did not answer straight away. He looked at the floor.
“More than three hundred.”
Zen heard himself breathe in, a sharp, broken sound, like someone who has been hit.
Three hundred. He tried to imagine it and could not. Three hundred times he had sat at that table and laughed. Three hundred times he had walked out of the gate to the dark car. Three hundred times.
“It always went the same way,” Dan said, very quietly. “We went to the sea. Asa drowned. I blamed you for it. Every time, Zen. Every single time, I told you it was your fault. And you believed me, and you felt so guilty that you went home. And when you got home, your parents strapped you to a table and used that machine on you, to take the essences out of you and sell them. Delta had changed the machine, so that the first time, it let you die instead of bringing you back. You died on that table. Three hundred times. Alone, in the dark, in a room under the ground.”
Zen’s mouth was dry. He could not swallow.
Something was coming up in him. Not a thought. A picture. It had been there his whole life, at the very bottom of his sleep, and he had never once let himself look at it properly.
“White lights,” he said. “I can’t turn my head.”
Dan looked at him.
“I dream it,” Zen said. The words came out of him in pieces. “I’ve always dreamed it. Ever since I can remember. There’s a strap across my forehead. It’s cold. Everything is so cold. And there’s someone sitting next to the bed. A girl. I can never turn my head to see her. She never says anything. But she’s there. She’s always there. I used to wake up and think, at least someone was there.”
His voice broke completely on the last word.
Dan’s face crumpled.
“That was her,” he said. “That was Delta. She sat with you every night. Every time. Three hundred times, she sat in that chair while you died, so that you wouldn’t be alone. She told me she didn’t know why she did it. She said that when she looked at you on that table, she saw herself.”
Zen put his face in his hands.
He did not make a sound. His shoulders shook, and he did not make a sound. Dan stayed on his knees in front of the bed and kept one hand on his arm, and did not let go, and did not say anything, for a long time.
When Dan spoke again, his voice was hoarse.
“Now there’s only one essence left in you,” he said. “It’s hers. They took it out of her before she escaped. All the others are free now. Hers is the last one. That’s why she joined us this last time, instead of only watching. For once, she wanted something for herself. She wanted to know what it was like to have friends.”
He swallowed.
“And then I got in the way. I couldn’t stand losing any of you. So every time you died, anywhere, I dragged everything back to March and tried again. Siem Reap. The sea. The bus. The roof. The mountains. Every one of those deaths was mine, Zen. That was me.”
“I remember the sea,” Zen said slowly. “I remember losing her in the sea.”
Dan nodded slowly. “I asked her about that. Why you remember the sea, when you don’t remember anything else.”
“What did she say?”
“She said a spell like hers listens. It listens to the one who pays for it. But it also listens to anyone caught inside it who wants something with their whole heart. And that kind of wanting doesn’t go away when time turns back. It stays behind. It builds up, turn after turn, like water behind a wall.”
Dan looked down at his hands.
“I was inside her loop for three hundred lives before I ever remembered one of them. And in every single one, I lost you. Asa in the water. You going home and never coming back. Every time, I wanted you all back so badly that I couldn’t stand it. I didn’t know it, but the wanting stayed, every time. It got bigger and bigger.”
“Why didn’t it do anything before?”
“Because it didn’t need to. In all her other turns, one of the witches inside you was set free, and paid for the next turn. The spell never needed me. But in her last loop, after Siem Reap, you died in the wrong place. Nobody was set free. Nobody was left to pay. Time should have just gone on.” He swallowed. “And by then, three hundred lives of wanting had built up in me. It was enough to turn time back on its own. That’s how I took it over. That’s why I’ve remembered every life since.”
“And me?”
“That was different,” Dan said. “She said it wasn’t wanting, with you. It was how many times.”
He made himself look at Zen.
“You’ve lived the sea more than you’ve lived anything else in the world. Three hundred times, in her loops, before I ever started. The beach. Someone lost in the water. Jess on the sand in your arms. Someone blaming you for it. Over and over, the same way every time. Then once more, in mine.” He rubbed his face with both hands. “She said a thing that happens that many times wears into you, like a path that’s been walked on too often. Time can turn back. But it can’t fill in a path that deep. Everything else happened to you once. The sea happened three hundred times. And the last time, the girl in the water was her.”
Zen was very still. “I know,” he said. “I remember all of it. Every day of it. The boats. The man from the coast guard with his cap in his hands. You, in the corridor.” His voice did not change. “And then I woke up in March, and she was sitting at our table, alive, eating rice. And nobody else remembered anything. I thought I was going mad. I didn’t tell anyone, because I thought I was going mad.”
“You weren’t,” Dan said.
Neither of them said anything for a long time.
“When I told her you remembered losing her,” Dan went on at last, “she didn’t say anything. She asked me twice if I was sure it was her you remembered, and not Asa. Then she said she didn’t understand why it hurt so much to hear it.”
“I know.”
“Why did she leave?”
This was the part Dan had been dreading.
“Because of how it ends. If you die on that table, her essence goes back to her. She’s whole again. The spell is finished, and time doesn’t go back anymore. That was always the plan.”
“And if I don’t?”
“Then it stays in you. She lives without it. And one day when she dies, she won’t be able to rest. Like the others couldn’t.”
Dan made himself say the rest.
“She chose that. She told me she can bear it. She said she couldn’t bear the other thing. She left so that you would never have to get on that table. She asked me to tell you to run, and never go home.”

### The Choice
@pov Zen

Zen walked for a long time that night.
He did not decide where to go. His feet took him. Across the dark courtyard, past the bench where she used to sit. Past the cafeteria, locked now, with the chairs turned upside down on the tables, and the table by the window just one more table among all the others. Round to the back stairs behind the library, where he sat down on the cold step and did not move for an hour.
He had two choices. Dan had laid them out for him plainly, the way Dan laid out everything, and then he had held on to Zen’s arm and cried.
He could run. Dan would help him. He could leave the country, change his name, and never see his parents again. He would live. He would get older. He might even be happy, one day, somewhere far away. And Delta’s essence would stay inside him. She would be incomplete for the rest of her life. And when she died, she would not be able to rest. She would be awake, alone, somewhere, forever. The thing she had watched happen to her friends for four hundred years. The thing she was most afraid of.
And his parents would look for him. They would never stop. They had built him. They had found a way to take the essences out and sell them, and there was one left, and they would want it. If they ever caught him, the machine would take hers too, and shut it in a steel case with a buyer’s name on it, and some old rich stranger would carry Delta inside him until it was used up.
Or he could go home, and get on the table, and die there. Then her essence would go back to her, freely. She would be whole. And one day, a long time from now, she would be allowed to rest.
He sat on the step and shook.
He was afraid. He was surprised by how afraid he was. He thought of the white lights. The strap across his forehead, so tight that he could not turn his head. The cold of the steel coming up through his back. He had dreamed it his whole life, and now he knew it was not a dream. It was a memory. He had lain on that table three hundred times and died there, and some part of his body remembered every one of them.
He thought about guilt.
He had felt guilty all his life, it seemed. For Asa in the water. For Jess on the mountain. For two girls on the ground under a roof. Dan had told him about all of those, gently, one by one. He had paid for each of them with his life, over and over, and each time it had fixed nothing at all, because none of those debts had really been his to pay.
This was different.
This was not guilt. Nobody was making him do this. Nobody was standing in a corridor telling him it was his fault. He was simply carrying something that belonged to someone else. She had been cut open for four hundred years, in the dark, and at the end they had taken the one thing she needed in order to rest, and they had put it inside him, and he had never even known.
And she had sat beside him three hundred times. In the worst room in the world. In the place where she had been tortured for four hundred years. She had walked back into it, again and again, and sat down in a chair beside a boy who could not even turn his head to see her, so that he would not have to die alone.
At least someone was there.
He put his face in his hands on the cold step and cried, properly, for the first time since the sea.
She was willing to go without her essence forever, for him. She had already made up her mind. She had walked away so that he would never have to choose.
He found, sitting there in the dark, that he was not willing to let her.
It was not the warmth. He knew now that the warmth had been hers all along, her own essence inside him, reaching for her. That did not matter. He would have chosen her without it. He had watched her drown, and walked the beach for six days, and knelt at the edge of the water and promised he would never forget her. And he hadn’t.
In the morning, he called his mother.
She answered on the second ring. Her voice was warm, the way it always was. “Sweetheart. Is everything all right?”
“I want to come home for a while,” Zen said.
There was a little pause. Then she said, very gently, “Of course you do. I’ll send the car this afternoon.”
She sounded pleased. He held the phone to his ear for a long time after she had hung up.
Before he left, he went to see Jess.
He knocked on her door, and for a long time nothing happened. Then she opened it, a little way, with the chain still on. Her eyes were red. She looked as if she had not slept in a week. She did not say anything. She only looked at him through the gap.
“I’m sorry,” Zen said. “For all of it. For not knowing. For letting you say it every day and not saying it back. I said it in Dan’s room, but I wanted you to hear it again. Just from me.”
Jess looked at him for a long time. Her lips pressed together, hard. He thought she was going to shut the door in his face, and he would have understood.
“I know,” she said.
It was what he had always said to her, every time she told him she loved him. I know. She heard herself say it, and almost smiled. It was a small, broken smile, and it was gone at once.
Then she closed the door.
He stood in the corridor for a moment. Through the door he heard her start to cry, very quietly, the way you cry when you do not want anyone to hear. He put his hand flat against the wood. Then he took it away, and went.
Then he went to look for Asa.
She was not in her room. He found her on the back stairs behind the library, on the same cold step where he had sat all night. She was alone, the way she so often was now, with her phone face down on her knee.
She looked up when she heard him, and for a second her face started to do the old thing. The bright smile began to come. Then it stopped, as if she had remembered that she did not have to do that any more.
“Oh,” she said. “It’s you.”
He sat down beside her. Not too close. For a while neither of them said anything. A door banged somewhere inside the library, and a group of first-years went past the bottom of the stairs, laughing.
“I’m going home for a while,” Zen said.
“Okay.” She turned the phone over on her knee, then turned it back. “Why are you telling me?”
“Because I didn’t want to just go.”
Asa looked at him properly then. He did not know what she saw in his face. Whatever it was, it made her stop pretending to be busy with her phone.
“I hated you, you know,” she said quietly. “For two years. Not all the time. Just every time she looked at you. Every time you made her laugh without even trying, when I had been trying all day.” She gave a small, tired laugh. “That’s a horrible thing to say to someone. Sorry.”
“It’s all right,” Zen said. “I didn’t know. I didn’t know about any of it until Dan’s room.”
“I know you didn’t. That was the worst part. You never did anything. You were just there, and she loved you, and it wasn’t your fault.” She pulled her sleeves down over her hands. “I wanted it to be your fault. It would have been easier.”
Zen did not know what to say to that. All his life, it seemed, people had told him things were his fault. Now here was someone telling him that something was not, when it would have been so much easier for her to say that it was.
“Asa,” he said. “Will you look after her?”
She went very still.
“She’s on her own now,” he said. “She sits by herself at lunch. She can’t look at me. Delta’s gone. I don’t want her to be alone. She said it herself, in Dan’s room. You were the only person who ever sat down next to her.”
“She doesn’t want me,” Asa said. Her voice shook. “She told me so. In front of everyone.”
“She doesn’t want you like that,” Zen said gently. “That’s not the same as not wanting you.”
Asa did not answer. She looked down at the phone on her knee.
“I left her soup,” she said at last. “The night after. Outside her door. She didn’t open it. But in the morning the bowl was empty, and she’d washed it, and put it back exactly where I left it.” Her mouth trembled. “That’s all we are now. A bowl outside a door.”
“That’s not nothing,” Zen said.
She pressed her lips together and nodded, hard, the way people do when they are trying not to cry in front of someone.
“I’ve always looked after her,” she said. “I’ll keep doing it. Even if she never—” She stopped. “I’ll keep doing it.”
“Thank you.”
He stood up. Asa stood up too, and for a moment they were just two people on a staircase who had never been very good at talking to each other. Then she put her arms around him, quickly and awkwardly, and let go again before either of them could think about it.
“Come back soon, okay?” she said.
Zen smiled at her. He did not say yes. He did not say no. He went down the stairs and did not look back, because if he looked back, he was not sure he would be able to keep walking.
Dan was waiting at the gate. He had not been told. He had known anyway. The dark car was already there, long and black and very clean, with its engine running. The driver did not get out.
Dan’s eyes were red too. He had his hands shoved deep into his pockets, the way he had kept them on the mountain, as if he did not trust them.
“You could still run,” Dan said. His voice cracked straight down the middle. “Zen. Please. You could still run. I’d come with you. Anywhere. I mean it.”
Zen looked at him. His oldest friend. The boy who had counted them every day for more lives than anyone should ever have to live.
“I know you would,” he said.
“If I stop you—”
“Then you’re holding on again,” Zen said gently. “And it all starts again. You know it does. Don’t. Please. Let it be finished.”
Dan stood with his fists closed. He was crying. He had died for his friends more times than he could count, and now he was being asked to do the one thing he had never managed. To stand still.
He stood still.
Zen put his arms around him. Then he got into the car.

### The Steel Table
@pov Zen

The car drove for a long time. The driver did not speak.
Zen sat in the back with his hands in his lap and watched the campus go past the window. The library. The courtyard. The bench, empty in the afternoon sun. Then the gate, and Dan standing beside it, getting smaller and smaller, still not moving. Zen turned round in his seat and watched him until the road bent and there was nothing left to watch.
After that it was the city, then the motorway, then the hills. He had never noticed before how quiet that car was. He could hear his own breathing. He started to count the breaths, the way Dan counted things. Then he stopped, because he did not want to know how many were left.
His mother was waiting at the door. She hugged him tightly, and held his face in both hands, and looked at him.
“You look so tired, sweetheart,” she said. “We’ll do a quick check-up tonight. Then you can rest.”
“Okay,” Zen said.
She smiled at him, and he smiled back. She did not know that he knew. He let her think he didn’t.
He had thought that when he saw her again he would be angry. He had thought he would hate her. He stood in the doorway with her hands on his face, and he did not hate her at all. She had called him sweetheart for as long as he could remember. She was his mother. He knew exactly what she was, and he still loved her, and that was the part he had not been ready for.
They did not wait long. That night his father took him downstairs, and then further down, through a door that had always been locked, to the stairs that went under the ground. Zen had never been through that door, as far as he could remember. But his body knew the way. His legs knew how many steps there were before he had counted them. On the last step his knees nearly gave way, and he had to put his hand flat against the wall until they stopped shaking.
The room was the one from the dream. White lights. Steel.
He stood in the doorway and could not go in.
For a moment he was going to run. He could feel it in his legs. Back up the stairs, through the locked door, out into the dark. Dan would answer on the first ring. Dan would come. Anywhere. I mean it. He could still live. He could get older. He could be happy, one day, somewhere far away.
And she would be awake forever. Alone. Somewhere. With no end to it.
He walked in.
He lay down on the table himself. Nobody had to make him. His father fastened the strap across his forehead, so that he could not turn his head, and then the straps at his wrists. The steel was cold through his shirt. His heart was beating so hard that he could feel it in his teeth. His hands had started to shake, and he held on to the edge of the table so that nobody would see.
His father read numbers from a screen. His mother stood beside him in a white coat.
Behind them, the machine began to hum. Zen knew that sound. He had heard it three hundred times. Every part of him wanted to pull at the straps and scream, and he made himself lie still. He thought of Dan at the gate with his fists closed, doing the one thing he had never managed. If Dan could stand still, so could he.
“Mum,” Zen said.
She looked down at him.
He did not know what he had meant to say. Did you ever love me? Was any of it real? Do you know what you are doing? None of it came out. In the end there was only the one word, the oldest word he had.
She put her hand on his hair and smoothed it back, once.
He closed his eyes. For one second, with her hand on his hair, he let himself pretend. He was a little boy who was ill, and his mother was sitting on the edge of his bed, and everything was going to be all right. He could not remember if that had ever really happened. He wanted so badly for it to have happened. He wanted her to leave her hand there, just a little longer.
Then she took her hand away and turned to the screen.
Tears ran out of the corners of his eyes. He could not turn his head, so they ran sideways, down into his ears and his hair. Nobody wiped them away. Nobody noticed.
There was a chair beside the table. He could see the edge of it. It was empty.
In the dream, there had always been someone in that chair. He had never been able to turn his head to see her. He had only known she was there, the way you know someone is in the room with you in the dark. At least someone was there.
Now nobody was.
And he wanted her. That was the truth, and he let himself have it, just once, because there was nobody left to hide it from. He wanted her in that chair so badly. He wanted to hear her breathing beside him. He wanted her to say something blunt and strange, the way she always did, and for her hand to be there, so that he would not have to do this alone.
But she had sat in that chair three hundred times. In the worst room in the world. He was not going to make it three hundred and one.
That was right. That was the reason he had come. She was somewhere in the sun, a long way from this room. Wiping a table, maybe. Or sitting somewhere with her eyes closed and her face turned up to the light, the way she used to on the bench. She did not know where he was. He was glad. He had made sure she would never have to sit in that chair again.
The machine was built to bring him to the edge of death, catch what came loose, and pull him back again. His parents had planned to pull him back. They did not know that it would not. Delta had changed it long ago, in a turn none of them could remember, and her change had come back with every turn since.
Zen had decided not to come back.
The humming grew louder. The lights seemed to get whiter, and further away. His father said a number, and then another number. Zen felt himself sliding toward the edge, slowly, the way you slide into sleep when you are very tired.
He thought of them. Jess behind her door, crying so quietly, so that nobody would hear. Asa on the back stairs, saying come back soon, okay? Dan at the gate, getting smaller and smaller. He was sorry. He was so sorry to leave them like this. But Asa had promised. Dan would stay. They had each other. They would be all right, in time. He had to believe that, so he did.
He thought of the table by the window. Asa telling a story with her hands. Jess stirring her lemonade, round and round. Dan watching all of them. His own pen, spinning between his fingers. The day that time had always turned back to.
He did not need it back. He let that go too.
When the edge came, he did not fight it. He let go.
He felt the warmth begin to move. All his life it had sat low under his ribs, so quietly that he had thought it was just a part of him. Now it rose. It did not go into the machine. It went past the machine, past the white lights, out of the room and up through the ground, as if it knew exactly where it was going.
It was going back to her.
Delta, he thought.
He did not say it out loud. He did not want her name in this room. He only thought it, once, with everything he had left, and hoped that when the warmth reached her, she would know it had come from him.
Far away, he heard his father say, “It isn’t catching.”
“Then bring him back,” his mother said.
“I’m trying. It won’t—”
Then he heard his mother say his name. Her voice was different. He had never heard her sound like that before. She sounded frightened. He would never know if she was frightened for him, or for the thing that was leaving him.
It did not matter now.
He was not afraid. It was strange. The chair was empty, and the lights were white, and all the same it was warm.

### The Word
@pov Delta

**Part Three — Delta**

She went back to her shop.
It was a long way. She sat by the window of the bus the whole time with her bag on her knees. She did not sleep, and she did not let herself look back.
The shop was exactly where she had left it, on the corner, with the motorbikes going past and the plastic stools stacked outside. The boy was behind the counter, wiping a glass. He looked up when she came in, and his mouth fell open.
“You came back,” he said.
“I was on holiday,” Delta said. “A long one.” She put her bag down behind the counter as if she had only gone out for an hour. “You’ve been putting too much ice in. I can tell from here.”
He laughed, because she had always said things like that. He did not see that her hands were shaking.
The boy had kept it well. The sign still said 75% in crooked red numbers. She stood in front of it and touched the edge of it with one finger. Three parts sweet, to carry one part bitter. The old man had told her that, a long time ago, with a glass in his hand. She had not understood it then. She thought she understood it a little better now.
She sat in her chair by the door, in the morning sun, and let the customers think she had been on a long holiday.
On the outside, she was exactly who she had always been. She made the coffee perfectly. She told people what they ought to order, and she was usually right. She laughed in the right places. Nobody who came into the shop would have guessed that anything had happened to her at all.
On the inside, she was broken apart.
She woke at midnight every night, the way she had in the loops, and lay very still, and waited for the ceiling to be March. It never was. The clock went on to one, and then two, and nothing turned back. In the mornings she made two glasses of coffee without meaning to, and had to pour one away. Every time the door opened, she looked up before she could stop herself. It was never him. She knew it would never be him. She looked up anyway.
She had made her decision before she left the campus.
