She would live without her essence. She would be incomplete until she died, and after that she would not rest. She knew exactly what that meant. She had felt three hundred others endure it through a wall.
For four hundred years she had lain in the dark and felt them, the witches whose essences had been taken. Awake. Unable to finish. They had not screamed. That was the worst part. They had simply gone on, and on, with no end coming, and she had felt every day of it. Soon that would be her. Alone, somewhere, with no end.
She was afraid of it. She was more afraid of it than she had ever been of anything.
She could bear that.
What she could not bear was Zen on that table one more time. She had watched it three hundred times, and it had been necessary. She could not make it necessary again. Not for herself.
She had sat in that chair beside him three hundred times. The strap across his forehead. The white lights. His eyes open, and unable to turn toward her. The first time, he had asked her to stay, and she had stayed, every time after that, until the end. She knew the exact moment his breathing changed. She knew the sound the machine made when it did not catch him. She had heard it three hundred times, and she thought she would hear it for the rest of her life.
She could not send him back there. Not even to save herself. Especially not to save herself.
She had turned the feeling over for months, the one that was not grief and not shame. The one that had wanted to say yes to him in the courtyard.
She remembered that day exactly. Zen in the sun, thinner, with the crease between his eyebrows. I like you. Not the way I liked Jess. And the thing that had risen in her, under the grief and the guilt, warm and frightening, that had wanted to say yes. She had been so afraid of it that she had said the cruellest thing she could find instead. I wish the one who died that day was you. And he had run to the roof.
Afterwards she had told herself that the dark part of her had come out because she was angry. It was not true. It had come out because she was frightened. She had felt something she had no name for, and she had tried to kill it before it could grow. She had killed him instead.
She had not understood it then. She had not understood it on the bench either, when Dan told her that Zen remembered losing her. She had only known that it hurt, much more than it should have, and that she did not know why.
She had watched humans for years, and she finally recognized it from the outside, the way she had recognized all the others.
There was a girl who came into the shop every morning with a boy from the garage across the road. She always got there first. She always ordered for both of them, because she knew what he liked before he said it. When he talked, she watched his face. When he left, she kept looking at the door for a while after it had closed.
Delta watched her for a week. Then, one morning, she understood that she was looking at herself.
She knew how Zen spun his pen when he was thinking. She knew the crease between his eyebrows, and what brought it. She knew that he asked what she thought of things, and then actually listened to the answer, which almost nobody had ever done. She had sat in a cold room three hundred times so that he would not die alone. She had walked away from him so that he would never have to choose. She had done every single thing that girl did, and more, and she had never once put a name to it.
She loved him.
She said it out loud, once, in the empty shop after closing, to hear what it sounded like. Her voice shook. Four hundred years. She had known the words for hatred, and grief, and shame. She had known every word of a curse against every human alive. And she had never once had a use for this one.
So that was the word. It was a small word for it.
She sat down on the floor behind the counter, where nobody passing in the street could see her, and cried until it was dark.
She was almost glad to know. It made the choice simple. He would run. He would live a long time, somewhere, and be kind to dogs, and he would never know what he was carrying for her.
Some mornings, sitting in the sun, she could still feel the faint pull of her own essence inside him, very far away. She did not let herself feel for where it was. If she knew where he was, she would go to him. So she only let herself know that he was alive, somewhere, and breathing.
When the shop was quiet, she pictured it. Zen at thirty, at fifty, at eighty, in a small house a long way from anyone who knew his name. Grey hair. A dog asleep on his feet. Laughing at something on the radio. She would never see any of it. That was all right. It would be happening, and that was the point.
That was enough.
She said it to herself every morning when she opened the shop, and every night when she closed it.
That was enough.

### What Came Back
@pov Delta

It happened on an ordinary morning. She was wiping a table.
The sun was coming in through the open door. The boy was singing something under his breath behind the counter. A man at the back was reading a newspaper. She had wiped that table a thousand times. She was not thinking about anything.
Then the pull inside her went still.
She knew that stillness. She had felt it once before, on a bench, after midnight, in the dark. Her hand stopped moving on the table.
And the hollow under her ribs, which had been empty for longer than Zen had been alive, was suddenly not empty.
Something came into it. It was warm, and it fitted exactly. It was like hearing her own voice, not from another room this time, but from her own mouth.
Her essence had come back to her.
She stood with the cloth in her hand.
For one second she felt only how good it was. She was whole. For the first time in years she could feel all of her own magic, every part of it, the way you feel your own hands. She had forgotten what it was like. She had forgotten she had ever been this.
And it was warm. That was the strange thing. It was hers, but it had been inside him for his whole life, and it had come back to her still warm from him. For one moment, so faint that she could not be sure, she thought she heard her name. Not out loud. Only once, the way someone says a name when it is the last thing they have.
Then she understood what it meant.
There was only one way for it to leave him. She had made that rule herself. The table, and his last breath.
He had not run.
“No,” she said, out loud. The man with the newspaper looked up. “No. No.”
He had gone home. He had lain down on that table, under those lights, with the strap across his forehead. And there had been nobody in the chair.
The cloth fell out of her hand.
He had known. That was what she could not get past. Dan had told him everything, because she had asked Dan to. Tell him the truth. All of it. Then tell him to run. She had been so sure that the truth would make him run. She had never once thought that it might make him do this.
He had done it for her. He had gone into the worst room in the world, on his own, so that she could be whole. He had made sure she would never have to sit in that chair again. And she had not been there. For three hundred deaths she had been there, and for the one he chose, she had been wiping a table.
She waited, without meaning to, for time to turn back. It did not. She had known it would not. Hers had been the last essence. There was no one left to pay for another turn, and Dan had let go.
Her hands moved anyway. Out of habit, toward the spell, the way they had moved three hundred times before. She stopped them.
She looked across the shop at the sign on the wall. 75%. Three parts sweet, to carry one part bitter. She remembered what she had said to Dan on the bench. That isn’t an ending. That’s why it never ends. If she reached for the spell now, she would be doing exactly what Dan had done. Holding on. Asking for a glass with nothing bitter in it. And it would all start again, and they would all die of it again, and Zen’s choice would mean nothing.
She put her hands flat on the table and kept them there.
The morning simply went on. A motorbike passed outside. Someone asked for a coffee.
She made it. Little ice, seventy-five percent sweetness. Her hands did not shake. She put it on the counter and said, “Enjoy,” and the customer smiled at her and went out into the sun, and did not know anything.
Zen was dead, and he was going to stay dead.

### Nothing Left to Hold
@pov Delta

She closed the shop early that day. She turned the chairs upside down on the tables, one by one, and swept the floor, and washed every glass and put each one back where it belonged.
Then she gave the boy the keys.
“Are you going away again?” he asked.
“Yes.”
“On holiday?”
“Something like that.” She put the keys into his hand and closed his fingers over them. “Keep them this time. The shop is yours.”
He stared at her. “I don’t understand.”
“You don’t have to.” She looked at the sign on the wall, at the crooked red numbers. “Just don’t ever change that. However many people ask you to. Promise me.”
He promised. He did not know what he was promising. That was all right. The old man had never properly explained it to her either. She had had to find it out for herself.
She walked out of the town, past the last houses, to a field, and sat down in the sun.
The grass was warm. There was nobody else there. She took off her shoes and put her feet in the grass, and turned her face up to the light, the way she had on the bench every day, back when there was still a table for the four of them.
She went through it carefully, the way she had once gone through her two counts. One thing at a time. Slowly. So that she would not miss anything.
The old man was gone. She had killed him, and learned everything from him.
He had found her when she was nothing but hatred. He had saved her life, and given her a glass of coffee, and explained the whole world to her with it. And she had killed him with one word, at midnight, in a hospital, before she had understood a single thing he told her. She had carried his lesson for the rest of her life. Sitting in the field, she thought she finally understood the end of it. The bitter part was not a mistake. You did not get to take it out. You could only choose what you carried it with.
Her people were gone. All three hundred and some. They were at rest, and she had done that. It was the one thing she was sure she had done right.
The four friends were alive. But the table by the window was empty, and it would stay empty. She had no place there.
And Zen was gone.
She had held on for four hundred years. First to hatred, then to the sun, then to a glass of coffee, then to her dead friends, then to four people at a table, then to him.
There was nothing left to hold.
She was not angry. She was not even very sad, at the end. She was tired, and she was whole, and a witch who is whole is allowed to rest.
She turned her face up to the sun and closed her eyes.
Then she let go.
She felt her essence rise out of her. She expected it to do what the others had done. To touch the world once, lightly, and be gone.
It did not go.
It stayed in the air above the field. Then, slowly, it began to drift.
It had no direction. It was not going where the others had gone. It moved over the grass like something that had not finished, and did not yet know where it was meant to be.

### Epilogue

The essence drifted for a long time.
It crossed the field and the town. It crossed the hills, and the place where a laboratory lay under the ground. It went on, with no direction, until there was no more land beneath it.
There was a beach.
A group of friends had come down to the sea for the day. There were six or seven of them. Someone had brought a speaker. Someone was trying to light a grill in the wind, and the others were laughing at him. Two girls stood in the shallows, holding hands.
One of the group sat a little apart from the rest, on the sand, with his arms resting on his knees. He was a handsome man. He was watching his friends the way a person watches something he has seen many times before.
The essence came down out of the bright air and landed on his hand.
None of the others noticed. One of his friends looked straight at his hand, saw nothing there, and turned back to the grill.
He could see it.
For a moment he did not move. Then he turned his hand over, slowly, so that the essence lay in his palm. He curled his fingers around it a little, carefully, the way you hold something that might break. He lifted it closer to his face and studied it.
It was warm.
“A witch’s essence?” he said, under his breath.
His eyebrows went up. He looked surprised. He glanced out over the sea, in the direction it had come from, and then back at the thing in his hand.
Then, slowly, he smirked.
“Interesting.”
